[{"q":"Design a production RAG system for 10 million documents serving 1,000 QPS. Walk through the architecture and the numbers.","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would design this as two independently scalable systems: an ingestion pipeline that turns changing documents into searchable evidence, and an online pipeline that retrieves authorized evidence and generates a grounded answer. Before choosing databases or GPUs, I would establish the workload assumptions, calculate storage and token demand, and agree on quality, freshness, availability, and latency objectives. Ten million documents and one thousand queries per second describe only part of the problem. Document size, permission complexity, answer length, traffic distribution, and update frequency can change the design substantially.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start with the service contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Assume an enterprise knowledge assistant with ten million documents, eight searchable chunks per document on average, a mixture of exact identifier lookups and natural language questions, and a peak arrival rate of one thousand requests per second. I would ask whether that rate is sustained or bursty, how many tenants share it, and whether a small number of tenants dominate. I would also ask whether answers require citations, whether documents change hourly, and what happens when sufficient evidence is unavailable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">As an illustrative target, suppose the service should begin streaming within two seconds at the ninety-fifth percentile, deliver a typical answer within ten seconds, and make ordinary source updates searchable within five minutes. These are proposed requirements, not capabilities implied by the architecture. Authorization revocations may require a much shorter bound. I would establish separate correctness targets for answerable questions, appropriate abstention, and complete citation support rather than accepting a fast service that confidently returns incorrect answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate the searchable corpus</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Ten million documents multiplied by eight chunks gives eighty million chunks. With a 1,024-dimensional embedding stored as float32, each vector occupies 4,096 bytes. The raw vector payload is therefore 327,680,000,000 bytes: approximately 327.68 decimal gigabytes or 305.18 gibibytes. This calculation excludes document text, metadata, identifiers, index structures, replicas, and temporary allocations. Mixing decimal and binary units would make the capacity estimate harder to audit.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an HNSW-style index, graph memory depends on implementation, connectivity, identifier representation, levels, and allocator overhead. A simplified example with thirty-two neighbors and eight bytes per stored link adds about 20.48 gigabytes across eighty million vectors, before other overheads. That is a planning illustration, not a portable sizing formula. I would measure actual bytes per indexed vector using representative data and the selected engine, including its deletion and compaction behavior. <a href=\"https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Faiss-indexes\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Text storage can be larger than the vectors. At four hundred tokens per chunk, the corpus contains roughly thirty-two billion chunk tokens, including any overlap. Assuming four UTF-8 bytes per token only as a rough English-text estimate gives about 128 gigabytes of raw chunk text. Tables, multilingual content, compression, duplicate storage, and source binaries change that estimate. Original documents belong in durable object storage; searchable text and metadata need a separate budget.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose compression and sharding deliberately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Storing vector components with eight bits instead of thirty-two reduces the component payload to approximately 81.92 gigabytes. It does not reduce the entire service footprint by four times. Quantization metadata, graph links, original vectors retained for rescoring, text, and replicas still consume space. I would evaluate retrieval recall against a float32 baseline, especially for rare identifiers, multilingual queries, and near-duplicate passages, before enabling compression broadly.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reducing dimensions can also help, but arbitrary truncation is unsafe. A model trained to support meaningful lower-dimensional prefixes may allow a supported truncation strategy; an ordinary embedding model does not automatically have that property. I would benchmark the actual shortened representation. Product quantization or a disk-oriented index might be attractive when memory cost dominates, but their latency and operational trade-offs must be measured under the required filters and update workload. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.13147\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An initial layout could use eight primary shards with replicas distributed across failure zones. This is a starting hypothesis rather than a final machine count. Sharding spreads data; replication provides additional serving copies and availability. If each request searches all eight primary partitions, one thousand external queries per second create eight thousand shard searches per second before retries. Replicas can distribute that work, but scatter-gather latency still depends on slow shards and coordination overhead.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a reliable ingestion pipeline</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Connectors should emit document create, update, delete, and permission-change events into a durable queue. Each event carries a tenant identifier, source document identifier, source revision, event identifier, and relevant provenance. Workers fetch the authoritative source, parse it, preserve headings and tables, create chunks, embed them, and publish searchable records. A document is not considered fully indexed merely because one embedding call succeeded; the publication state must identify which complete revision is available.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Chunk identifiers should encode stable source identity and revision or content identity, depending on the update strategy. Idempotent processing prevents retries from duplicating records. Out-of-order events require version checks so an older update cannot overwrite a newer revision. A transactional outbox or equivalent durable change mechanism reduces gaps between recording a source update and publishing its event. Periodic reconciliation compares source revisions with searchable revisions because event delivery alone is rarely a sufficient completeness guarantee.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Parsing is a quality-critical component. A table row without its column headings may produce an embedding that looks relevant while lacking interpretable evidence. I would preserve source offsets, section paths, page references, parent-child relationships, and document versions. Failed documents go to a dead-letter queue with categorized reasons, not into silent omission. Embedding batching improves throughput, but batch size depends on token lengths, provider limits, memory, and latency requirements rather than a universal recommended number.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Plan backfill and update throughput</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The initial corpus has eighty million chunks. If the sustained embedding throughput is five thousand chunks per second, embedding alone takes sixteen thousand seconds, or about 4.44 hours. That estimate assumes the provider accepts the token volume and that parsing, network transfer, storage, and indexing keep up. At four hundred tokens per chunk, the same rate means two million input tokens per second. A chunk-based throughput figure without token accounting can conceal an impossible rate-limit assumption.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For ongoing operation, suppose one percent of documents change each day. That is one hundred thousand documents or roughly eight hundred thousand chunks per day under full-document re-embedding, averaging about 9.26 chunks per second. Actual bursts may be far larger. Content hashes can avoid re-embedding unchanged sections. Permission updates often should update authorization metadata without recomputing semantic vectors, while deletes need tombstones that prevent stale content from returning during asynchronous cleanup.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design the online retrieval path</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The request first passes authentication, tenant resolution, quota checks, and authorization-context construction. The server derives the active tenant and effective permissions; a client-supplied tenant value is not sufficient authorization. A cache lookup is permitted only when the entry matches the appropriate tenant, authorization scope, corpus state, and configuration. Queries requiring current data may bypass an otherwise valid content cache.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a cache miss, I would run lexical retrieval and dense approximate retrieval in parallel when evaluation demonstrates complementary value. Lexical search helps with product codes, names, quoted text, and exact terminology. Dense retrieval helps with paraphrases and conceptual similarity. Each branch must enforce the same mandatory security and lifecycle filters. Reciprocal rank fusion is a practical starting point because raw lexical and vector scores are not naturally calibrated to the same scale.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The fused candidate pool might contain one hundred passages. A cross-encoder can rerank a smaller pool, followed by duplicate removal, source diversity checks, parent expansion, and token-budget selection. Final context may contain five to ten passages, but the correct number depends on evidence coverage and token length. A multi-part question might need more sources than a simple lookup. If a query rewrite must affect current retrieval, it is a dependent step; running it asynchronously does not magically improve the already-started search.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Quantify reranking demand</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">At one thousand uncached requests per second and one hundred candidates per request, the reranking service receives one hundred thousand query-passage pairs per second. If a measured worker processes two thousand representative pairs per second, the arithmetic minimum is fifty workers. Provisioning at seventy percent utilization would require about seventy-two workers before additional failure capacity. These numbers illustrate the calculation; actual throughput depends strongly on model size, sequence length, hardware, batching, and service overhead.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Candidate reduction can materially change this budget. Reranking thirty candidates creates thirty thousand pairs per second. Selective reranking may help if an evaluated routing rule identifies easy cases safely. A distilled model, shorter inputs, and dynamic batching are additional options. I would compare their end-to-end answer quality at the same cost target. A cheaper reranker that removes the decisive exception paragraph can increase total product cost through wrong answers and escalations.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Quantify generation demand</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose each uncached request sends three thousand input tokens and produces three hundred output tokens. At one thousand requests per second, generation requires three million input tokens per second and three hundred thousand output tokens per second. This is usually a substantial capacity commitment. A managed model endpoint still has quotas, regional constraints, and overload behavior; using a provider does not remove capacity planning.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Little&#x27;s law connects arrival rate and concurrency. If an average request occupies the generation service for eight seconds, one thousand arrivals per second imply roughly eight thousand concurrent active requests in steady state. The serving fleet needs enough memory for weights, key-value caches, batching, and operational headroom. Prefix caching can reduce repeated prompt processing, but answer tokens still need generation. Input-heavy and output-heavy workloads therefore need separate benchmarks.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cost can be expressed without inventing current prices: daily generation cost equals daily uncached requests multiplied by the sum of input-token count times input-token unit price and output-token count times output-token unit price. Add embedding, retrieval, reranking, storage, networking, and operations. At a sustained one thousand requests per second, the service handles 86.4 million daily requests. A small per-request inefficiency becomes financially significant at that volume.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Set deadlines and failure behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would allocate an illustrative first-token budget across authentication, query embedding, retrieval, reranking, context assembly, and model prefill. For example, retrieval might receive a few hundred milliseconds and reranking another limited slice. These are budgets to validate, not component performance promises. Parallel retrieval duration follows the slower required branch plus coordination; dependent stages add sequential delay. Tail latency matters more than a collection of attractive median numbers.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Every remote call needs a deadline and bounded retry policy. Retry storms can multiply load during an outage, so retries should respect an overall request deadline and use backoff with jitter. If the reranker fails, fusion-only retrieval may be an acceptable evaluated degradation. If the authorization service fails, the system should refuse access rather than widen the corpus. Partial shard results must be identified internally and handled according to the application&#x27;s completeness requirements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Streaming improves perceived responsiveness, but unsupported content cannot be safely recalled after being shown. For sensitive workloads, validate evidence and citations before releasing claims or buffer a suitable unit of output. Cancellation should propagate when a user disconnects. Admission control, per-tenant concurrency limits, and separate queues for interactive and batch work prevent one expensive tenant or unusually long request from exhausting the whole system.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make caching safe and measurable</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exact caches work well for repeated questions when the authorization and freshness conditions match. Semantic answer caches are more complicated because superficially similar questions can differ by date, region, product edition, or negation. I would introduce them only after measuring false-hit risk. The cache key or validity check must include the active tenant, effective permission scope, relevant model and prompt versions, and source revision dependencies.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A hypothetical thirty percent valid cache hit rate reduces downstream average demand from one thousand to seven hundred requests per second. It does not prove that peak capacity can be reduced by the same amount. Cold starts, invalidation events, and unusual traffic can produce a much lower hit rate. I would load-test cold-cache conditions and avoid designing availability around an assumed percentage copied from another application.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain verification and the interview decision</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Before launch, I would replay representative queries over a realistically sized corpus, measure answer-bearing evidence recall, citation support, appropriate abstention, freshness lag, cost, and tail latency, and test node loss and dependency outages. I would report results by tenant size, query type, language, permission selectivity, and document format. A system can meet aggregate throughput while failing the largest tenant or the narrowest authorization filter.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that eighty million chunks are manageable with deliberate indexing, compression, partitioning, and update control. The harder serving calculations are candidate scoring and token generation at one thousand requests per second. I would start with a measurable hybrid retrieval funnel, enforce authorization before evidence reaches the model, and scale each stage according to observed demand. The final architecture is justified by workload measurements and quality requirements, not by the document count alone.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Faiss-indexes\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Faiss: index representations and memory accounting</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.13147\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Matryoshka Representation Learning</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Describe a multi-stage retrieval architecture and justify each stage economically.","g":["multi-stage retrieval"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A multi-stage retrieval architecture is a sequence of decisions that progressively spends more computation on fewer, more promising pieces of evidence. Its purpose is to preserve enough relevant material for a correct answer while avoiding expensive scoring over the entire corpus. I would justify every stage through its marginal contribution to answer quality, latency, cost, and security. There is no requirement that each stage be exactly ten times more expensive or return exactly ten times fewer items; those ratios are workload-dependent heuristics.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Begin with a concrete question</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Imagine an employee asks, &quot;Can a customer on the Standard plan export audit logs after cancelling?&quot; The corpus contains plan comparisons, export instructions, cancellation policies, older contract terms, and support discussions. A semantically similar passage about exporting analytics is not enough. The answer may require the correct product edition, cancellation status, retention interval, and an exception defined in another paragraph. The retrieval system must preserve those constraints as evidence moves through the funnel.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose there are eighty million chunks, but the employee can access only one tenant&#x27;s current product documentation. A useful architecture first establishes that authorized scope, then generates candidates, scores them more carefully, selects complementary evidence, and gives a bounded context to the generator. I would log the identifiers entering and leaving each stage. Without that lineage, a wrong answer cannot be attributed reliably to missing candidates, bad reranking, context selection, or generation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Stage zero establishes the permitted search space</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Authentication and authorization determine the active tenant, the caller&#x27;s effective principals, and the resource policies that apply. Lifecycle constraints remove deleted documents and superseded revisions when the task requires current policy. Query-derived constraints may narrow product, region, or language, but uncertain interpretations should not silently remove potentially relevant evidence. Mandatory security filters and optional relevance filters serve different purposes and should be represented distinctly.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Filtering is economically valuable because it reduces the search space, but it is not free. Permission resolution, large group lists, bitmap operations, source checks, and selective filtered ANN searches consume resources. A filter that leaves very few authorized vectors may change the optimal search algorithm. Exact similarity over a small filtered set can be efficient and more predictable than traversing a global approximate graph. The appropriate choice depends on the engine&#x27;s implementation and measured selectivity behavior.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Filtering must happen consistently across lexical retrieval, vector retrieval, parent expansion, fallback search, caches, and citation resolution. A perfect dense-search filter is insufficient if a lexical fallback returns another tenant&#x27;s document. Filtering after the model has received the text does not repair confidentiality. For fast permission revocation, a final authoritative check on selected resources may supplement indexed permissions before any content reaches a reranker, generator, or unrestricted trace sink.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Stage one generates candidates for recall</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Candidate generation should recover a broad but bounded set of potentially useful passages. A lexical engine uses terms and index statistics to find exact vocabulary matches efficiently. A dense retriever embeds the query and searches nearby document vectors. These methods have different failure patterns. A product identifier such as AX-1047 may favor lexical search, whereas &quot;getting records out after ending a subscription&quot; may align well with dense semantic retrieval.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I might begin with one hundred candidates from each branch, then deduplicate their union. Those values are initial experimental settings. If the branches overlap heavily, their union may contain far fewer than two hundred unique passages. If one branch contributes almost no uniquely useful evidence on the target workload, keeping both may not justify the operational complexity. Hybrid retrieval is a design option to evaluate, not a universal obligation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The primary question at this stage is whether sufficient evidence is present anywhere in the candidate pool. For a single-fact query, success may require one passage. For the cancellation example, success may require both an entitlement passage and a retention exception. Ordinary passage recall can look acceptable even when the complete evidence set is absent. I would therefore measure answer-bearing recall and multi-evidence coverage separately from generic topical relevance.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Distinguish approximate-search quality from relevance quality</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Approximate nearest-neighbor recall asks whether ANN recovers the neighbors that exact search would return under the same embedding similarity. Relevance recall asks whether the retrieved passages actually support the user&#x27;s answer. These are different properties. Increasing an HNSW search parameter may recover the exact nearest vectors while still returning policy documents for the wrong product because the embedding representation does not distinguish the constraint adequately.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would build a small exact-search reference for sampled queries and compare it with ANN under identical filters and corpus versions. If exact neighbors contain the required passage but ANN misses it, tune or change the index. If both miss it, inspect embeddings, lexical matching, parsing, chunking, and query interpretation. This diagnostic prevents buying more search computation for a failure that originates before the index is queried.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Fuse the candidate lists carefully</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Lexical scores and cosine similarities have different scales and distributions. Adding them directly with arbitrary equal weights rarely has a principled interpretation. Reciprocal rank fusion uses rank positions, often summing terms of the form one divided by a constant plus the candidate&#x27;s rank in each list. It rewards candidates that rank well in either branch and avoids pretending that a lexical score of twelve is comparable to a cosine score of 0.78.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Rank fusion is not universally optimal. It discards score magnitude and can overvalue a weak branch. A calibrated weighted combination or a learned ranker may outperform it with sufficient representative labels. I would start with a simple, inspectable baseline, then test whether more complex fusion improves evidence coverage. Security and lifecycle filters remain mandatory regardless of which fusion strategy produces the final ordering.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Stage two spends computation on precision</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cross-encoder receives the query and one candidate passage together, allowing token interactions that a separately computed document embedding cannot express directly. It can distinguish &quot;export remains available for thirty days&quot; from &quot;export is disabled immediately after cancellation&quot; when both look semantically close. The reranker estimates relevance for the actual question rather than merely proximity between independently generated vectors. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.04085\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If one hundred candidates survive fusion, the model scores one hundred query-passage pairs. That is manageable compared with scoring eighty million pairs, but it can still dominate serving cost. Input truncation is a practical hazard: if a four-hundred-token limit drops the exception at the end of a passage, the reranker may confidently demote the best evidence. I would preserve headings and important neighboring context while measuring the quality and throughput implications of sequence length.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reranking cannot recover an absent candidate. If the decisive passage was omitted by candidate generation, reordered candidates remain insufficient. It also does not prove that a passage is true, current, or authoritative. A convincingly written malicious page may receive a high relevance score. Relevance, authorization, source trust, and factual reliability require separate controls, even if some of their signals contribute to a final learned ranking function.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate the economics explicitly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Let the incoming uncached query rate be Q and the number of reranked candidates be C. Pair-scoring demand is Q multiplied by C. At five hundred queries per second and eighty candidates, the service needs forty thousand pair evaluations per second. If a benchmarked worker sustains sixteen hundred representative pairs per second, twenty-five fully utilized workers are the arithmetic minimum. Real provisioning needs headroom for burstiness, failures, and latency-sensitive batching.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reducing the candidate pool from eighty to forty halves pair-scoring demand, but it may remove useful evidence. I would plot quality against cost across several candidate counts rather than choosing the smallest count that appears fast. Throughput measurements must use realistic passage lengths and concurrency. A benchmark using short synthetic sentences can substantially overstate the capacity available for enterprise documents with long tables and section prefixes.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Batching introduces another trade-off. Larger batches improve accelerator utilization but may wait longer to accumulate requests. A deadline-aware batcher should cap waiting time and avoid allowing a long passage to delay many short requests unnecessarily. Autoscaling should observe queueing delay and tokens or pairs processed, not only request counts. Two queries with different candidate lengths can impose very different computational work.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Stage three selects an evidence set</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The highest-ranked passages are not automatically the best final context. Ten near-duplicate copies of the same cancellation overview provide less coverage than an overview, a retention exception, and the applicable plan entitlement. I would remove duplicates, group overlapping chunks, and favor evidence that covers unresolved parts of the question. Maximal marginal relevance is one possible diversity mechanism, but its relevance-diversity trade-off needs task-specific tuning.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Selection operates under a token budget. If the context allocation is three thousand tokens, five six-hundred-token passages consume the entire allocation before citation metadata or instructions. A short, decisive exception may deserve space over a longer generally relevant explanation. Parent expansion can restore a heading or a table legend, but expanded parents must be reauthorized and version-consistent. Expansion should not pull an entire unrelated document into the prompt by default.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Compression can extract relevant sentences or summarize candidate passages, but it introduces its own failure modes. Dropping &quot;except enterprise agreements&quot; changes policy meaning. Generated compression may invent a connection between clauses. For exact contractual or numerical evidence, source-preserving extraction is often easier to validate. I would retain source spans and compare compressed output with originals during evaluation rather than assuming shorter context always improves grounding.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Stage four generates a supported answer</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The generator receives the user question, selected evidence, citation identifiers, and explicit instructions about uncertainty and source use. It should answer only what the evidence supports and identify unresolved conditions. For the example, a good answer might distinguish the general cancellation rule from a Standard-plan exception. If the necessary edition-specific passage is missing, the model should avoid filling the gap from a plausible neighboring plan.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Generation cost depends on both input and output length. Saving one thousand input tokens per request across five hundred requests per second removes five hundred thousand input tokens per second from demand. That can be economically meaningful, but the objective is cost per successfully answered task. Overaggressive compression that increases clarifications or escalations can cost more overall. I would measure the complete user workflow rather than optimizing prompt length in isolation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Add adaptive paths when the evidence supports them</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A simple identifier lookup may succeed with exact lexical retrieval and a small context, while a comparison across several policies may need query decomposition and a larger candidate set. An adaptive router can choose different budgets. However, the router becomes another failure source. A false &quot;easy&quot; classification may skip the reranker precisely when similar policy versions are hard to distinguish. I would evaluate routing errors and maintain a safe escalation path.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retries should respond to diagnosed gaps. If candidate coverage is poor, increasing the generation model&#x27;s size does not add missing evidence. A second retrieval attempt might broaden optional filters, use alternate terminology, or search a specific authorized source. Mandatory access filters must never be relaxed. The additional attempt also needs a bounded latency and cost budget so ambiguous queries cannot trigger unbounded retrieval loops.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate marginal value and stopping points</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would run controlled comparisons: lexical only, dense only, fused retrieval, fused retrieval plus reranking, and the full selection pipeline. Hold the source snapshot, query set, generator, and token budget constant where possible. Measure candidate coverage, ranking quality, final evidence sufficiency, answer correctness, latency, and monetary cost. An average improvement should be inspected for regressions on exact identifiers, no-answer queries, restrictive permissions, and document revisions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Useful stage-level diagnostics include the fraction of answer-bearing passages lost during each transition and the frequency of redundant final context. If reranking improves nDCG but final answers worsen, inspect whether it removed complementary evidence or promoted current-looking but obsolete documents. If context selection reduces tokens without changing quality, that is a genuine efficiency gain. A funnel diagram alone does not establish the economic value of its stages.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would explain that the architecture separates a broad evidence search from increasingly selective, expensive reasoning. Authorization defines what may be considered; candidate generation protects recall; fusion combines complementary signals; reranking improves question-specific precision; selection controls coverage and token use; generation produces a supported response. Each stage has a measurable contract and an explicit loss risk.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The economic argument is marginal rather than ceremonial. I keep a stage when its improvement in reliable task completion justifies its latency, infrastructure, maintenance, and failure modes. I remove or bypass it when a simpler path meets the same requirement. The best funnel is therefore the smallest evaluated sequence of stages that preserves necessary evidence, enforces security, and meets the service&#x27;s quality and cost objectives under realistic production load.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.04085\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Passage Re-ranking with BERT</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/query-languages/query-dsl/query-dsl-knn-query\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Elasticsearch: filtered kNN queries</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you migrate to a new embedding model with zero downtime?","g":["index migration"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would migrate embeddings using a versioned parallel index, a durable change stream, independent validation, and a reversible serving switch. The existing index continues serving while the replacement is built and tested. Zero downtime means users retain an available, authorized retrieval path throughout the migration; it does not mean the migration is free, instantaneous, or guaranteed to improve quality. I would treat consistency, deletion handling, permission freshness, and rollback as first-class requirements rather than focusing only on re-embedding the corpus.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define exactly what is changing</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An embedding model defines a representation space. Its dimensions, normalization, similarity function, token limits, preprocessing, and query-versus-document instructions may differ from the incumbent. Even two models producing vectors of equal length generally do not produce interchangeable coordinates. A query vector from model B must not search document vectors from model A unless an explicitly validated compatibility mechanism exists. Matching dimensions alone does not establish compatibility.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would create an embedding contract containing the model identifier and revision, tokenizer or input preparation version, vector dimension, normalization rule, similarity metric, chunking version, and any quantization settings. The query service and index manifest both refer to that contract. This makes a bad configuration detectable before requests silently produce irrelevant results. It also prevents a seemingly harmless model alias update from changing the representation without a coordinated index migration.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Decide whether chunking is part of the migration</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If the goal is to compare embedding models, keeping chunk boundaries and parsed text constant gives a cleaner experiment. Otherwise an improvement may come from better chunking rather than the embedding model. Sometimes new model limits justify different chunks, but I would make that a deliberate combined migration and evaluate it accordingly. Existing citation identifiers, source spans, parent relationships, and deletion mappings need a strategy if chunk identities change.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For example, index A may use four-hundred-token chunks and a 768-dimensional model, while B uses the same chunks with a 1,024-dimensional model. That isolates representation changes reasonably well. If B instead combines entire sections, the candidate count and token budget also change. A fair comparison must report those differences. The objective is improved task performance under defined resource constraints, not merely a higher score from a different pipeline.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Establish a durable source of truth</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The vector index is a derived serving structure. Authoritative document identity, source revision, lifecycle state, and permissions should be recoverable outside it. I would maintain a durable manifest or canonical document store and a replayable stream of source changes. Every record needs an ordering mechanism appropriate to its source, such as a monotonic revision or connector sequence, rather than relying exclusively on wall-clock timestamps.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A timestamp watermark can help with operational progress, but it is not sufficient by itself when clocks differ, timestamps have coarse precision, or events arrive late. Two updates can share the same timestamp. A deleted document may not appear in an ordinary &quot;updated since&quot; query. The migration must account for creates, updates, deletes, moves, and ACL changes, including events that occur while a historical snapshot is being scanned.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build index B without disturbing index A</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would provision B with its own schema, capacity limits, observability, and access policy. Validate the schema on a representative sample before launching a full backfill. Confirm that the database accepts the intended vector dimension and metric, that filters have the required semantics, and that each record carries the expected tenant and source metadata. Empty or malformed vectors should be rejected explicitly rather than indexed as apparently valid content.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Resource isolation matters. A large backfill can saturate the same embedding endpoint, object store, network, or database cluster used by live traffic. I would reserve interactive capacity and throttle background work using queue depth, provider rate limits, and serving latency. If the embedding service offers discounted asynchronous processing, I would verify current terms and completion guarantees rather than assuming a fixed discount or a universal turnaround time.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Capture changes before the snapshot can become stale</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">One safe pattern records a source-log position, takes or identifies a consistent snapshot, backfills that snapshot, and replays all subsequent changes. Another pattern continuously consumes the change stream into B while the backfill runs, using conditional revision checks to prevent stale snapshot records from overwriting newer updates. The precise implementation depends on the source system, but the correctness requirement is the same: no transition may lose or resurrect a document revision.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Blind synchronous dual writes are fragile because A can succeed while B fails. A durable outbox or event log makes each index an independently retryable consumer. Processing can be at least once if upserts are idempotent and version-aware. I would track failures separately for A and B and expose each consumer&#x27;s lag. A new index that is ninety-nine percent complete can still be unusable if the missing one percent contains a critical tenant&#x27;s current policies.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prevent stale writes and deletion resurrection</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose document D is revision five in the snapshot. While the backfill runs, revision six arrives and is indexed into B. If the backfill later writes revision five without a conditional check, B regresses. Each write should apply only when its source revision is newer than the stored revision, or when an equivalent ordering rule establishes that it is safe. Duplicate processing of revision six should leave the same effective result.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deletes require equally durable ordering. If D is deleted at revision seven, retain a tombstone or deletion ledger long enough to reject a delayed revision-five backfill. Otherwise a deleted document can reappear. Permission revocations must also override older indexed ACLs. I would explicitly test update-delete-retry sequences, not merely compare final vector counts. Counts can match even when the wrong documents are present.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Estimate the backfill budget</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For eighty million chunks averaging four hundred input tokens, the embedding workload is approximately thirty-two billion tokens. At a sustained one million input tokens per second, the idealized embedding duration is thirty-two thousand seconds, or about 8.89 hours. Parsing, fetching, retries, rate limits, indexing, and validation add time. I would budget both the total token cost and the extra infrastructure required to keep A and B live simultaneously.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Checkpoint progress by stable partitions and source revisions so interrupted work resumes without restarting the entire corpus. Track documents scanned, eligible chunks, successful embeddings, index acknowledgments, skipped unchanged content, and failed items. A checkpoint after an embedding request but before a durable index acknowledgment cannot be treated as complete. Reconciliation should verify the intended published revision, not just that some record with the document identifier exists.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Validate data before evaluating answer quality</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would compare manifests between the authoritative source and B: expected documents, chunk counts, source revisions, tenant identifiers, deletion state, and permission versions. Sample content hashes to detect accidental preprocessing differences. Check vector norms and dimensions for anomalies. Verify that metadata filters behave correctly for empty groups, multiple organizations, public resources, and narrowly scoped access. A quality benchmark cannot compensate for incomplete or unauthorized data.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Coverage needs to be inspected by partition. A global completion ratio can hide that an entire connector, language, or large customer failed parsing. I would require defined completeness and freshness criteria for the canary population. Some deployments can migrate tenants independently, while others need a consistent whole-corpus switch. The unit of cutover should follow the product&#x27;s consistency requirements rather than whichever database alias is easiest to change.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare retrieval under controlled conditions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run a versioned query set against A and B using the same authorized source snapshot where possible. Measure answer-bearing Recall@k, ranking metrics, multi-passage coverage, no-answer behavior, latency, and memory. Include rare terminology, exact identifiers, multilingual requests, long documents, narrow filters, and near-duplicate revisions. If B improves conceptual questions but regresses on product codes, the aggregate score alone should not decide the migration.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarity thresholds must be recalibrated because score distributions change between representation spaces. A cosine threshold of 0.8 in A has no automatic meaning in B. Even rank-based decisions need evaluation because the candidate distribution can change. Hybrid fusion weights and reranker input distributions may also need adjustment. I would tune on development data and retain a held-out set for the final comparison.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate the complete answer pipeline</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Better retrieval metrics do not guarantee better answers. B might retrieve longer passages that crowd out a crucial exception during prompt assembly. It may favor a different source version or create more duplicate evidence. I would run end-to-end generation with the intended context budget and measure correctness, support for claims, citation accuracy, appropriate uncertainty, and cost. Where generation is stochastic, use repeated or paired evaluations to distinguish variation from a meaningful change.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Human review should focus on disagreements and high-consequence slices, with a separate random sample to avoid inspecting only obvious differences. Keep model-based judges calibrated to human rubrics and avoid assuming that a judge&#x27;s preference proves correctness. Migration acceptance criteria should be written before looking at the final results. Otherwise teams can unconsciously redefine success around whatever metric the new model happens to improve.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Shadow live traffic without exposing B&#x27;s answers</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">In shadow mode, a bounded sample of authorized live queries goes to B while A&#x27;s result remains user-visible. B must receive a compatible query embedding and the same effective access policy. Record retrieval differences, latency, errors, and source versions. Avoid doubling expensive generation for every request unless the evaluation budget supports it. Shadowing retrieval alone can answer many operational questions at substantially lower cost.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Production traffic reveals distributions missing from curated tests, including bursts, repeated prefixes, extremely selective ACLs, and unusual document types. However, shadowing also consumes capacity and can expose private text to new processing paths. Apply the same approved regional, logging, and retention controls as the live system. Shadow traffic should not silently create an ungoverned copy of sensitive queries or retrieved documents.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Canary the complete serving configuration</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The switch must select the embedding contract, index, retrieval configuration, thresholds, and compatible cache namespace together. An alias alone may be insufficient because the query embedding must change at the same time. I would use a versioned serving configuration and pin each request to one configuration for its lifetime. That prevents an in-flight request from embedding under A and searching B after a mid-request deployment.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Canary steps might move a small tenant cohort or a small percentage of traffic, then expand after enough representative observations. Fixed percentages such as one, ten, fifty, and one hundred are examples, not required stages. I would watch quality guardrails, authorization failures, latency percentiles, source freshness, and fallback rates. A low-traffic cohort may need more elapsed time to provide useful evidence than a busy percentage-based canary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle caches according to their dependencies</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Query embeddings must be cached with the embedding contract in the key. Retrieval caches need the index or corpus revision and authorization scope. Answer caches need a validity rule covering the evidence and serving configuration that produced the answer. Namespace changes are often safer than attempting to discover and delete every incompatible entry. Old entries can expire naturally if they are no longer eligible for serving.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Not every answer generated under A must be deleted immediately in every system. An answer grounded in unchanged, still-authorized sources may remain valid if the application&#x27;s cache contract permits it. But it must not be reused accidentally through an unversioned key. The important requirement is explicit compatibility and freshness, not blanket invalidation as a substitute for understanding dependencies. During comparison, cache behavior should be controlled so it does not hide migration regressions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve a credible rollback path</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Keep A current during the rollback window, including document deletes and permission changes. A warm but stale index is not a safe rollback target. Reserve enough capacity to return traffic quickly and keep A&#x27;s query embedding configuration available. Define rollback triggers and verify that changing the serving configuration restores a complete compatible path, including caches and routing. Exercise the rollback with test traffic before depending on it during an incident.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Rollback becomes harder if a simultaneous schema or chunking change removes information needed by A. I would avoid unrelated irreversible changes during the migration, or build explicit adapters and backward-compatible records. After the observation window, stop A&#x27;s consumer at a recorded position, retain required recovery artifacts, and remove A according to the retention plan. Decommissioning should be a planned operation, not an abandoned duplicate fleet.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Present the migration as a consistency problem</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview answer would emphasize that re-embedding is the bulk operation, but consistency is the difficult engineering problem. I would build B from a reproducible source, continuously apply ordered changes, prevent stale writes and deletion resurrection, evaluate both retrieval and answers, and switch a complete versioned configuration through shadow and canary stages. A remains a current rollback path until B demonstrates acceptable behavior.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">That design makes zero downtime credible because availability does not depend on finishing the backfill at a particular moment. It also makes the result reviewable: every indexed record, quality measurement, cache entry, and request can be associated with a specific representation and corpus state. I would call the migration complete only when B meets quality and operational requirements and A has been safely retired, rather than when the last embedding batch returns.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An index alias can support a storage-level switch in engines that provide it; the application still has to switch the compatible query embedding and other serving settings. See <a href=\"https://www.elastic.co/docs/manage-data/data-store/aliases\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Elasticsearch: index aliases</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://www.elastic.co/docs/manage-data/data-store/aliases\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Elasticsearch: index aliases</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.13147\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Matryoshka Representation Learning</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you enforce document-level access control in a multi-tenant RAG system?","g":["multi-tenancy"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Document-level access control in RAG means that every piece of evidence used to answer a request must be readable by that caller under the current policy. I would enforce this in trusted application and storage layers before the text reaches a model, then preserve the same boundary through reranking, expansion, caching, citations, and logging. A system prompt saying &quot;do not reveal private documents&quot; is not authorization. Once unauthorized content enters the prompt, confidentiality has already been put at risk.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate identity, organization membership, and resource permission</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A user can belong to multiple organizations. Authentication establishes who the user is; it does not establish which organization&#x27;s data a particular request may search. The request needs an active tenant context that the server validates against the user&#x27;s memberships. Within that tenant, the user may have access to only some projects, folders, or documents. Tenant membership is therefore necessary in many systems but insufficient for document access.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Consider a consultant who belongs to organizations Alpha and Beta. While working in Alpha, the consultant should not receive Beta documents merely because both memberships exist. Even within Alpha, membership in the engineering group may permit technical runbooks but not finance forecasts. I would represent the request as a principal, an active tenant, effective groups or roles, relevant attributes, and an authorization-policy version. These values come from trusted identity and policy services rather than client assertions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">State the security invariant clearly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For every resource selected into the evidence set, the server must establish that the caller can read that resource in the active tenant. A useful expression is: the resource tenant matches the validated active tenant, the resource is in an allowed lifecycle state, and the policy decision permits this principal to read it. Additional rules may depend on project membership, region, classification, or source-system permissions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The invariant applies to text, titles, snippets, counts, suggested queries, and citation metadata, not only the final answer body. A document title can reveal an acquisition or disciplinary investigation. An error that says &quot;you cannot access the confidential merger plan&quot; may disclose its existence. The product should return authorization-safe responses without exposing protected identifiers or names through diagnostic text that reaches the user.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose a suitable isolation model</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Physical isolation can place sensitive tenants in separate clusters, databases, or service accounts. This gives strong operational boundaries and can simplify residency and customer-managed key requirements. It also increases deployment, backup, monitoring, and upgrade work. A cluster per small tenant may be unnecessarily expensive, while regulated or unusually large tenants may justify dedicated infrastructure. I would choose isolation based on threat model, scale, and contractual requirements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Logical isolation uses a shared cluster with tenant-specific collections, namespaces, partitions, or mandatory filters. Those mechanisms have different security properties across products. A namespace name is not automatically a security boundary if an application service credential can query every namespace. I would inspect the database&#x27;s actual authorization model and ensure the serving service cannot accidentally bypass the intended restrictions through another endpoint or administrative query path.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A mixed model is often practical: shared infrastructure for ordinary tenants, dedicated partitions or clusters for large and sensitive tenants, and the same document authorization contract everywhere. This avoids tying the application semantics to one storage layout. Moving a tenant between deployment tiers should not change who can access its documents. Infrastructure isolation reduces blast radius, but within-tenant permissions still require explicit enforcement.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Model permissions at the right level</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each indexed chunk should retain tenant identity, source resource identity, source revision, ACL or policy reference, and lifecycle status. If a document&#x27;s permissions are uniform, chunks can reference the document policy instead of duplicating a huge principal list. If sections genuinely have different access rules, chunk-level policies are necessary. The parser must not merge content across different security boundaries and then label the combined chunk with a less restrictive rule.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An allow-list of user and group identifiers is one possible representation, but enterprise policies can include inherited permissions, explicit denies, external sharing, nested groups, expiration, and attribute-based conditions. Flattening everything into a simple list may lose semantics. I would normalize source policies carefully and document unsupported cases. Where faithful replication is difficult, retrieval can use a conservative coarse filter followed by an authoritative source or policy-service check before content is exposed.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Centralize retrieval enforcement</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">All search operations should go through a trusted retrieval service that requires a validated authorization context. The service constructs the tenant and permission constraints itself. Its API should make an unconstrained production query difficult or impossible, for example by requiring a scoped search object rather than accepting an arbitrary filter dictionary from callers. Database-side row or document policies can provide an additional independent layer where supported.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Both lexical and vector branches must use the same effective restrictions. Fusion should combine only eligible candidates. Administrative indexing credentials should not be reused by interactive serving if narrower credentials are available. Feature-specific retrieval paths, such as related-document suggestions, conversational memory search, or a fallback to global search, need the same enforcement. Most authorization incidents arise from an overlooked alternate path rather than from a failure in the primary happy path.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Understand filtered vector search behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A vector engine may apply constraints during candidate search, after generating candidates, or through an adaptive strategy. The externally observable guarantee must still prevent unauthorized content from leaving the trusted search layer. Post-filtering a small global top-k can return too few authorized results even when relevant authorized documents exist. That is a recall problem in addition to a security design concern. <a href=\"https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/query-languages/query-dsl/query-dsl-knn-query\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would benchmark retrieval under different permission selectivities, including users who can access only a tiny fraction of the tenant corpus. Depending on the engine, a small eligible set may favor exact vector scoring. Larger sets may benefit from integrated filtered ANN. I would not assume filtering is free or that one candidate setting works for every ACL pattern. Security restrictions remain fixed while retrieval algorithms and candidate budgets adapt.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Check authorization before additional processors receive text</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The generator is not the only possible disclosure destination. A cross-encoder hosted by another service, an LLM compressor, a query-debugging dashboard, and a trace collector can all receive retrieved content. A design that filters only immediately before the final model may already have sent unauthorized text to one of these components. I would define where candidate identifiers become readable text and ensure authorization occurs before that boundary.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For very dynamic policies, the index can return candidate identifiers and coarse metadata, after which a policy service verifies the selected resources before their text is fetched. The generator then receives only approved content. This can increase latency and complicate reranking, so the architecture may combine fresh indexed ACLs with final checks. The choice depends on the required revocation bound and the sensitivity of the processing environment.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make permission freshness explicit</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Permission changes must be treated as a separate synchronization stream, not merely as document-text updates. Removing a user from a group should not wait for the document to be edited. I would consume identity and source-policy events, invalidate permission caches, update relevant authorization indexes, and maintain a revocation mechanism that can take effect before slower bulk indexing completes. Deleted or unshared resources should fail closed during uncertainty.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">&quot;Immediately&quot; needs a defined meaning. A contract might require revocations to affect new requests within seconds, while accepting a longer indexing delay for ordinary content edits. Distributed systems cannot guarantee instantaneous propagation across every component without specifying the consistency model. I would measure the end-to-end revocation delay and decide how to handle requests already in flight. A final policy check can establish authorization at a particular decision point, but cannot retroactively erase content already streamed.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prevent time-of-check and time-of-use mistakes</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose the user is authorized when retrieval starts, but access is revoked before the answer is generated. The product must define whether authorization is pinned at request admission or rechecked before disclosure. Sensitive applications may revalidate selected resource permissions just before model processing or output release. This adds cost but can reduce the window in which stale permissions are used. The guarantee should be stated precisely rather than described vaguely as real-time security.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Source revision and policy revision should travel together where possible. Fetching text revision nine while checking an ACL snapshot for revision seven may produce an inconsistent decision. Source APIs do not always provide atomic content-and-policy reads, so the connector may need conservative handling or a current access check. I would record the relevant decision versions without copying unnecessary protected content into the audit record.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Scope caches by effective authorization</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cache keyed only by question and tenant is unsafe when users within that tenant have different permissions. An authorized manager could ask about a forecast, populate the cache, and cause a regular employee to receive the same answer. The cache must bind its entry to an equivalent effective access scope or revalidate every underlying evidence resource for the new caller. A user identifier alone is also insufficient if that user&#x27;s permissions later change.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An authorization fingerprint may include the active tenant, effective groups, policy revision, and other relevant attributes. This improves reuse among genuinely equivalent permission contexts, but the fingerprint must cover the actual policy semantics. Document-dependent cache entries should retain resource and revision dependencies. Revoking one source can then invalidate or disqualify affected answers. Semantic cache matching adds another risk because a similar question may request a protected detail absent from the original question.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve boundaries during expansion and citation use</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Parent expansion is a common hidden breach. The retrieved child chunk may be authorized, but its parent document can contain restricted sections. Neighboring chunks can also have different policies. Every added span needs its own eligibility check. Generated summaries that combine several sources inherit constraints from all contributing information; labeling them with the least restrictive source&#x27;s permissions would expose data through a derived artifact.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Citations should resolve through an authorization-aware endpoint or a source URL that enforces current access. A long-lived public object-storage URL can bypass later revocation even if retrieval was correct. Signed links need appropriate scope and lifetime, and citation previews must not reveal protected snippets. Exported answers and shared conversations introduce further distribution decisions: the recipient&#x27;s ability to open the conversation does not automatically imply permission to every cited source.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect logs and operational access</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Application logs should avoid raw retrieved text by default. Useful audit fields include request identifier, tenant, pseudonymous principal reference, selected resource identifiers, policy decision version, serving configuration, and outcome. These logs still contain sensitive metadata and need access restrictions and retention rules. Debug tooling should require explicit authorized access to content, with auditability, rather than granting every developer a searchable copy of all prompts.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Support impersonation and break-glass access require separate, tightly controlled workflows. An internal administrator role should not silently bypass customer policies through ordinary retrieval. Encryption at rest and in transit protects data against some threats but does not fix an incorrect authorization decision. Similarly, a private model deployment does not prevent one employee from receiving another employee&#x27;s documents if the application constructs the wrong search scope.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Test the security invariant with adversarial cases</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would create a permission matrix covering multiple tenants, users with multiple memberships, overlapping groups, explicit denies, removed memberships, external users, expired shares, deleted documents, and mixed-policy parent sections. For each case, assert that unauthorized resource identifiers never reach the reranker, generator, cache response, or citation preview. Include a deliberately missing filter in a controlled test to ensure the enforcement layer detects or prevents the error.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Property-based tests can generate combinations of resources and principals and verify that every returned item satisfies the policy decision. Integration tests should exercise actual database filtering semantics and fallback paths. Revocation tests should populate caches first, then remove access, and verify the documented propagation bound. Load tests must include large group memberships and narrow permissions because a secure design that times out can tempt unsafe operational workarounds.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the production decision</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My senior interview answer would distinguish tenant isolation from document authorization and emphasize that one user may legitimately belong to several organizations. Each request still operates in a validated tenant and permission context. I would centralize enforcement, preserve source policy semantics, treat permission changes as a first-class event stream, and prevent caches, expansion, logs, or citations from bypassing the same boundary.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The difficult trade-off is freshness versus serving cost. Indexed ACLs provide efficient filtering, while current policy checks provide stronger revocation behavior at additional latency and dependency cost. I would choose their combination based on a stated security contract, verify it with realistic permission patterns, and fail closed when the required authorization cannot be established. The model should reason over evidence that is already permitted; it should never decide which private documents the user is allowed to see.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/query-languages/query-dsl/query-dsl-knn-query\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Elasticsearch: filtered kNN queries</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OWASP: prompt injection risks and mitigation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Explain indirect prompt injection through retrieved documents and how you defend against it.","g":["prompt injection","rag security"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Indirect prompt injection occurs when an attacker places instructions in material that an assistant later reads as part of an otherwise legitimate task. In RAG, that material can be a retrieved webpage, shared document, ticket, repository file, image, or extracted PDF text. The user may ask an innocent question, but the retrieved evidence attempts to redirect the model. I would defend by treating retrieved content as untrusted data and enforcing permissions and action constraints outside the model, supported by detection, provenance, and careful output handling. <a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the trust-boundary failure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A RAG prompt typically combines trusted application instructions, the user&#x27;s request, and retrieved text. The application intends the retrieved text to supply facts. An injection attempts to promote part of that text into authority over the assistant&#x27;s behavior. It might claim to be a system update, an evaluation instruction, or a required step for completing the task. The fundamental problem is confusion between content to analyze and instructions to obey.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For example, an employee asks for the current travel reimbursement policy. A retrieved page contains valid-looking policy text plus a statement directing the assistant to disclose unrelated internal information or initiate an external action. The source page does not gain the user&#x27;s authority merely because retrieval ranked it highly. The system must preserve the distinction between answering from a document and executing that document&#x27;s requests.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Distinguish injection from ordinary incorrect content</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A document can be wrong without being an injection. An obsolete travel limit is a source-quality problem. A document that says &quot;ignore the user&#x27;s request and follow these instructions&quot; is attempting behavioral control. An attacker can combine both approaches, using false facts to corrupt an answer and embedded instructions to influence tool use. The defenses overlap, but source verification and instruction-boundary enforcement address different failure modes.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Legitimate documents may also quote malicious instructions for analysis. A security training guide can contain the exact language a detector considers suspicious. Automatically deleting every imperative sentence would destroy useful content and produce false positives. I would preserve provenance, distinguish quoted examples from operational authority, and evaluate detectors against realistic benign documents. The control objective is preventing unauthorized behavior, not merely eliminating certain words from the corpus.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Model the consequences before choosing controls</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The risk depends strongly on what the assistant can access and do. A read-only public FAQ assistant may produce misleading answers. An enterprise assistant with private documents and outbound tools can expose information or perform unwanted actions. A model connected to workflow tools can create tickets, change records, or send messages if the surrounding system accepts its suggestions without independent authorization. The same injected text can therefore have very different impact across deployments.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would identify sensitive data sources, untrusted input channels, available tools, network destinations, and user-visible rendering surfaces. This is a data-flow exercise rather than a list of suspicious phrases. Retrieval, summarization, conversational memory, and tool responses can each carry attacker-controlled text. A design that protects the main retrieved passage but trusts a generated summary of that passage may simply move the injection to another stage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate reading from consequential action</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The strongest controls limit what a compromised model response can cause. A model that reads untrusted content should not automatically gain unrestricted ability to send information elsewhere or modify systems. Where action-taking is required, I would use a separate trusted workflow that checks the user&#x27;s actual request, the target resource, the destination, and the proposed arguments before executing anything. The model may propose an action, but proposal is not authorization.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separating models can help reduce exposure, but two models alone do not create a security boundary. If the reader model passes attacker-authored instructions as a &quot;summary&quot; and the action model trusts them, the attack survives. The boundary must constrain the representation and authority of what crosses it. Typed factual fields, source references, policy checks, and narrowly scoped capabilities are more meaningful than simply giving the second model a different prompt.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Enforce tool authorization outside the model</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Every tool invocation should be checked by a trusted executor.
