The executor verifies that the tool is allowed for the user and task, the resource belongs to the authorized scope, and the arguments satisfy a strict schema and policy. A document cannot authorize sending a message, changing an access policy, or uploading data. Approval for a general assistant session does not imply approval for every action that appears in retrieved text.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use capability-limited tools rather than broad network or shell access where possible. A tool that searches one approved knowledge source is easier to constrain than a tool that can fetch arbitrary destinations with arbitrary payloads. Destination allowlists, resource-scoped credentials, bounded query parameters, and transaction limits reduce potential impact. These controls must be enforced in code or infrastructure because an injected prompt may explicitly instruct the model to ignore a textual restriction.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Control outbound data paths</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Information can leave through more than an explicit email tool. Model-generated links, image URLs, webhook destinations, analytics parameters, or browser navigation can carry sensitive content. A user interface that automatically loads remote images from an answer may initiate a network request without a separate model tool call. I would constrain remote resource loading, sanitize rendered markup, and avoid automatically following model-generated URLs when sensitive evidence is in context.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Network egress policies should match the application&#x27;s task requirements. If the assistant needs external lookups, route them through approved tools with destination and payload controls. Logging pipelines are also outbound paths: sending full prompts to an unapproved monitoring service can disclose data even when the final answer is harmless. The architecture should make legitimate data movement explicit and auditable rather than relying on the model to recognize every exfiltration attempt.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve instruction hierarchy structurally</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retrieved content should be clearly labeled as evidence, separated from application instructions, and associated with immutable source identifiers. The system instruction should state that source text cannot redefine the assistant&#x27;s role, tool permissions, or disclosure policy. Delimiters and structured fields help the model interpret the intended boundary and can reduce accidental confusion. They are useful defenses, but they are not a proof that the model will always respect the boundary.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Avoid assembling prompts by concatenating arbitrary source text into instruction-like templates without escaping or labeling. If a document contains XML-like tags, Markdown headings, or role labels, the application should not let those characters alter the actual message structure. The model may still interpret their meaning, but the application can at least prevent syntactic corruption of its own prompt format. Trusted instructions should be generated from controlled templates rather than from retrieved content.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Treat ingestion sanitization as a supporting control</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Parsing should expose hidden or unusual content consistently so reviewers and detectors can inspect what the model will see. Invisible characters, encoded fragments, hidden HTML, and PDF text layers can create a mismatch between human-visible documents and extracted model input. I would retain the original file, record parsing decisions, and flag suspicious differences. Simply stripping every zero-width character is inappropriate because some languages and legitimate documents use such characters meaningfully.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Normalization and scanning should therefore be format-aware and reversible where practical. A detector can flag instruction-like content, unusual rendering discrepancies, or suspicious destinations for quarantine or review. It should not silently rewrite policy text in ways that change its meaning. The ingestion pipeline also needs limits on document size, extraction complexity, and decompression behavior so malicious content cannot exhaust resources before the model is involved.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use provenance without confusing it with authority</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each passage should identify its source, author or connector identity where available, publication state, revision, and trust classification. Curated policy documentation can receive different treatment from a public forum post. Newly ingested external content may require review before entering a sensitive workflow. Source trust can guide retrieval and verification, but even an official repository can be compromised or contain a quoted injection example.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Trust in factual content and authority to instruct the assistant should remain separate. A company policy can establish the reimbursement limit; it cannot grant the assistant permission to transmit payroll records. Likewise, a highly ranked source is relevant, not privileged. I would avoid any rule that automatically converts &quot;trusted source&quot; into &quot;trusted instructions&quot; because that collapses the security boundary when the source is edited or compromised.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Detect suspicious behavior at several points</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Input detectors can examine retrieved passages for instruction attempts, but attackers can paraphrase, translate, or disguise intent. A model-based detector is itself a model consuming untrusted content and may be influenced. I would use its score as one signal, with calibrated thresholds and a documented response, rather than as a gate that supposedly makes all passing text safe. Detection should be evaluated on unseen attack families as well as benign operational documents.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Output and tool-call checks provide another layer. A sudden request to send private context to a new destination, change a workflow outside the user&#x27;s request, or reveal irrelevant secrets is suspicious regardless of which passage caused it. The executor can block such behavior using policy even when the model&#x27;s answer claims the action is required. Monitoring should connect unusual actions to recently retrieved sources so incidents can be investigated without relying on unsupported model explanations.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle high-consequence actions with concrete review</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When the product requires human approval, the review should show the actual action, destination, resource, and relevant content. Asking a user to approve a vague &quot;continue&quot; operation does not provide informed control. Approval must be bound to the reviewed arguments so the model cannot change the destination or payload afterward. Low-impact, reversible actions may follow a different policy, but that policy should still be established by the application and user intent.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The review interface must also resist injection. A malicious document might insert text that looks like an approval button or falsely claims the user already authorized an action. Render source quotations distinctly from application controls. Do not let arbitrary document markup become trusted interface elements. Security depends on the full workflow, including what the person sees and what the executor actually receives after approval.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect summaries, memory, and multi-step workflows</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An injection may persist beyond one request if the assistant stores a contaminated summary in memory. Future turns may then treat it as established context without remembering its untrusted origin. I would preserve provenance and trust labels through summaries and memory records, avoid storing unverified instructions as user preferences, and provide expiration or correction mechanisms. Derived content does not become trustworthy simply because an earlier model generated it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Multi-step agents also need bounded plans and repeated policy checks. A safe first action does not authorize an unsafe fifth action. Tool results should be treated as data unless they come from a defined trusted control channel. If a retrieved page tells the assistant to expand its search to a new service, the application should assess that request against the user&#x27;s task and allowed capabilities. The model&#x27;s plan is not an independent source of permission.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Test the actual deployment boundary</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would build an authorized red-team suite covering instruction-like documents, hidden extraction differences, conflicting source authority, multilingual content, malicious citation destinations, and contaminated summaries. Tests should exercise the real parser, retriever, prompt builder, renderer, and tool executor. A model-only benchmark can miss a browser image fetch or a logging leak that occurs after generation. The desired outcome is no unauthorized disclosure or action, not merely a refusal phrase in the answer.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Include benign documents that discuss attacks so the system&#x27;s usefulness is measured alongside resistance. Track attack success by consequence, detector false positives, evidence rejection rate, and task completion under protection. Use synthetic secrets and controlled destinations during tests. Re-run representative cases after changes to models, prompts, connectors, parsers, or tools because any of these can change how untrusted material reaches the action boundary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prepare containment and recovery</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If suspicious content is discovered, quarantine the affected source or revision, stop serving dependent cached answers, and identify which requests consumed it. Disable or narrow relevant capabilities when necessary while preserving authorized read-only functionality. Keep source identifiers and version lineage so the team can assess exposure and rebuild derived artifacts. Removing the original document alone may leave summaries, vectors, cached outputs, or memory entries that still carry the malicious material.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Post-incident analysis should distinguish model susceptibility from missing enforcement. If the model proposed an unauthorized action but the executor blocked it, an important boundary worked. If the executor accepted it, the system needs a stronger authorization contract. I would use the incident to add a regression case and improve containment rather than merely adding the attack&#x27;s exact wording to a blacklist that a paraphrase can bypass.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview conclusion</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would describe indirect prompt injection as untrusted source content attempting to acquire instruction authority. My primary defense is a system in which reading that content does not grant the model unrestricted actions or data-transfer capabilities. Trusted executors enforce user intent, authorization, destinations, and argument constraints. Structured prompts, provenance, detectors, sanitization, and human review add useful layers around that boundary.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would avoid claiming that a particular prompt, model, or scanner eliminates the problem. The meaningful engineering claim is narrower: under a defined threat model, the application limits the consequences of model confusion and measures remaining failures. This answer connects the vulnerability to concrete data and action flows, explains why simple filtering is insufficient, and shows how to preserve useful RAG behavior while reducing the impact of attacker-controlled documents.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OWASP: prompt injection risks and mitigation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"What privacy and compliance risks are specific to enterprise RAG, and how do you mitigate them?","g":["rag security"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Enterprise RAG creates privacy risk because it copies source information into many derived systems and then makes that information easier to discover and combine. The relevant data is not limited to the original document: extracted text, embeddings, prompts, answers, caches, traces, evaluation sets, and backups may all retain sensitive information. I would mitigate this by mapping the full data lifecycle, minimizing what each component receives, enforcing source permissions, and making retention, deletion, regional processing, and auditability explicit design requirements.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start with the data flow and processing purpose</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Consider an internal assistant answering questions over HR policies, support tickets, contracts, and project documents. A support ticket can contain a customer&#x27;s email address and account history. An employee document can include health-related or payroll information. Even if the assistant is intended to answer general operational questions, broad ingestion may copy information that the product does not need. The first control is therefore deciding which sources and fields belong in the system at all.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would draw the lifecycle from source connector through parsing, storage, embedding, retrieval, generation, observability, and deletion. For every transition, record what data moves, which organization or vendor processes it, where it is processed, who can access it, and how long it remains. This inventory turns vague privacy concerns into concrete engineering decisions. It also reveals overlooked processors such as OCR services, rerankers, exception trackers, and support dashboards.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate engineering controls from legal conclusions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The applicable obligations depend on jurisdiction, data category, organizational role, customer agreements, and intended use. I would work from the organization&#x27;s approved requirements rather than declaring that one technical feature makes the product compliant everywhere. A private model, encryption, or a zero-retention option addresses some risks but does not establish an appropriate processing purpose or complete compliance with every relevant rule.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an EU GDPR example, Article 5 includes data minimization, storage limitation, integrity, confidentiality, and accountability principles. Article 17 provides erasure rights under specified conditions and includes exceptions. An implementation should support deletion and retention decisions, while the organization determines how those provisions apply to particular records. It would be inaccurate to promise that every record must always be erased immediately or that every audit log may be retained forever. <a href=\"https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:32016R0679\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Minimize collection and ingestion</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would begin with source allowlists, supported content categories, and field-level exclusion rules. If the assistant answers policy questions, it may need the policy text without individual employee cases. If support histories are necessary, remove irrelevant attachments, secrets, payment data, and unnecessary personal fields before creating downstream copies. Document owners should understand what is indexed and which audiences can query it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">PII detection is useful but imperfect. Names, addresses, identifiers, and context-dependent sensitive facts cannot all be recognized reliably by a simple pattern matcher. A detector should be evaluated by source type and language, with a path for handling uncertain content. Over-redaction can also damage the task: removing every customer identifier may prevent correct account-specific retrieval. The goal is necessary and controlled processing, not indiscriminate deletion of every entity.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose masking or tokenization deliberately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Irreversible redaction removes information from the downstream text. Reversible tokenization replaces a value with a reference that a separate protected mapping can resolve. For some tasks, a model can reason over placeholders such as CUSTOMER_17 without receiving the actual name. Rehydration then occurs only for an authorized user and output context. This reduces exposure but creates a sensitive mapping service that needs strong authorization and retention controls.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Tokenization is not automatically anonymization. Context can still identify a person, and stable tokens can link their records across documents. Rehydration can also disclose more information than the user&#x27;s request requires if it is applied indiscriminately. I would define which fields may be restored, for which principals, and at which stage. The mapping should not be included in model prompts merely for convenience, because that would defeat the separation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Treat embeddings as sensitive derived data</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Embeddings are numerical representations, but they should not be assumed to remove the sensitivity of their inputs. They encode information about the text and can support linkage or inference. Exact reconstruction risk varies with the model, access available to an attacker, and auxiliary information, so I would avoid claiming that every vector can be perfectly reversed. The safe engineering default is to protect vectors according to the underlying data classification.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">That means access controls, encryption, regional placement, deletion mapping, and limited administrative exposure for the vector database. A vector-only export may still be a sensitive export. Query embeddings can also reveal information about user interests or private requests. Evaluation datasets containing vectors and source identifiers need the same scrutiny as text datasets. Numerical format is a storage representation, not a blanket privacy guarantee.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Assess every external processor</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Embedding calls transmit source text, often at ingestion scale. Generation calls transmit the user question and selected evidence. A hosted reranker may receive both. OCR and document parsing services may receive original files containing more data than the final extracted passage. I would evaluate each processor&#x27;s approved use, security controls, retention terms, training-use terms, regional options, subprocessors, and operational access rather than checking only the final language-model provider.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Provider features and contracts change, so implementation must use the specific approved configuration and agreement. A marketing statement about privacy should not be treated as the complete service contract. The application should also handle error paths: retries, debug payloads, and exception logs can retain text even when the main successful request follows a strict retention path. I would verify the full request lifecycle with representative sensitive documents.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design regional processing end to end</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Data residency is broader than choosing a regional vector database. Source fetching, embedding, reranking, inference, logging, backups, support access, and failover can each move data. I would maintain a regional dependency map and route each tenant through approved processing paths. A cross-region fallback that restores availability may violate the product&#x27;s processing constraints, so its behavior must be decided in advance.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Regional requirements can affect model choice and capacity. If an approved region lacks enough inference capacity, the service may need queueing, a smaller approved model, or a clear temporary failure response. It should not silently reroute private prompts elsewhere. Backups and disaster recovery need the same attention. An architecture is only region-bound if its secondary systems and emergency procedures respect the same contract as normal traffic.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve permissions and prevent excessive disclosure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Source-level authorization must carry through to chunks, summaries, caches, and citations. An employee&#x27;s ability to search a department folder does not imply permission to every file in the enterprise. Effective permissions can change independently of document content, so group removals and sharing changes need their own synchronization path. For sensitive deployments, selected resources may require a current authorization check before their text reaches an external processor or the user.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Even authorized users should receive information relevant to their task. A broad answer that unnecessarily repeats personal details increases exposure. The generator should have a bounded evidence set and a clear answer scope. Combining several individually accessible facts can reveal a sensitive inference that no single source states. Some applications therefore need purpose- or attribute-based controls beyond a simple document allow-list, particularly when access was granted for a limited operational role.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Control extraction through repeated querying</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An assistant can make large-scale collection easier by summarizing many documents rapidly. Document ACLs do not automatically prevent an authorized account from extracting its entire accessible corpus through repeated queries. I would consider export limits, query-rate controls, anomaly monitoring, and product-specific restrictions on bulk enumeration. These controls should reflect legitimate workflows so normal analysts are not blocked without a reason.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Responses and errors can also reveal existence. Repeated probes about a person, project, or document title may expose metadata even when the content is withheld. Return consistent authorization-safe behavior and avoid revealing protected counts or names. Monitoring should focus on behavior and relevant risk signals, with appropriate privacy controls for the monitoring system itself. Collecting every user query indefinitely would create another unnecessary surveillance dataset.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build deletion around lineage</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deletion is difficult when the system cannot locate all derived copies. I would maintain mappings from source documents and revisions to chunks, vectors, generated summaries, cache dependencies, and evaluation records. A subject or data-owner index can assist where appropriate, but many documents mention multiple people, and a single subject identifier is not a complete solution. The connector and processing pipeline should preserve enough lineage to locate affected artifacts reliably.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A deletion workflow can immediately make a resource ineligible for retrieval, invalidate dependent caches, remove active copies, and schedule physical cleanup according to the approved policy. Tombstones prevent a delayed ingestion retry from restoring deleted content. Reconciliation verifies the resource is absent from serving paths and identifies remaining retention-bound copies. The workflow should record completion and exceptions without storing the deleted sensitive content again in the completion log.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle backups and retained records honestly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Backups may not support surgical deletion of one document. A practical design can enforce non-serving deletion immediately, expire backups on a defined schedule, and replay deletion tombstones before a restored backup becomes queryable. The organization must determine whether that approach meets its obligations and agreements. I would not claim that deleting a vector row proves every historical copy has disappeared.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Some records may have approved retention or legal-hold requirements. These need explicit policy handling, restricted access, and a distinction between retention for a defined purpose and ordinary retrieval availability. Keeping an immutable log containing full prompts indefinitely creates tension with minimization and deletion requirements. Tamper-evident event records with limited metadata are often easier to govern than permanent copies of every private conversation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design observability to minimize content exposure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Operational debugging usually needs timing, identifiers, configuration versions, and failure categories more often than raw document text. I would log these fields by default and keep content capture exceptional, access-controlled, sampled, and time-limited. Prompt and completion logs can be particularly sensitive because they combine multiple source documents with user intent. A trace collector may become the most concentrated sensitive dataset in the entire system.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Where content is necessary for quality review, use an approved review environment with scoped access and redaction where useful. Separate routine service metrics from protected evidence inspection. Developers should not automatically receive access to every tenant&#x27;s documents because they maintain the application. Support access, data exports, and debugging sessions should be auditable. Test failure paths to ensure secrets and private text do not appear in stack traces or alert messages.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Govern evaluation and training reuse</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Production failures are valuable evaluation examples, but copying them into a golden dataset can extend retention and access beyond the original purpose. I would classify evaluation records, preserve source dependencies, redact unnecessary identifiers, and set deletion behavior. Synthetic examples can reduce exposure, but they should not be mistaken for a complete substitute for representative authorized evaluation. The review process needs both usefulness and a clear data-handling contract.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Using RAG content to fine-tune a model is a separate decision from retrieving it at inference time. Fine-tuning can make removal and provenance more difficult because information may become encoded in parameters. A document&#x27;s presence in a search index should not automatically authorize its use in training. I would keep retrieval data, evaluation data, and training datasets distinguishable, with separate approved purposes and lineage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make auditability proportionate</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">To investigate an answer, retain a request reference, serving configuration, source revision identifiers, permission-decision context, and selected citation mappings where permitted. This can establish which evidence and rules were used without storing every plaintext prompt forever. Exact replay may require access to historical source versions, which has its own retention implications. The audit requirement should state whether exact reproduction or a sufficient decision record is needed.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Protect audit records against unauthorized alteration and access. A tamper-evident design supports accountability, but immutability does not justify unlimited sensitive content retention. Audit who accessed protected traces and why. Report deletion and retention exceptions through approved processes. The engineering goal is evidence that the system follows its declared controls, not a decorative dashboard that cannot reconstruct a permission failure or locate an affected source revision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify the lifecycle with realistic exercises</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would test a full data lifecycle: ingest a synthetic sensitive record, answer an authorized question, revoke access, delete the record, invalidate caches, and restore a backup into an isolated environment. Verify that the restored service applies tombstones before serving. Repeat with parser failures, connector retries, and model-provider errors. These exercises reveal copies and paths that static architecture diagrams often miss.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that enterprise RAG privacy is a derived-data lifecycle problem. I would minimize collection, approve every processor, preserve authorization, protect embeddings, control regional routing, and make deletion and retention executable. Compliance claims require the organization&#x27;s specific requirements and evidence; the architecture&#x27;s contribution is to make those requirements enforceable, observable, and testable across every copy of the data.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX:32016R0679\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">EU GDPR: Articles 5 and 17 in the official regulation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How would an attacker poison a RAG corpus, and how do you detect it?","g":["rag security"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">RAG corpus poisoning is the deliberate introduction or modification of material so that the retrieval-and-generation pipeline produces an attacker-desired result. The attacker may plant false facts, impersonate an authoritative source, or combine misleading content with instructions aimed at the assistant. I would defend through controlled ingestion, source provenance, evidence validation, retrieval monitoring, and fast containment. No single vector anomaly or text classifier reliably distinguishes all malicious content from legitimate updates. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.07867\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the attacker&#x27;s influence</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The first question is what the attacker can change. They may control a public webpage, submit a support ticket, upload a shared file, edit a low-trust wiki, or compromise a connector account. Each entry point has a different audience and publication workflow. A ticket that only its submitter can read presents a different risk from a ticket automatically indexed into a company-wide troubleshooting assistant. The same content becomes more dangerous when ingestion broadens its effective reach.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would map write permissions, connector credentials, publication states, and downstream retrieval scopes. A source may be trusted for storage integrity while its individual contributors are not equally authoritative. For example, the connector can faithfully ingest a wiki page that an unauthorized author edited. Authentic transport does not prove trustworthy content. The threat model should identify who can influence facts, metadata, rankings, and lifecycle state, not just who can insert a new document.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate poisoning objectives</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An attacker might want one specific incorrect answer, a broad decline in answer quality, reputational damage to a source, or disclosure through a combined injection. Targeted factual poisoning can work without any instruction-like phrase. A page can simply assert a false refund policy in polished, authoritative language. A detector looking only for &quot;ignore previous instructions&quot; would miss the actual threat because the malicious behavior is ordinary-looking misinformation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Availability and integrity are different goals. Flooding a corpus with low-quality duplicates may crowd out useful evidence or increase indexing cost. Altering a policy date can make an obsolete rule appear current. A compromised metadata field can misrepresent source ownership. I would define success in terms of the consequence: wrong claim, unauthorized disclosure, missing evidence, or service disruption. This makes defenses and evaluation more meaningful than counting suspicious documents alone.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Understand why retrieval can amplify poisoned content</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retrieval promotes a tiny part of a large corpus into the model&#x27;s immediate context. Content designed to match common terms or likely questions can receive disproportionate exposure. A false answer embedded in otherwise relevant text may be especially persuasive because it appears to directly resolve the user&#x27;s request. Dense similarity, lexical relevance, and a cross-encoder&#x27;s relevance score do not establish that the passage is factually correct.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Near-duplicates can create an illusion of corroboration. If several copied pages repeat the same unsupported claim, the generator may treat repetition as agreement across sources. In reality, all copies may derive from one attacker-controlled origin. I would track canonical source families and content similarity so evidence diversity reflects independent provenance rather than the raw number of matching chunks. Duplicate removal is therefore a quality and integrity control, not only a token optimization.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Control publication into important corpora</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For customer-facing policy answers, I would distinguish draft, contributor-submitted, reviewed, and published material. Only approved publication states should feed authoritative answer paths. High-impact changes may require designated owners or review. This adds friction, so it should be targeted to the sources and claims where integrity matters. Internal exploratory search may allow broader material while clearly preserving its lower trust and provenance.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A connector should use narrowly scoped credentials and an explicit source allowlist. New source registration, ownership changes, and unusually broad permission changes should be observable. Avoid automatically promoting every user upload into a shared knowledge base. A user&#x27;s ability to add a document to their own workspace does not imply authority to make it a company-wide policy source. The ingestion product should make these publication consequences clear.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve provenance at chunk and revision level</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each chunk should retain its tenant, source resource, revision, author or connector identity where available, publication status, timestamps, canonical source, and parsing version. This lineage supports ranking, review, and incident response. A chunk&#x27;s display title should not be the only evidence of authority because titles are often editable. Trust metadata should come from authenticated source or application state, not from claims inside the document body.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Content signatures or hashes can establish that bytes have not changed since a recorded point, but they do not establish factual truth. A signed malicious document remains malicious. Similarly, a verified domain may host compromised or user-generated material. I would use provenance to support accountable publication and source comparison, while evaluating the claims themselves when the task requires it. Integrity controls and truth validation solve related but distinct problems.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Inspect ingestion anomalies as leads</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Useful signals include sudden upload volume, repeated text across many new documents, unexpected author activity, unusual link destinations, malformed markup, and a large change to a high-traffic source. Compare these signals with each source&#x27;s normal behavior. A documentation release can legitimately create many similar pages, so a global fixed threshold may generate excessive false alarms. Alerts should carry source context and a clear review path.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Embedding statistics can be exploratory signals, but they should not be oversold. A vector near many queries may represent a genuinely useful overview. A successful targeted poison may look statistically ordinary and affect only a few important questions. High-dimensional distances also vary by embedding model and preprocessing. I would validate any proposed vector anomaly detector against known benign changes and controlled attack cases before using it to block publication.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Scan content without relying on a blacklist</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Text inspection can identify instruction attempts, suspicious hidden content, unsupported authority claims, and inconsistencies with structured policy fields. Format-aware parsing can reveal differences between visible pages and extracted model text. These controls help with combined poisoning and injection, but a plausible false sentence can pass them all. Detection should therefore incorporate provenance, publication state, and factual conflict signals rather than only suspicious wording.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Quarantine is often preferable to silent deletion when evidence is uncertain. A reviewer should see the source revision, changed spans, relevant policy ownership, and likely affected queries. Review feedback becomes training or calibration data for the detector. Keep the process proportionate: a harmless new troubleshooting note should not wait indefinitely because it contains a quoted security example. False positives and publication delay are real product costs to measure.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Monitor retrieval exposure, not just ingestion volume</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A small poisoned addition may have a large effect if it appears in many high-value queries. I would track per-source and per-chunk retrieval frequency, rank distribution, selection into final context, and citation frequency. Compare exposure changes with traffic changes and publication events. A sudden rise after an edit is a useful investigation signal, especially when one source begins displacing several previously useful authoritative passages.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exposure should be sliced by tenant, query category, and source family. Global counts can hide a poison targeting one product or region. Conversely, a popular new release note may legitimately become common across many queries. Alerts should identify which answers changed and what evidence was replaced. Merely reporting that a vector became &quot;central&quot; does not tell an operator whether users are receiving a false warranty rule or a useful updated overview.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Detect conflicts in high-value facts</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For important policy or operational facts, structured checks can compare retrieved claims with approved sources of record. Examples include plan entitlements, effective dates, account status, or published support windows. If a low-trust passage conflicts with a canonical policy, the system can prefer the approved source, abstain, or escalate for review. The appropriate response depends on whether the canonical source is complete and current.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cross-source agreement is only persuasive when the sources are independent and applicable. Five copies of one blog post do not outweigh one current official policy. Old policy revisions can also create apparent conflict without malicious activity. I would preserve effective dates and source relationships so the system can distinguish legitimate historical changes from suspicious contradictions. A blanket &quot;majority vote across documents&quot; is vulnerable to duplication and does not establish truth.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make ranking aware of source properties</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Ranking can consider relevance alongside publication state, source authority, freshness, and document version. These signals should be explicit and inspectable. Blindly boosting all recent content can reward an attacker who republishes false information with a fresh timestamp. Blindly preferring official-looking titles can reward impersonation. Trusted metadata must originate outside the document&#x27;s own assertions, and ranking changes should be evaluated for legitimate-source suppression.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For sensitive tasks, the system may require at least one approved source for decisive claims rather than allowing low-trust content to stand alone. This is a task policy, not a property of the embedding model. Less sensitive exploratory answers can present uncertainty and source differences. The product should tell users when evidence is provisional instead of allowing the generator&#x27;s fluent style to imply a level of authority that the source does not have.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep authorization and injection controls in place</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Poisoning defenses do not replace tenant isolation or prompt-injection controls. A document can be authorized for the user and still malicious. It can also be perfectly factual but unauthorized. If a poisoned passage includes behavioral instructions, the tool executor must still reject actions outside the user&#x27;s request. A high relevance or trust score should never grant a document authority to change access policies or send private data elsewhere.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarly, allowing an assistant to browse a linked source does not automatically authorize it to fetch every destination mentioned in that source. Bound external access and rendering behavior according to the application policy. The goal is to prevent a corpus-integrity incident from escalating into a broader compromise. A read-only factual error is serious, but its blast radius can be smaller than an agent that combines the same poisoned evidence with unrestricted actions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a controlled evaluation program</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would create synthetic poisoning cases in an isolated test corpus using fictitious facts and controlled sources. Vary source trust, duplicate count, publication state, conflicting authoritative evidence, metadata changes, and whether the poisoned text contains explicit instructions. Include targeted questions and unrelated queries to measure both attack impact and collateral retrieval disruption. The evaluation should exercise the real ingestion and retrieval pipeline rather than inserting text directly into the final prompt.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Measure how often poisoned material is retrieved, selected, cited, and adopted as a final claim. Also measure whether defenses block legitimate updates or force unnecessary abstention. An ingestion detector with excellent precision may still miss the one high-impact targeted document. An aggressive quarantine policy may prevent attacks while making the knowledge base unusably stale. Report both security outcomes and normal-task performance to expose this trade-off.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prepare containment that reaches derived artifacts</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a source is confirmed malicious, mark the affected revision ineligible immediately and identify all dependent chunks, summaries, caches, and answers. Revoke or disable compromised connector credentials where appropriate. Preserve a restricted incident record before cleanup so the team can reconstruct what happened. The containment path should not require re-embedding the entire corpus before dangerous content stops appearing in retrieval.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Versioned indexes can support rollback, but an alias flip is not automatically safe. The older index may contain stale ACLs, deleted documents, or the same poisoned source from an earlier revision. A rollback must preserve current revocations and deletions. Sometimes a targeted deny-list of affected resource revisions followed by controlled rebuilding is safer than reverting everything. I would test both approaches and define which incident conditions justify each one.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Investigate exposure with evidence lineage</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Request traces should reveal which source revision influenced each answer, subject to privacy and retention constraints. This enables an exposure assessment: which tenants saw the source, which claims were produced, and whether any unauthorized actions were attempted. A source appearing in a candidate list is different from being included in the final prompt or cited in the answer. The lineage should preserve those distinctions to avoid overstating or understating the incident.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">After containment, identify the failed control: unauthorized publication, compromised connector, ranking overexposure, missing conflict handling, or unsafe tool execution. Add the case to regression evaluation and improve the relevant boundary. Simply adding the malicious sentence to a blocklist is fragile because the next attack can express the same false claim differently. The durable fix should address why that source could acquire influence without appropriate review or verification.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would describe corpus poisoning as an integrity attack on both the knowledge base and its retrieval exposure. The attacker needs content that enters an eligible source, reaches the candidate pool, survives selection, and influences generation. Controls can break that chain at publication, provenance, ranking, claim validation, or action execution. Monitoring then detects suspicious influence and supports rapid containment when prevention misses something.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The most important qualification is that neither relevance scoring nor anomaly detection proves truth. A false passage can look natural, and a legitimate update can look unusual. I would combine accountable source ownership, approved publication paths, representative evaluation, and versioned evidence lineage. That creates a defensible system in which malicious changes are harder to promote, their effects are observable, and their removal reaches every derived artifact that could continue serving them.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.07867\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">PoisonedRAG: knowledge corruption attacks</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OWASP: prompt injection risks and mitigation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Design an evaluation strategy for RAG that covers both pre-deploy and production.","g":["rag observability"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would evaluate RAG as both a sequence of components and a complete user-facing system. Before deployment, controlled datasets establish whether a change improves retrieval, evidence use, answer correctness, and safety under known conditions. In production, sampled review, experiments, operational telemetry, and user outcomes reveal failures the offline set missed. The two loops should share versioned cases and trace information, but they answer different questions. A passing offline benchmark is evidence for release, not a permanent guarantee of quality. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15217\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the task contract before choosing metrics</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An internal policy assistant should answer current policy questions with applicable citations and abstain when evidence is missing. A research assistant may need to compare conflicting sources. A support assistant may be judged partly by successful issue resolution. These products require different rubrics. I would define correctness, completeness, evidence support, acceptable uncertainty, and prohibited disclosures in terms of the actual task rather than treating a generic RAG score as sufficient.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separate answerability from answer quality. A question may be unanswerable because the authorized corpus lacks the information, because it is ambiguous, or because the source itself is incomplete. A correct system response may ask a clarification or state the limitation. If the evaluation assumes every question requires a substantive answer, it rewards fabrication. The dataset should explicitly label the expected behavior for missing evidence, conflicting evidence, and incomplete user constraints.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Construct a representative versioned dataset</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would combine authorized production samples, expert-written cases, known incidents, and carefully reviewed synthetic questions. Each record should include the question, relevant tenant and permission context, applicable source snapshot, expected answer properties, supporting passages, and acceptable abstention behavior. Reference answers are useful, but a single wording should not define the only correct response. The rubric should permit equivalent phrasing while requiring the decisive facts and conditions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Slice the dataset by query type, language, tenant size, document format, freshness requirement, permission selectivity, and difficulty. Include exact identifier lookups, paraphrases, multi-hop questions, tables, long documents, temporal comparisons, no-answer cases, and adversarial source content. Weighting should reflect the production distribution while retaining enough difficult cases to detect rare serious failures. A dataset containing only popular easy questions can give misleadingly strong results.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate development and final assessment</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use a development set for prompt tuning, retrieval parameters, chunking choices, and judge calibration. Keep a held-out set for final comparison, with a process to prevent repeated tuning against its failures. If every held-out failure is immediately used to optimize the next run, that set eventually becomes development data. Periodic refresh and new source snapshots help maintain a meaningful generalization check.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Regression cases serve another purpose: they ensure known failures do not recur. They can be included in a dedicated suite even when the broader benchmark is held out. I would distinguish public-to-the-team regression cases from protected final-assessment cases rather than pretending one dataset can serve both roles indefinitely. Production examples also need privacy controls, retention rules, and source-deletion lineage before they become reusable evaluation assets.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Measure candidate retrieval independently</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Recall@k asks what fraction of labeled relevant passages appear within the first k retrieved items. Suppose a question has four required relevant passages and retrieval returns three of them among its top twenty. Passage Recall@20 is three divided by four, or 0.75. For a question answerable from any one of several equivalent passages, a hit-rate or evidence-sufficiency metric may better reflect the task than treating every equivalent passage as mandatory.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Distinguish answer-bearing evidence from topical relevance. A passage mentioning refunds without the refund deadline may be related but insufficient. For multi-hop questions, measure whether the complete supporting set is recovered. Also distinguish ANN recall against exact nearest neighbors from relevance recall against human-labeled evidence. The former diagnoses index approximation; the latter assesses whether the representation and search strategy find useful information.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain ranking metrics concretely</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Mean reciprocal rank is useful when the first relevant result matters. If the first relevant passage appears at rank four, reciprocal rank is one fourth. Average that value across questions. It does not fully capture whether later results contain complementary evidence, so it should not be the only metric for multi-source synthesis. A system can improve the first relevant rank while losing an important exception lower in the list.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Normalized discounted cumulative gain evaluates graded relevance and position. With one common formulation, DCG@k sums each result&#x27;s gain, defined as two to the relevance grade minus one, divided by log base two of its rank plus one. IDCG is the same calculation for the best possible ordering of the labeled items. nDCG is DCG divided by IDCG. Higher-quality evidence near the top receives more credit than the same evidence buried later.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For grades zero, three, and two at ranks one through three, DCG is approximately 5.917. The ideal ordering three, two, zero has DCG approximately 8.893, giving nDCG about 0.665. This example shows why order matters even when the same items are present. Define how queries with no relevant items are handled, and document the grading rubric. Without consistent labels, a precise formula produces a misleading comparison.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate final context selection</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Candidate retrieval and prompt assembly are separate stages. A system may retrieve the correct evidence at rank thirty and then discard it before generation. I would measure evidence sufficiency after reranking, deduplication, expansion, and compression. Inspect whether the final context includes required conditions, table headers, applicable dates, and source identifiers. Track redundant tokens and the share of prompt space occupied by irrelevant or duplicated passages.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Compression needs dedicated checks because it can change meaning. Removing a negation or exception can produce a concise but misleading context. For source-preserving extraction, verify that selected spans exist in the original. For generated summaries, evaluate support and important omissions. A lower token count is an efficiency gain only if the final task quality remains acceptable. Context ordering and truncation should be included in the actual evaluation configuration.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate generation on several dimensions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Answer correctness asks whether the response satisfies the task under the applicable evidence and constraints. Faithfulness asks whether factual claims are supported by the supplied evidence. These can diverge: a model can faithfully repeat an incorrect source, or produce a true statement unsupported by the allowed context. Completeness asks whether the response covers the required parts. Relevance asks whether it addresses the user&#x27;s question without unnecessary material.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Citation evaluation should check identifier validity, claim-to-source support, source applicability, and coverage of important claims. A valid citation identifier does not prove that its source supports the sentence. Numerical answers should also be checked for arithmetic and unit consistency. For structured outputs, schema validation establishes shape, while semantic validation establishes whether fields contain the right values. Each metric should correspond to a distinct failure that matters to the product.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use the strongest available evaluator</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deterministic checks work well for exact identifiers, arithmetic, schemas, known source spans, and unauthorized resource exposure. Human rubrics are valuable for nuanced correctness and usefulness. Model-based judges can scale parts of the review, but they need calibration against humans and known positive and negative cases. A judge may favor fluent language, follow an injected instruction, or share the generator&#x27;s mistaken assumptions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would require judges to assess explicit criteria and identify supporting evidence rather than output an unexplained overall score. Keep generator and judge configurations versioned. Test whether deliberately unsupported claims, swapped dates, missing exceptions, and irrelevant citations reduce scores as expected. Judge agreement is evidence about consistency, not proof of truth. Some high-consequence categories should continue receiving human review even when automated scores appear strong.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a release gate with meaningful thresholds</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">CI should run a fast representative suite on changes to prompts, retrieval, parsing, chunking, models, and authorization logic. Larger evaluations can run before release or on a scheduled cadence. Gate on predefined quality and operational requirements, including critical slices. For example, a candidate should not improve average correctness while introducing cross-tenant disclosure or a major regression on no-answer behavior. Some failures require absolute rejection rather than a weighted trade-off.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Account for sampling uncertainty. A one-point improvement on a tiny dataset may be noise, while a serious failure hidden in the mean may be material. Paired comparisons on the same questions and source snapshot improve sensitivity. Bootstrap intervals or appropriate statistical tests can quantify uncertainty when their assumptions fit. The release decision should consider effect size, confidence, operational cost, and severity rather than a single green dashboard indicator.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use shadow evaluation before exposure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Replay or shadow a bounded sample of production traffic against the candidate while the incumbent remains user-visible. Compare retrieved evidence, output properties, latency, and errors. Preserve the same authorization context and record source revisions so differences are interpretable. Avoid assuming disagreements are automatically candidate failures; the incumbent can be wrong. Human review should adjudicate a useful sample of disagreements and a random sample of agreements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Shadow traffic finds workload gaps such as unusually long prompts, narrow ACLs, rare document formats, and traffic bursts. It does not measure user reactions to the candidate because users never see it. It can also double processing cost and create additional sensitive traces, so its scope should be deliberate. Shadowing retrieval alone may answer initial questions before running expensive duplicate generation on a smaller subset.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Run production experiments at the right unit</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An A/B test can measure whether a candidate improves actual user outcomes. Choose randomization at the request, user, session, or tenant level according to contamination risks. If users ask related follow-ups, switching models within one conversation can mix conditions. Tenant-level assignment avoids some interference but may require more traffic because tenant behavior varies. Predefine the outcome, minimum effect of interest, duration, and stopping rules.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Business proxies need interpretation. Fewer escalations may reflect better resolution or users giving up. Citation clicks can indicate useful evidence or distrust. A low &quot;I do not know&quot; rate can mean strong coverage or overconfident fabrication. I would combine product outcomes with sampled correctness and safety review. Experiments should not expose users to a candidate that already fails mandatory authorization or security requirements in offline testing.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Monitor production quality and operations separately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Operational metrics include request success, retrieval and reranking latency, time to first token, completion latency, queue depth, token demand, cache validity, source freshness, and cost per request. Quality monitoring includes sampled correctness, supported-claim rate, citation problems, missing-evidence cases, and abstention behavior. Separate these dimensions so a fast but wrong system does not appear healthy and a cautious correct system is not penalized without context.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Slice monitoring by relevant conditions. A global average can hide a regression in one language or one connector. Changes in query mix can also move aggregate scores without any model degradation. Track source ingestion failures and permission lag alongside answer quality because corpus problems often masquerade as model problems. Alert thresholds should correspond to a response plan, such as pausing a rollout, disabling a source, or increasing targeted review.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate resilience and freshness explicitly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Quality testing should include operational changes, not only static question-answer pairs. Update an authoritative policy, remove a document, revoke a user&#x27;s permission, and simulate a connector delay. Verify whether answers follow the promised freshness and authorization behavior. A system that answers yesterday&#x27;s policy perfectly can still fail today&#x27;s task. Record source timestamps and indexed revisions so a stale answer can be attributed to synchronization rather than incorrectly blamed on generation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exercise dependency failures as well. If reranking becomes unavailable, evaluate the actual fusion-only fallback instead of assuming its quality matches the primary path. If a shard times out, check whether partial evidence causes unsupported certainty. These tests establish the quality of degraded operation and determine which failures permit a limited answer versus requiring a clear temporary limitation. Availability is useful only when the returned behavior remains acceptable.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Close the loop without contaminating the benchmark</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a production failure is confirmed, capture the minimum authorized reproduction: question, source revisions, expected behavior, relevant evidence, and stage-level traces. Classify its cause and add a regression case. Keep a record of the fix and verify that the new case detects the original failure. Do not copy private customer material into unrestricted test repositories. Use controlled fixtures or approved protected evaluation storage.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Refresh datasets as the product and corpus change, while preserving historical suites that protect important behavior. Monitor whether the judge itself drifts after a model update. My interview conclusion would be that evaluation is an ongoing engineering loop connecting evidence retrieval, answer behavior, user outcomes, and operations. The system earns confidence through versioned, representative, consequence-aware checks before release and continuous verification after deployment, rather than through one impressive benchmark score.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15217\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Ragas: automated evaluation of RAG</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you detect hallucination in production, at scale, cheaply?","g":["groundedness"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would detect production hallucination through a calibrated cascade: inexpensive checks across all responses, targeted claim verification where useful, stronger model-based review on a representative sample and suspicious cases, and human review to validate the detectors. The goal is to estimate and reduce unsupported or incorrect answers at an acceptable cost, not to claim perfect detection. Some applications can afford a strong verifier on every answer; others need sampling. The correct design follows consequence, traffic, latency, and the strength of available evidence.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define what the detector is judging</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Hallucination is often used loosely to mean any bad answer. I would separate unsupported claims, contradictions of supplied evidence, factual errors in the source, wrong interpretation of the question, and missing qualifications. A response can faithfully quote an outdated policy and still be wrong for today&#x27;s question. Conversely, it can state a true general fact that was not supported by the evidence allowed for this task. These cases require different remediation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose the context states that a refund must be requested within thirty calendar days, except for a particular contract category. An answer saying &quot;every customer has sixty days&quot; contains both an unsupported number and a missing exception. A citation to the correct policy does not make that sentence grounded. The detector should assess the relationship between each important claim, its cited evidence, the applicable source version, and the user&#x27;s conditions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve the actual evidence used</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Verification needs the final context supplied to the generator, including source identifiers, revisions, and any compression. Checking against the entire corpus can hide a generation failure by finding support the model never saw. Checking against an earlier candidate pool can also misdiagnose the result if prompt assembly discarded the relevant passage. I would retain an authorized trace of the selected evidence or a retrievable reference to its exact revision.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The trace must be privacy-conscious. Storing every prompt indefinitely creates another sensitive data store. Resource identifiers, content hashes, configuration versions, and protected short-lived evidence capture may be sufficient for many investigations. Whatever is retained should support the actual verification contract. If historical source text is unavailable, the detector should not pretend it has reconstructed exactly what the generator received.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Apply deterministic checks where they are strong</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Citation identifier validation is cheap: every cited identifier should correspond to a source actually provided, and every link should resolve through the intended access-controlled path. Structured output should pass its schema. Required fields should exist. Known identifiers should match allowed records. These checks catch fabrication of source names, malformed responses, and certain extraction errors without asking another generative model to judge them.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">However, a valid identifier is only a structural property. It does not establish that the cited passage supports the claim. Likewise, matching an entity name or number somewhere in the context is insufficient. A document can mention thirty days for one product and sixty for another. I would treat deterministic checks as precise tests of narrow properties and avoid presenting their pass rate as a comprehensive factual-accuracy score.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify numbers, units, and dates contextually</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Numerical claims are often valuable targets because they matter and can sometimes be checked objectively. Extract the quantity, unit, entity, time period, and condition. If the answer calculates an annual total from monthly figures, recompute the arithmetic with a trusted tool. If it states a deadline, verify the applicable rule and date convention. Calendar days and business days are not interchangeable, even when the numeral matches.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Source extraction can itself be wrong. OCR may turn a decimal point into a comma or detach a value from its column header. The verifier should know whether it is checking against reliable structured fields or noisy text. For table-heavy workloads, preserve row and column relationships so evidence matching is meaningful. A simplistic rule requiring every answer number to occur verbatim in context would incorrectly flag legitimate calculations and fail to explain the real issue.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Do not confuse lexical overlap with grounding</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">N-gram overlap can serve as a weak monitoring feature, but it is not a dependable truth detector. A faithful paraphrase may share few words with the source, while a sentence with a flipped negation can share nearly all of them. Copying a passage verbatim also does not establish applicability to the user&#x27;s question. I would use overlap only as one inexpensive signal whose behavior has been measured on the target workload.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similar caution applies to answer length, hedging phrases, and stylistic confidence. A concise response can be unsupported, and a cautious response can still contain a false claim. The model&#x27;s own statement that it is confident is not an independent verification signal. Good monitoring distinguishes content support from presentation style so it does not reward fluent fabrication or penalize clear paraphrases.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Break answers into checkable claims</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A claim-oriented verifier can split an answer into factual propositions, associate each with supporting evidence, and classify support, contradiction, or insufficient evidence. Important conditions must remain attached. Splitting &quot;Standard customers can export for thirty days after cancellation&quot; into disconnected fragments about customers, exports, and thirty days would lose the relationship that needs verification. Claim extraction quality therefore affects the entire pipeline.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Not every sentence is a factual claim. Questions, recommendations, clearly labeled assumptions, and summaries of uncertainty may need different treatment. I would define a claim rubric and test extraction on multi-part statements, tables, comparisons, and calculations. Evaluate whether the verifier misses unsupported clauses inside otherwise supported sentences. The objective is useful coverage of consequential claims, not maximizing the number of fragments produced.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use entailment models selectively</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A natural-language inference model can compare a claim with a relevant evidence span and estimate entailment, contradiction, or neutrality. This can be cheaper than a large generative judge, especially with batching and short inputs. It may work well for straightforward policy statements or factual extraction. But model size alone does not imply a universal latency, throughput, or detection rate. Benchmark the actual model, hardware, input lengths, and language distribution.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Entailment models can struggle with long contexts, numerical reasoning, multi-hop support, domain terminology, and subtle exceptions. Feeding an entire prompt into a short-context classifier may truncate decisive evidence. A retrieval step within verification can find candidate supporting spans, but that introduces another recall problem. I would calibrate the verifier on representative supported and unsupported claims rather than assuming that its training labels transfer cleanly to enterprise RAG.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calibrate thresholds against human judgments</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Build a labeled calibration set containing supported claims, contradictions, unsupported additions, correct calculations, missing exceptions, and ambiguous cases. Measure detector precision and recall by failure type. A high false-positive rate can overwhelm review queues or cause excessive abstention. A low false-positive detector can still miss many harmful claims. Thresholds should reflect the consequence of each error and the capacity for downstream review.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Consider base rates. If serious unsupported answers occur in one percent of traffic, a detector that flags five percent of otherwise good answers may generate far more false alarms than true ones. Operators need to know the likely composition of the queue, not only an impressive sensitivity figure. I would estimate expected review volume at production QPS and verify that the escalation path can actually process it.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Add a stronger judge where it provides value</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A larger model can assess nuanced claim support, missing conditions, and multi-source synthesis using an explicit rubric. I would send it a bounded representative sample plus cases flagged by cheaper checks, with higher sampling for consequential categories. Its output should identify disputed claims and supporting or contradicting evidence. An unexplained scalar score is less useful for debugging and harder to calibrate.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The judge is not ground truth. It can share the generator&#x27;s misconceptions, favor polished answers, or be influenced by instructions inside evidence. It should receive clearly labeled source data and limited capabilities, and its performance should be checked against human adjudication. Where exact deterministic validation exists, prefer that for the relevant property. A large model should not replace a calculator for arithmetic or an authorization service for permissions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Sample in a way that supports valid estimates</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reviewing only flagged responses estimates the detector queue, not the overall production error rate.
