I would retain a random baseline sample alongside targeted oversampling of difficult slices and alerts. If sampling probabilities differ, use appropriate weighting when estimating population rates. Otherwise a heavily sampled risky category can make the global rate look worse, or a detector that misses a category can make it look artificially clean.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Report uncertainty and sample sizes. Rare severe failures need enough exposure to estimate meaningfully, and a quiet day is not proof of absence. Segment by query type, model version, source connector, language, context length, and freshness. The purpose is to identify actionable changes rather than display one global &quot;hallucination percentage&quot; that combines different failure definitions and sampling schemes.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate verification cost explicitly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">At one thousand answers per second, the system produces 86.4 million answers per day if that rate is sustained. Judging one percent still means 864,000 daily reviews. If each review uses two thousand input tokens, that is about 1.728 billion input tokens per day, before judge outputs. &quot;Only a small sample&quot; can therefore be expensive at scale. Use the actual model&#x27;s approved token prices and capacity limits for budgeting.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Claim-level verification can multiply work further. Five claims per answer at one thousand answers per second create five thousand claim evaluations per second if all are checked. Batching, short evidence spans, selective routing, and asynchronous review can reduce cost, but each has quality or latency implications. The budget should include evidence retrieval, storage, review operations, and remediation, not merely the final judge call.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Decide whether detection happens before or after release</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Asynchronous monitoring is economical for measuring trends and discovering regressions, but it cannot prevent an already displayed error. A real-time gate can block or revise unsupported content before disclosure, at the cost of latency and additional failure dependencies. High-consequence answers may justify that trade-off. Lower-risk general assistance may use cheap real-time checks plus sampled post-response review. The product should state which guarantee it offers.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Streaming complicates intervention. Once a false claim is shown, later verification cannot fully retract its effect. Options include verifying the planned evidence first, buffering a sentence or answer segment, or using a final validation stage before release. None eliminates all errors. I would evaluate user experience and safety together rather than treating immediate token streaming as an unconditional requirement for every workload.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Route failures to the right repair</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If the answer adds an unsupported fact despite sufficient evidence, revise generation instructions, context presentation, or model choice. If the needed evidence was missing, improve retrieval or ask a clarification. If the source is outdated, fix ingestion and freshness. If a numerical result is wrong, use deterministic calculation. A generic &quot;regenerate&quot; button may repeat the same failure because it does not address the actual cause.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Automatic correction should be bounded and reverified. Repeatedly asking the same model to fix itself can produce a different confident error. The system may instead abstain on the unsupported portion while preserving supported information. Capture the original and corrected versions under the approved retention policy so evaluation can determine whether repair improves outcomes or merely hides problems from the dashboard.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use uncertainty and consistency as secondary signals</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Multiple generated answers can disagree, which may identify unstable reasoning. Agreement, however, can reflect a shared wrong assumption. Generating twice also adds cost and latency, so it should be justified by measured value. Token log probabilities can describe confidence under the model&#x27;s distribution but do not directly establish factual correctness. A familiar false claim can receive high probability, and an unusual correct identifier can receive low probability.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would calibrate these signals only on the intended task and avoid converting them into universal truth scores. They may help prioritize review when combined with evidence coverage, source conflict, and deterministic checks. The final system should expose meaningful uncertainty about missing support, rather than an arbitrary confidence percentage whose relationship to actual error has never been measured.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My answer would emphasize a consequence-aware verification cascade with an unbiased monitoring sample. Apply strong cheap checks to narrow objective properties, use calibrated claim verification where it works, spend larger-judge compute on representative and suspicious cases, and keep human review as an external reference. Measure detector misses, false alarms, operational volume, and cost, not only how often a guardrail fires.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would explicitly reject universal promises such as a small classifier catching most hallucinations in every domain or low token probability reliably identifying falsehoods. The defensible production claim is that the service detects defined failure classes with measured accuracy under a specified workload, routes failures to appropriate repairs, and continuously checks the detectors themselves. That is how hallucination monitoring becomes an engineering system rather than another unverified model opinion.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a published factual-consistency metric, see <a href=\"https://aclanthology.org/2023.acl-long.634/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">AlignScore: evaluating factual consistency</a>. Its benchmark findings do not establish a universal latency or accuracy guarantee for the verification cascade proposed here.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://aclanthology.org/2023.acl-long.634/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">AlignScore: evaluating factual consistency</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15217\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Ragas: automated evaluation of RAG</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you handle documents that exceed the context window, and when do you use hierarchical summarisation versus RAPTOR?","g":["multi-stage retrieval"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would choose the strategy according to how much of the document the question actually requires. A localized question usually needs precise retrieval plus nearby context. A whole-document summary requires broad coverage and often hierarchical processing. RAPTOR is an option when repeated queries need both detailed passages and thematic summaries from a relatively stable corpus. A long context window can simplify some cases, but fitting the text into a model does not guarantee that the model uses every relevant detail correctly.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start by measuring the task and the document</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The relevant length is token count under the selected model&#x27;s tokenizer, not page count alone. A four-hundred-page filing may contain dense tables, repeated headers, appendices, or scanned images. OCR can expand or corrupt the text. The available context budget also includes instructions, conversation history, source labels, tool outputs, and reserved answer tokens. I would calculate the usable input budget before deciding whether the original document truly fits.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Question scope matters more than document length. &quot;What notice period applies to termination for convenience?&quot; may be answered from a small contract section and its definitions. &quot;Summarize the major risks across all business segments&quot; requires coverage across many sections. &quot;List every subsidiary&quot; is an exhaustive extraction task, where missing one row matters. These tasks should not all be routed through the same top-five semantic retrieval path.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve structure before choosing a model strategy</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Parsing should retain headings, page references, section order, tables, footnotes, definitions, and cross-references. A table value without its row and column labels is not interpretable evidence. A clause that refers to &quot;the period defined in Section 2&quot; needs a path to that definition. Good document structure improves retrieval, summaries, citations, and verification. Poor extraction cannot be repaired reliably by simply increasing the context window.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would create stable source spans and parent-child relationships. Small chunks support precise retrieval, while parent sections preserve the meaning around them. Store the source revision and document identity so expanded context does not mix incompatible versions. For scanned documents, retain page-image references where visual verification is necessary. The processing plan should distinguish reliable extracted text from uncertain OCR rather than treating both as equally authoritative.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use retrieval with expansion for localized questions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a clause lookup, retrieve small answer-bearing passages, rerank them against the actual question, and expand to the minimum surrounding section needed for interpretation. The expansion might add the heading, a preceding definition, a following exception, or a table legend. This gives the generator focused evidence without processing the whole document. It is often economical because most document tokens never enter the online prompt.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Expansion should be constrained. Pulling every parent to the document root defeats the token-saving purpose and introduces distractions. Track which required conditions remain unresolved and retrieve those specifically. If the clause references a definition elsewhere, follow that reference or issue a targeted second search. Reauthorize all expanded material when permissions vary by section. A child chunk&#x27;s visibility does not automatically authorize the entire parent document.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Recognize the limits of top-k retrieval</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarity search is good at finding likely evidence, but it does not establish exhaustive coverage. If the user asks for every liability exception, the highest-ranked passages may omit a rare exception in an appendix. Increasing k helps only until the context budget is exhausted and still does not prove completeness. I would use section enumeration, structured extraction, or a coverage-aware scan for tasks where missing one item is unacceptable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The same issue applies to global summaries. A few highly similar passages can overrepresent the introduction and miss important later sections. For broad synthesis, the system needs a plan that accounts for the document&#x27;s structure and distribution of content. Retrieval can help identify relevant sections, but the answer should not claim to summarize the entire document unless the processing path actually provides suitable coverage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use hierarchical summarization for broad synthesis</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Hierarchical summarization processes manageable document units, creates intermediate representations, and combines them in later stages. A map stage summarizes sections or extracts facts. A reduce stage synthesizes those results into a broader account. Multiple reduction levels may be necessary. The useful design principle is controlled coverage under a bounded context, rather than the assumption that repeatedly shortening text preserves every important fact.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The map output should follow the task. For a risk summary, it might preserve risk category, affected business unit, severity indicators, supporting quotations, and source spans. For a contract review, it might preserve obligations, parties, deadlines, exceptions, and cross-references. Task-aware structured notes are often easier to verify than generic prose summaries. The reduce stage then has explicit evidence fields rather than a collection of fluent but potentially incomplete paragraphs.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Quantify an illustrative summarization budget</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose a document contains two hundred thousand tokens. Mapping it into twenty-five units of eight thousand tokens processes the original text once, excluding any overlap. If each map output contains six hundred tokens, the first reduction receives roughly fifteen thousand tokens. That may fit in one call or require another level. The total input is more than the original two hundred thousand because intermediate summaries and instructions are processed too.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If overlap is added between map units, account for its duplicated token cost. If map outputs are too short, important details may be lost; if they are too long, reduction becomes expensive and difficult. Actual latency depends on parallelism, rate limits, model throughput, and retries. A map stage can run concurrently, but the reduce stage waits for required outputs. The architecture should budget both critical-path latency and total compute.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect details through the hierarchy</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Summaries are lossy. A section-level summary may drop a qualifier that later becomes essential to the final answer. I would carry important exact facts alongside prose: amounts, units, dates, named entities, exceptions, and source spans. The final synthesizer can then refer back to originals for decisive claims. A generated summary should help navigation and synthesis, not become an unquestioned substitute for the authoritative text.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cross-section dependencies need special handling. A limitation in one section may modify an obligation described elsewhere. If each section is summarized independently, neither map output may express the relationship. A cross-reference index, a second pass over unresolved references, or a structured obligation graph can help. The solution should state where relationships are recovered rather than assuming the reduce model will infer links that intermediate summaries omitted.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain what RAPTOR adds</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">RAPTOR constructs a retrieval structure by recursively embedding, clustering, and summarizing text chunks, producing representations at several levels of abstraction. At query time, retrieval can use original passages and higher-level summaries. Its published design includes both tree-traversal and collapsed-tree retrieval approaches. The central idea is to make thematic information searchable alongside local evidence, rather than relying only on short contiguous chunks. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.18059\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a document collection, a detailed question may benefit from a leaf passage, while a broad thematic question may benefit from a summary node. That does not mean every abstract question automatically retrieves the correct level or that the tree guarantees a complete answer. Summary content, embedding quality, retrieval policy, and context selection still matter. I would treat the published method as a candidate architecture and validate its value on the actual query distribution.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Understand RAPTOR&#x27;s operational costs</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Building and maintaining summary nodes adds work beyond ordinary chunk embedding. Each node needs lineage to contributing children, source revisions, permissions, and the summarization configuration. Updating one source can invalidate derived summaries. The maintenance cost depends on the clustering strategy and how broadly a change affects the structure. It is inaccurate to assume every update always requires rebuilding everything, but it is equally unsafe to assume every change is local.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Permission boundaries can be especially challenging. A summary that combines documents with different ACLs may disclose information from a restricted child. A conservative policy might require access to all contributing sources, which can reduce usefulness. Alternative designs build separate structures within stable authorization domains or generate user-scoped summaries at request time. This trade-off should be addressed before introducing cross-document summary nodes into a multi-tenant enterprise system.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare on-demand and precomputed work</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">On-demand hierarchical summarization pays processing cost when a user asks a broad question. Precomputed summaries pay upfront and can amortize across repeated queries. The economic comparison depends on corpus size, update rate, query volume, and the share of queries requiring global synthesis. A rarely queried document that changes daily may not justify an elaborate precomputed hierarchy. A stable manual queried thousands of times may benefit from reusable summaries.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would estimate the break-even using measured build and maintenance costs versus per-query savings at comparable quality. Include storage, invalidation, permission complexity, and operator time. Precomputation can improve online latency but create freshness risk. On-demand processing can use the latest source but take longer. Neither is inherently superior; the product&#x27;s freshness and interaction requirements determine which cost is more acceptable.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider long-context models as another tool</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If the full document fits with sufficient answer budget, direct long-context processing can avoid some intermediate summarization loss. It may be attractive for one-off analysis or documents whose cross-references are difficult to partition. However, reading more tokens increases cost and prefill work, and performance can vary with evidence position, distracting content, and task complexity. A large advertised context limit is not a guarantee of reliable exhaustive analysis. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.03172\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would compare direct long context with retrieval and hierarchical approaches on the same tasks. Include localized questions, global summaries, exact extraction, and cross-section reasoning. Check whether the model cites the right spans and preserves exceptions. Prefix caching can reduce repeated processing of a stable document where supported, but it does not remove the need to verify answer quality or to enforce current authorization for the cached content.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Route according to the acceptance criterion</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a local factual lookup, start with retrieval plus bounded parent and reference expansion. For a broad summary of one document, use a coverage-aware hierarchical plan or a validated long-context path. For repeated mixed local and thematic queries over stable material, evaluate a precomputed hierarchy such as RAPTOR. For exhaustive extraction, enumerate sections or rows and reconcile outputs against a manifest rather than trusting ordinary top-k retrieval.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The router should detect uncertainty about task scope. &quot;Review this agreement&quot; is underspecified compared with &quot;extract every renewal deadline.&quot; A clarification may improve the result substantially, but the system can still inspect document structure and estimate the workload first. If the product chooses a default review scope, state that scope. Silent routing to a narrow retrieval path can produce a polished answer that appears comprehensive without actually being so.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify coverage and source support</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For local questions, measure answer-bearing passage recall and whether expansion restores required conditions. For global summaries, create a section or topic coverage rubric and assess material omissions. For exhaustive tasks, compare extracted records with known source counts or a reviewed reference. Evaluate citation correctness, numerical fidelity, contradictions, and source-version consistency. Generic fluency scores cannot establish that a summary includes the important risk in an appendix.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test adversarial layouts: a critical exception near a chunk boundary, a footnote modifying a table, repeated section names, conflicting revisions, and a definition far from its use. These cases reveal whether the processing strategy preserves relationships. Human review should inspect the decisive source spans rather than only rate the final prose. A summary can read beautifully while omitting the single condition that changes the user&#x27;s decision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design caching and freshness for derived summaries</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cache summaries by source revision, processing configuration, task scope, and authorization compatibility. A summary of an older policy should not remain eligible after the underlying rule changes. Track parent-child dependencies so affected nodes can be invalidated or rebuilt. During rebuilding, the service may use original passages or a clearly defined alternative path rather than serving stale summaries without disclosure.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deletes must propagate through derived nodes as well as leaf vectors. Removing a document while retaining a summary that quotes its sensitive content does not complete deletion from the serving system. Rebuilding should publish consistent versions so the retriever does not combine a fresh leaf with an obsolete parent summary. These operational details often determine whether an elegant research architecture is suitable for a frequently changing enterprise corpus.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would explain that the context-window problem is really a scope, coverage, and information-preservation problem. Local retrieval avoids unnecessary processing. Hierarchical summarization provides broad coverage through intermediate representations. RAPTOR precomputes multiple abstraction levels for retrieval, trading build and maintenance complexity for potential benefits on repeated mixed-scope questions. Long-context processing is another option to benchmark, not an automatic replacement for all three.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The decision follows the question distribution and source lifecycle. I would begin with the simplest path that meets the task&#x27;s coverage requirement, preserve exact source lineage through every summary, and verify the important details against originals. This makes the system&#x27;s claims about completeness defensible and prevents an attractive summary hierarchy or large context limit from being mistaken for a guarantee that no relevant evidence was lost.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.18059\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">RAPTOR: retrieval across abstraction levels</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.03172\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Lost in the Middle: evaluating use of long contexts</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you balance recall and precision at scale, and what breaks as the corpus grows?","g":["multi-stage retrieval"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would balance recall and precision by separating broad candidate retrieval from selective evidence ranking and by diagnosing where relevant evidence is lost. As a corpus grows, more documents can compete for the same query, duplicates can crowd the results, and approximate indexes can face harder latency-recall trade-offs. Those are risks, not a theorem that precision must always decline. Corpus composition, embeddings, filters, and indexing determine whether growth actually harms the workload. I would measure each failure mechanism rather than treating document count as the explanation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define recall and precision in task terms</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Precision@k is the fraction of the top k results that are relevant under a defined label. If four of ten returned passages are relevant, Precision@10 is 0.4. Recall@k is the fraction of all labeled relevant passages recovered in those k results. If six relevant passages exist and four are returned, Recall@10 is about 0.667. These metrics depend on what &quot;relevant&quot; means and how completely the reference set is labeled.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For RAG, I care especially about answer-bearing evidence. A passage can discuss the topic without containing the required fact, date, condition, or exception. If the question needs two independent facts, returning one is insufficient even if every result is topically related. I would therefore track evidence sufficiency and multi-hop coverage alongside standard information-retrieval metrics. A high average similarity score is not a substitute for either precision or recall.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the trade-off with an example</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Imagine a question asking whether a Standard-plan customer can retain exports after cancellation. At ten thousand chunks, the correct policy may be among the nearest semantic matches. At ten million chunks, the corpus might contain many versions of cancellation policies for different products, regions, and contract types. These passages are all related, but only a subset applies. Increasing the candidate count can recover the right one while also admitting more near-misses.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">That is why one k value should not serve every stage. Candidate generation can return a relatively broad pool to protect evidence recall. A reranker and context selector then narrow it for precision and complementary coverage. If the final prompt receives every broad candidate, generation cost and distraction grow. If the initial pool is too narrow, later stages cannot recover the omitted evidence. The useful balance is achieved across stages rather than through one universal threshold.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Do not assume growth automatically causes degradation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Adding irrelevant documents that are far from the query may have little effect. Adding authoritative missing material can improve both recall and precision. Adding many near-duplicates or confusingly similar policies can make ranking harder. Growth can also coincide with changes in language, document quality, permissions, or query mix. I would compare controlled source snapshots so that observed degradation is not attributed to scale when another change caused it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The claim that precision worsens while recall stays constant also needs a metric definition. With a fixed k and an unchanged set of relevant passages, replacing relevant results with irrelevant ones reduces both retrieved-relevant count and recall. Some aggregate metrics can move differently across a changing corpus, but that does not justify a blanket statement. A careful senior answer names the measured quantities and explains the actual competition among candidates.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Establish an end-to-end baseline</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would preserve a representative query set and evaluate it at several corpus sizes or snapshots. Record authorized scope, source versions, retrieval parameters, candidate counts, reranker configuration, context budget, and generator version. Measure answer-bearing recall, ranking quality, evidence sufficiency, final correctness, latency, and cost. Keep the query distribution comparable or report changes explicitly so the experiment answers a clear question.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Inspect slices such as rare identifiers, conceptual questions, multilingual queries, old-versus-current policies, tables, restrictive permissions, and large tenants. A global average can remain stable while a valuable long-tail category collapses. Query-level differences are often more useful than the aggregate: which previously successful questions lost their decisive passage, and at which stage? That points directly to the appropriate intervention.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Diagnose parsing and chunking before tuning ANN</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">First establish that the answer exists in the authorized source and survived extraction. Inspect the actual indexed text, not just the original PDF or webpage. A table may lose its column headings, an exception may be split from the rule, or a scanned page may fail OCR. If no indexed chunk contains interpretable evidence, increasing ANN search effort cannot solve the problem. The representation pipeline must be repaired.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For suspected chunking failure, locate the exact answer span in the source and trace it to chunks. Check whether the heading, subject, number, qualifier, and reference remain together or can be restored through expansion. Compare retrieval with and without parent context on a controlled set. A strong diagnostic is that the correct document is found but its retrieved passages lack the answer relationship. Structure-aware chunking and reference expansion then address an observed failure rather than a guessed one.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate ANN misses from embedding misses</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run exact similarity search for sampled queries over the same eligible vectors and compare its top results with ANN. If the answer-bearing vector ranks highly in exact search but ANN omits it, index approximation is a likely contributor. Tune search breadth, candidate exploration, partition probes, or quantization settings according to the engine. Measure the resulting latency and memory cost rather than assuming a parameter increase is free.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If exact search also ranks the passage poorly, the issue is not merely ANN. The embedding model may miss negation, domain terminology, entity identity, or the query&#x27;s constraints. Chunk content may be too broad or incomplete. Lexical retrieval, better preprocessing, domain adaptation, or a different representation may help. Exact search establishes the best neighbors under the current similarity function; it does not establish the most relevant evidence under human judgment.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Narrow the corpus with reliable constraints</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Mandatory tenant and authorization filters come first. Additional metadata such as product, region, document type, effective date, and language can improve relevance when the query provides reliable constraints. Searching the applicable product&#x27;s current policies is often easier than distinguishing them from every historical policy after retrieval. Structured metadata can preserve distinctions that semantic embeddings blur.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">However, incorrect routing can destroy recall. If the user does not specify a region, silently choosing one may exclude the correct policy. I would distinguish certain constraints from inferred ones, evaluate router recall, and allow bounded multi-route search or clarification when ambiguity matters. Mandatory security filters are never relaxed. Relevance filters can be broadened in a controlled fallback when they were uncertain and evidence remains insufficient.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use hybrid retrieval when it adds complementary evidence</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Lexical search is useful for exact identifiers, acronyms, quoted clauses, and rare names. Dense retrieval handles paraphrases and conceptual similarity. Their combination can recover evidence missed by either alone. I would measure unique relevant contributions from each branch and fuse results with a calibrated or rank-based method. Raw lexical scores and cosine scores should not be added casually because their scales differ.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Hybrid search is not mandatory for every product. A small, well-structured semantic corpus may perform adequately with dense retrieval alone. An identifier-heavy catalog may benefit primarily from exact and lexical lookup. The decision follows the workload and operating cost. Adding a second retrieval engine without measurable complementary value increases complexity, latency exposure, and security-filter surface without necessarily improving answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Remove duplicates without discarding important differences</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exact and near-duplicate documents can occupy many top-k positions, reducing evidence diversity. Canonicalize known copies, group overlapping chunks, and limit redundant context. Preserve source families so repeated copies are not treated as independent corroboration. This is especially useful in large enterprise corpora with copied templates, mirrored documentation, and repeated email attachments. Reducing duplication can improve effective recall within a fixed context budget.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deduplication needs care with revisions and exceptions. Two policies can be nearly identical while differing in the one sentence that answers the question. A similarity threshold that merges them may erase a meaningful regional or temporal distinction. I would retain version metadata and compare changed spans. Deduplicate presentation or storage where safe, but preserve the ability to retrieve and cite the exact applicable source revision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Invest in reranking with realistic limits</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cross-encoder can compare the query and passage jointly and distinguish near-misses that a bi-encoder places close together. This becomes valuable when many candidates share broad terminology but differ in applicability. Rerank a bounded candidate pool, then evaluate whether the answer-bearing evidence rises into the final selection. Include headings and conditions so the reranker sees the distinctions it is expected to recognize.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The benefit is not guaranteed to grow monotonically with corpus size. A weak candidate generator may omit the right passage entirely, and a domain-mismatched reranker may prefer fluent but inapplicable text. At one thousand queries per second, scoring one hundred passages per query means one hundred thousand pair evaluations per second. Candidate budgets, batching, hardware, and model choice therefore need explicit cost-quality measurements.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Select complementary evidence under a token budget</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Final context should cover the question&#x27;s requirements rather than merely contain the first few individually relevant passages. A comparison may need one source for each product. A policy answer may need a general rule plus an exception and definition. Use deduplication, coverage-aware selection, and bounded parent expansion to assemble that set. Diversity methods can help, but excessive diversity can also replace decisive evidence with unrelated variety.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Token budget matters more directly than chunk count. Ten tiny passages and ten long sections impose different costs and truncation risks. Reserve room for the answer and instructions, and verify that the prompt builder does not silently drop the last crucial passage. Context selection should expose which requirements are supported and which remain unresolved. If evidence is insufficient, the generator should not compensate with a plausible guess.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calibrate thresholds and abstention carefully</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Absolute similarity thresholds often shift in usefulness when the embedding model, corpus, language, or query mix changes. A cosine value is not automatically a probability of relevance. Relative score gaps and rank-based features can be useful, but they are not universally stable either. A narrow gap may reflect many good passages or many equally bad ones. I would calibrate answerability decisions against labeled evidence sufficiency.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A useful abstention policy combines signals such as candidate coverage, reranker scores, source applicability, contradictions, and missing query conditions. Evaluate false abstentions as well as unsupported answers. Returning &quot;I do not know&quot; for every difficult request avoids some fabrication but fails the product. The threshold should reflect task consequence and available follow-up options, including clarification or a targeted authorized retrieval retry.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider representation improvements after diagnosing the gap</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If relevant passages consistently rank poorly under exact dense search, a better embedding model or domain adaptation may help. Training data should include hard negatives: passages that share terminology but differ in product, date, entity, or condition. Random unrelated negatives can make training look successful without teaching the distinctions that cause production failures. Keep training and evaluation queries separated to avoid inflated results.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Changing embeddings requires a compatible reindexing and migration plan. Compare query and document representations under the new model, retune thresholds, and inspect long-tail regressions. A larger model is not automatically better for every domain. Metadata extraction, chunk prefixes, or exact identifier handling may solve a specific failure more economically. Choose the intervention that addresses the observed ranking error rather than defaulting to the most expensive representation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Scale partitions without hiding routing losses</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Several scoped indexes can improve efficiency and relevance when boundaries reflect real query structure, such as tenant, product family, or source category. But partitioning introduces routing errors and cross-partition synthesis work. A question spanning two products must reach both relevant partitions. More shards can also increase scatter-gather overhead and tail latency. There is no general rule that several smaller indexes always outperform one large index.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would evaluate routing recall, per-partition load, skew, failure behavior, and the global merge strategy. Large tenants may require dedicated capacity while small tenants share infrastructure. Search parameters may adapt to authorized corpus size and filter selectivity. The architecture should preserve consistent security and evidence semantics across layouts so operational scaling does not change the meaning of a retrieved result or an answerability decision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make changes through controlled comparisons</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run ablations for candidate count, ANN effort, metadata filters, lexical retrieval, reranking, deduplication, and context budget. Hold other variables fixed where practical, then evaluate important interactions. Plot quality against latency and cost rather than optimizing a single metric. Review individual recovered and lost cases. A small average gain can conceal an unacceptable regression on a critical policy category.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that recall is protected early and precision is improved later, while evidence coverage is preserved throughout. Growth creates more opportunities for approximation misses, confusing near-neighbors, duplicates, and source-management problems, but the correct fix depends on measured failure. I would trace the answer from original source to indexed chunk, candidate pool, final context, and generated claim. That diagnosis supports a targeted redesign instead of blindly increasing k, adding GPUs, or replacing the model.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Faiss-indexes\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Faiss: index representations and memory accounting</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/1901.04085\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Passage Re-ranking with BERT</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Model the cost of a production RAG system and name the biggest levers.","g":["cost optimization"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would model production RAG cost from workload units rather than start with a claimed industry percentage. Separate the one-time or periodic cost of building knowledge from the recurring cost of serving requests, then calculate cost per successfully completed task. Generation may dominate a chat workload, while document processing, image extraction, vector storage, or reranking can dominate other deployments. The biggest lever is the component consuming the most avoidable resources under the actual quality and reliability requirements.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the accounting boundary</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The cost model should include source connectors, parsing and OCR, chunking, embeddings, optional enrichment, storage, indexing, retrieval, reranking, generation, caching, evaluation, network transfer, and operational labor. Managed-service charges and self-hosted infrastructure need different accounting. A provider invoice is an explicit variable expense; an idle GPU fleet or database reservation still costs money when request traffic falls. Keep committed, usage-based, and engineering costs distinguishable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would also distinguish initial backfill, steady-state updates, and exceptional rebuilds. Changing an embedding model may require processing the entire corpus again while both indexes remain online. A new parser can trigger another expensive pass. The monthly operating budget should therefore include an amortized rebuild allowance and a migration reserve, rather than treating the first successful ingestion as the last substantial offline expense.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Write a workload-driven formula</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Let N be daily requests, h the valid answer-cache hit fraction, I average generation input tokens, O average output tokens, and pI and pO prices per million tokens. Uncached generation cost is N times one minus h, multiplied by the sum of I times pI and O times pO, divided by one million. This is a baseline formula, not a complete bill: provider-specific caching, reasoning-token accounting, minimums, retries, and multiple model calls require additional terms.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For ingestion, multiply newly processed input tokens by the embedding unit price, then add parsing, indexing, and enrichment work. For self-hosted stages, multiply measured provisioned worker-hours by their effective hourly cost. Include utilization and availability headroom. Dividing a GPU&#x27;s hourly price by its best isolated benchmark throughput understates real cost if production needs spare capacity, short batching waits, and fault tolerance.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Work through a hypothetical daily bill</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Assume one million daily requests, a thirty percent valid answer-cache hit rate, three thousand generation input tokens, and three hundred output tokens per miss. Use purely illustrative prices of one dollar per million input tokens and four dollars per million output tokens. Seven hundred thousand misses consume 2.1 billion input tokens and 210 million output tokens. Generation costs 2,100 plus 840 dollars, or 2,940 dollars per day under these assumptions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Now add retrieval workers, reranking capacity, storage, monitoring, and ingestion separately. If those total five hundred dollars per day, the modeled daily expense is 3,440 dollars. That number is not a quote for any product or provider. It is a transparent scenario that can be recomputed when traffic, token length, cache eligibility, or actual contracted rates change. Sensitivity analysis is more useful than presenting its result as a universal production benchmark.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Size storage and serving compute independently</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate the searchable corpus</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Ten million documents multiplied by eight chunks gives eighty million chunks. With a 1,024-dimensional embedding stored as float32, each vector occupies 4,096 bytes. The raw vector payload is therefore 327,680,000,000 bytes: approximately 327.68 decimal gigabytes or 305.18 gibibytes. This calculation excludes document text, metadata, identifiers, index structures, replicas, and temporary allocations. Mixing decimal and binary units would make the capacity estimate harder to audit.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an HNSW-style index, graph memory depends on implementation, connectivity, identifier representation, levels, and allocator overhead. A simplified example with thirty-two neighbors and eight bytes per stored link adds about 20.48 gigabytes across eighty million vectors, before other overheads. That is a planning illustration, not a portable sizing formula. I would measure actual bytes per indexed vector using representative data and the selected engine, including its deletion and compaction behavior. <a href=\"https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Faiss-indexes\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Text storage can be larger than the vectors. At four hundred tokens per chunk, the corpus contains roughly thirty-two billion chunk tokens, including any overlap. Assuming four UTF-8 bytes per token only as a rough English-text estimate gives about 128 gigabytes of raw chunk text. Tables, multilingual content, compression, duplicate storage, and source binaries change that estimate. Original documents belong in durable object storage; searchable text and metadata need a separate budget.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose compression and sharding deliberately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Storing vector components with eight bits instead of thirty-two reduces the component payload to approximately 81.92 gigabytes. It does not reduce the entire service footprint by four times. Quantization metadata, graph links, original vectors retained for rescoring, text, and replicas still consume space. I would evaluate retrieval recall against a float32 baseline, especially for rare identifiers, multilingual queries, and near-duplicate passages, before enabling compression broadly.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reducing dimensions can also help, but arbitrary truncation is unsafe. A model trained to support meaningful lower-dimensional prefixes may allow a supported truncation strategy; an ordinary embedding model does not automatically have that property. I would benchmark the actual shortened representation. Product quantization or a disk-oriented index might be attractive when memory cost dominates, but their latency and operational trade-offs must be measured under the required filters and update workload. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.13147\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An initial layout could use eight primary shards with replicas distributed across failure zones. This is a starting hypothesis rather than a final machine count. Sharding spreads data; replication provides additional serving copies and availability. If each request searches all eight primary partitions, one thousand external queries per second create eight thousand shard searches per second before retries. Replicas can distribute that work, but scatter-gather latency still depends on slow shards and coordination overhead.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Quantify reranking demand</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">At one thousand uncached requests per second and one hundred candidates per request, the reranking service receives one hundred thousand query-passage pairs per second. If a measured worker processes two thousand representative pairs per second, the arithmetic minimum is fifty workers. Provisioning at seventy percent utilization would require about seventy-two workers before additional failure capacity. These numbers illustrate the calculation; actual throughput depends strongly on model size, sequence length, hardware, batching, and service overhead.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Candidate reduction can materially change this budget. Reranking thirty candidates creates thirty thousand pairs per second. Selective reranking may help if an evaluated routing rule identifies easy cases safely. A distilled model, shorter inputs, and dynamic batching are additional options. I would compare their end-to-end answer quality at the same cost target. A cheaper reranker that removes the decisive exception paragraph can increase total product cost through wrong answers and escalations.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Quantify generation demand</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose each uncached request sends three thousand input tokens and produces three hundred output tokens. At one thousand requests per second, generation requires three million input tokens per second and three hundred thousand output tokens per second. This is usually a substantial capacity commitment. A managed model endpoint still has quotas, regional constraints, and overload behavior; using a provider does not remove capacity planning.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Little&#x27;s law connects arrival rate and concurrency. If an average request occupies the generation service for eight seconds, one thousand arrivals per second imply roughly eight thousand concurrent active requests in steady state. The serving fleet needs enough memory for weights, key-value caches, batching, and operational headroom. Prefix caching can reduce repeated prompt processing, but answer tokens still need generation. Input-heavy and output-heavy workloads therefore need separate benchmarks.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cost can be expressed without inventing current prices: daily generation cost equals daily uncached requests multiplied by the sum of input-token count times input-token unit price and output-token count times output-token unit price. Add embedding, retrieval, reranking, storage, networking, and operations. At a sustained one thousand requests per second, the service handles 86.4 million daily requests. A small per-request inefficiency becomes financially significant at that volume.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Rank levers by marginal savings</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">In the hypothetical bill, increasing valid answer-cache hits from thirty to forty percent removes one hundred thousand generation calls. At the stated token sizes and prices, that saves 420 dollars per day in generation before accounting for additional cache infrastructure and lookup work. It does not reduce every fixed infrastructure charge by ten percent. A hit only saves the downstream stages actually bypassed, and some entries require authorization and freshness revalidation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reducing output from three hundred to two hundred tokens saves 280 dollars per day across seven hundred thousand misses at the assumed output rate. Reducing input by one thousand tokens saves 700 dollars. These savings are workload-specific. A shorter answer that omits an important qualification may create expensive follow-ups or incorrect decisions. I would compare task success and total conversation cost, not just the first response&#x27;s token count.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use model routing with an explicit escalation model</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A smaller generator can handle easy lookups while a stronger one handles complex synthesis. Calculate the combined cost of routing, first attempts, escalations, and repeated context processing. If half of requests escalate after a full unsuccessful small-model answer, the savings may be much lower than a simple small-versus-large price ratio suggests. A cheap classifier also makes mistakes, so its error costs belong in the evaluation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The routing signal should reflect evidence coverage and task difficulty rather than uncalibrated model confidence alone. Compare small-only, large-only, and routed pipelines on the same cases. Include rare identifiers, multi-source questions, and appropriate abstention. A routing policy is valuable when it lowers cost at an acceptable completion rate; a headline percentage from another company&#x27;s workload does not establish that result for yours.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Distinguish answer, retrieval, and prefix caching</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Answer caching can bypass generation when its entry remains applicable and authorized. Retrieval caching avoids search and perhaps reranking while still paying generation. Prefix caching reuses eligible prompt processing and may reduce input cost or latency under the selected engine&#x27;s rules. These mechanisms have different hit definitions and savings. Reporting one blended cache hit rate obscures whether the most expensive work was actually avoided.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For example, if one thousand of the three thousand input tokens receive a hypothetical fifty percent prefix discount on every miss, the daily input saving is 350 dollars in the worked scenario. Actual providers may use different rates, minimum lengths, retention windows, and eligibility rules. Keep those settings as parameters and verify billed usage. Prefix caching does not avoid generating new output tokens, and private prefixes still need appropriate isolation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Optimize reranking without hiding its quality loss</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate the economics explicitly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Let the incoming uncached query rate be Q and the number of reranked candidates be C. Pair-scoring demand is Q multiplied by C. At five hundred queries per second and eighty candidates, the service needs forty thousand pair evaluations per second. If a benchmarked worker sustains sixteen hundred representative pairs per second, twenty-five fully utilized workers are the arithmetic minimum. Real provisioning needs headroom for burstiness, failures, and latency-sensitive batching.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reducing the candidate pool from eighty to forty halves pair-scoring demand, but it may remove useful evidence. I would plot quality against cost across several candidate counts rather than choosing the smallest count that appears fast. Throughput measurements must use realistic passage lengths and concurrency. A benchmark using short synthetic sentences can substantially overstate the capacity available for enterprise documents with long tables and section prefixes.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Batching introduces another trade-off. Larger batches improve accelerator utilization but may wait longer to accumulate requests. A deadline-aware batcher should cap waiting time and avoid allowing a long passage to delay many short requests unnecessarily. Autoscaling should observe queueing delay and tokens or pairs processed, not only request counts. Two queries with different candidate lengths can impose very different computational work.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Candidate count, passage length, model size, batching, and hardware utilization all affect reranking expense. I would test a cost-quality frontier rather than distill a reranker simply because smaller sounds cheaper. A distilled model may preserve average relevance while regressing on the exceptions that matter most. Selective reranking can help, but include the router&#x27;s cost and false easy classifications. The useful metric is saved expense per equivalent reliable answer.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Account for offline enrichment and churn</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Generated document summaries or contextual prefixes can create substantial ingestion expenditure because they process far more source tokens than the online query stream. Measure how often the enriched content changes and how many downstream nodes need rebuilding. A daily-changing corpus may erase the advantage of expensive precomputation. Reuse unchanged content by revision-aware hashes where valid, while preserving lifecycle and permission updates even when text is unchanged.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Asynchronous processing may offer operational or price advantages, but use the current service&#x27;s actual terms rather than assuming a fixed discount. Some backfills tolerate hours of latency; urgent policy updates do not. Reserve capacity for freshness-critical ingestion and model the extra cost of that priority. A cheap overnight rebuild is not useful if the product promises newly published security guidance within minutes.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Include quality review and hidden multipliers</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Model-based evaluation, safety checks, repair attempts, query rewriting, and agentic retries can multiply model usage. A pipeline diagram showing one final generator may conceal several preceding calls. Track attempted calls, successful calls, cancellations, and tokens billed after client disconnects. Also measure source expansion and conversation growth, because long sessions can become much more expensive than isolated questions used in a prototype benchmark.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Operational complexity has a cost too. Adding a second database to save a small amount of compute may increase on-call burden and recovery work. Dedicated tenant deployments can be necessary but create minimum fixed expense. Allocate shared infrastructure transparently and report marginal serving cost separately from fully loaded product cost. Both are useful, but they answer different budgeting and pricing questions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Present the decision with guardrails</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would build a parameterized model, identify the largest measured avoidable expense, propose a bounded change, and evaluate it against task correctness, citation support, latency, freshness, and authorization. Compare cost per successful task before and after. Investigate whether apparent savings come from serving fewer users, refusing more questions, or silently dropping evidence. Those outcomes should not be confused with efficiency improvements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that there is no universal ordering of cost levers. Safe cache reuse, smaller evidence sets, model routing, efficient reranking, incremental ingestion, and appropriate storage representations are strong candidates. Their value follows the workload arithmetic and measured quality. The senior contribution is making the assumptions visible, quantifying sensitivity, and showing that the proposed savings survive realistic traffic, failure capacity, and the complete user workflow.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Faiss-indexes\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Faiss: index representations and memory accounting</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.13147\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Matryoshka Representation Learning</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Design the caching strategy for a RAG system. What are the correctness risks?","g":["caching"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would design RAG caching around a validity contract: a cached result may be served only when its inputs, authorization scope, source dependencies, and freshness requirements still match the current request. Different cache layers avoid different work and carry different correctness risks. I would start with deterministic, easily validated reuse, then introduce semantic answer reuse only where measured benefit justifies false-hit risk. A high hit rate is not a success if the cache returns another user&#x27;s data or an outdated policy.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Begin with a realistic failure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Two employees ask, &quot;What is the forecast for next quarter?&quot; A manager can access a restricted finance document; another employee cannot. If the answer cache is keyed only by normalized question and organization, the manager&#x27;s response can leak through the second employee&#x27;s request. Even a user-specific key can become invalid when the manager later loses access. The cache must account for effective authority over the evidence at serving time.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarly, &quot;refund policy for monthly plans&quot; and &quot;refund policy for annual plans&quot; can have very similar embeddings but different correct answers. &quot;Can we retain data?&quot; and &quot;Can we not retain data?&quot; differ by a small textual change with important meaning. These cases show why semantic similarity is a candidate-matching signal, not proof that one answer satisfies another request. Cache design is therefore part of the answer-correctness architecture.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Cache document embeddings by representation contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For document embeddings, use a hash of the actual model input together with the embedding model revision, preprocessing version, supported dimensions, and normalization settings. Hashing the original file alone may be insufficient if the parser or chunking changes what is sent to the model. Conversely, identical chunk text in two source documents can reuse the numerical embedding while retaining distinct source identities and permissions in the index.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An embedding-cache hit should not suppress required metadata updates. Text can remain unchanged while its ACL, publication status, effective date, or tenant association changes. The ingestion pipeline must still process those events. Query embeddings need a compatible key as well, including query instructions or task type when the model uses them. A vector of the right dimension is not automatically valid for every model or retrieval configuration.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Cache retrieval results with evidence dependencies</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A retrieval cache can store ordered chunk identifiers, scores, and the retrieval configuration that produced them. It avoids candidate search and possibly reranking while allowing a fresh answer to be generated. Store identifiers and source revisions rather than treating an old text snapshot as implicitly current. Recheck whether selected resources still exist and remain authorized before materializing evidence. The final prompt budget and expansion policy can also affect compatibility.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">This cache still has semantic correctness risks. A newly published document may become the best answer even though none of the old result dependencies changed. Invalidating only when a previously selected source changes misses that case. Options include an index-generation key, a source-partition epoch, targeted query invalidation for known changes, or a bounded freshness window. The choice trades cache reuse against how quickly newly relevant evidence must influence answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define an exact-answer cache key carefully</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An exact cache key should cover the effective question, relevant conversation state, active tenant, authorization fingerprint or equivalent validity check, locale, personalization, source snapshot or dependency policy, model configuration, and prompt-template version. Not every system needs every field, but omissions must be justified by the task contract. An apparently identical question can refer to a different product established in the preceding turn.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Normalization should be conservative. Whitespace normalization may be safe, while removing punctuation can change identifiers or code. Lowercasing may matter for case-sensitive values. Removing dates or numbers can collapse distinct questions. I would document which transformations preserve meaning for each request class and test counterexamples. The cache&#x27;s equality rule should follow application semantics rather than an aggressive text-cleaning routine intended only to improve hit rate.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Bind reuse to effective permissions</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Scope caches by effective authorization</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cache keyed only by question and tenant is unsafe when users within that tenant have different permissions. An authorized manager could ask about a forecast, populate the cache, and cause a regular employee to receive the same answer. The cache must bind its entry to an equivalent effective access scope or revalidate every underlying evidence resource for the new caller. A user identifier alone is also insufficient if that user&#x27;s permissions later change.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An authorization fingerprint may include the active tenant, effective groups, policy revision, and other relevant attributes. This improves reuse among genuinely equivalent permission contexts, but the fingerprint must cover the actual policy semantics. Document-dependent cache entries should retain resource and revision dependencies. Revoking one source can then invalidate or disqualify affected answers. Semantic cache matching adds another risk because a similar question may request a protected detail absent from the original question.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A permission fingerprint must represent the policy dimensions that actually influence source access. Large group lists, explicit denies, attribute-based restrictions, and temporary shares may not fit a simplistic role string. An alternative is to retain every evidence dependency and authorize those resources for the new caller before serving. This can allow safe reuse across users without assuming that all members of a group have identical access in every context.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Treat semantic answer caching as a verified lookup</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The semantic cache first retrieves candidate cached questions, then applies compatibility checks. Match structured entities, dates, product editions, jurisdiction, requested operation, and answer scope where relevant. A lightweight equivalence model may help but needs calibration and cannot replace deterministic authorization. High-consequence or time-sensitive queries may be ineligible. There is no universal cosine threshold, including 0.95, that establishes semantic equivalence across embedding models and corpora.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Do not use a blanket rule that every question containing a number must bypass semantic caching. Some workloads have stable, safe templates with explicit parameters, where a structured key is better than free-text matching. Equally, a question without any digits can still differ critically by plan name or negation. Define eligibility by meaning and consequence, then measure false hits using intentionally similar questions with different correct answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Version caches during configuration changes</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle caches according to their dependencies</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Query embeddings must be cached with the embedding contract in the key. Retrieval caches need the index or corpus revision and authorization scope. Answer caches need a validity rule covering the evidence and serving configuration that produced the answer. Namespace changes are often safer than attempting to discover and delete every incompatible entry. Old entries can expire naturally if they are no longer eligible for serving.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Not every answer generated under A must be deleted immediately in every system. An answer grounded in unchanged, still-authorized sources may remain valid if the application&#x27;s cache contract permits it. But it must not be reused accidentally through an unversioned key. The important requirement is explicit compatibility and freshness, not blanket invalidation as a substitute for understanding dependencies. During comparison, cache behavior should be controlled so it does not hide migration regressions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Configuration namespaces simplify compatibility because a new retrieval or prompt contract can stop reading old entries without synchronously deleting them all. Track storage growth and expiry for abandoned namespaces. When several configurations coexist during a canary, record which one created and served each entry. Otherwise cached incumbent answers can contaminate treatment measurements and make a new model appear better or more consistent than it actually is.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle derived summaries and source changes</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design caching and freshness for derived summaries</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cache summaries by source revision, processing configuration, task scope, and authorization compatibility. A summary of an older policy should not remain eligible after the underlying rule changes. Track parent-child dependencies so affected nodes can be invalidated or rebuilt. During rebuilding, the service may use original passages or a clearly defined alternative path rather than serving stale summaries without disclosure.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deletes must propagate through derived nodes as well as leaf vectors. Removing a document while retaining a summary that quotes its sensitive content does not complete deletion from the serving system. Rebuilding should publish consistent versions so the retriever does not combine a fresh leaf with an obsolete parent summary. These operational details often determine whether an elegant research architecture is suitable for a frequently changing enterprise corpus.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Content dependency tracking needs both change invalidation and discovery freshness. An answer based on three unchanged policies may become obsolete when a fourth policy is introduced. For tightly scoped sources, a collection revision can provide a simple conservative validity boundary. For very large corpora, selective invalidation may offer better economics, but its completeness should be tested. TTL remains a useful backstop; it is not a full freshness guarantee.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use prefix caching within its actual contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Prompt or key-value caching reuses eligible model computation for an identical prefix. It generally aims to preserve the semantics of that exact input rather than substitute a different question&#x27;s answer. Still, prefix identity, model version, position handling, authorization isolation, retention, and engine-specific behavior matter. It is not correct to call private prefix reuse automatically free of correctness or confidentiality concerns.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Place stable instructions or approved shared context where the selected implementation can reuse them without distorting the prompt&#x27;s meaning. Do not reorder evidence solely to obtain a cache hit if the change harms answer quality. A new document revision or revoked permission can invalidate an otherwise computationally reusable prefix. The serving system must decide whether the caller is allowed to use the stored context, regardless of whether the model backend can technically reuse it.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prevent cache stampedes and inconsistent population</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a popular entry expires, hundreds of simultaneous misses can generate the same expensive answer. A single-flight mechanism lets one worker compute while others wait within a deadline or use an approved alternative. Use bounded waiting and distributed ownership where necessary. The cache-population operation should record the complete dependency set and configuration atomically so another reader cannot observe a half-built answer.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Stale-while-revalidate is useful only for request classes that tolerate bounded staleness. It should not serve a revoked private answer or a deleted source merely to reduce latency. Negative caching also needs care: caching &quot;no answer&quot; can conceal newly ingested evidence. Use shorter or dependency-aware validity for such entries and distinguish a genuine evidence gap from a temporary retrieval failure that should not be memorized as a fact.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect the cache as a sensitive data store</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Answers and retrieved passages can contain information from multiple sources, making the cache highly sensitive. Apply access controls, encryption, regional placement, retention, and deletion workflows consistent with the data it contains. Keep administrative inspection restricted.
