A public or cross-tenant cache service can undo otherwise correct source permissions if entries are accessible through predictable keys or a debugging endpoint.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cache poisoning can arise when low-trust or malformed requests populate entries later used by others. Control who can create shared entries, validate successful generation before caching, and distinguish transient error responses from reusable answers. Limit entry size and request cardinality to prevent resource exhaustion. The cache should not become an uncontrolled alternate knowledge base with weaker publication and lifecycle rules than the main corpus.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Measure valid hits and hidden failures</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Record the cache layer, lookup outcome, entry age, configuration, dependency validation result, and downstream work avoided. Sample hits and recompute fresh answers under the same source state to detect semantic false hits and stale evidence. Compare supported-claim rates and task completion between eligible cached and uncached paths while accounting for query difficulty. Cache hits often come from easier repeated questions, so an unadjusted quality comparison can be misleading.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Measure tail latency during cold starts and invalidation storms. A high warm-cache hit rate does not prove the service can survive a large source update. Capacity planning should include a cold-cache scenario or a controlled admission strategy. Also track cache cost, invalidation traffic, and verification overhead. A sophisticated semantic cache can cost more to maintain and validate than the work it saves on a low-repeat workload.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the correctness hierarchy</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would introduce document-embedding reuse first, then carefully scoped retrieval and exact-answer caches, with prefix reuse following the engine&#x27;s guarantees. Semantic answer caching comes later because it substitutes one request&#x27;s response for another&#x27;s based on an imperfect equivalence judgment. Each layer needs its own eligibility, key, invalidation, and measurement policy. A single generic cache decorator is usually too weak for all these contracts.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that caching in RAG is safe reuse under changing evidence and permissions. The core risks are unauthorized disclosure, semantic false hits, stale answers, missing conversational context, and incompatible model or index state. I would make those conditions explicit, validate them before serving, and report valid avoided work rather than raw hit rate. That preserves caching&#x27;s economic value without turning it into a hidden source of confident errors.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://redis.io/docs/latest/develop/use-cases/semantic-cache/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Redis: semantic caching concepts</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"What observability do you need for a RAG system, and what would you alert on?","g":["rag observability"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would make a RAG request explainable from source ingestion to the final answer, while protecting the sensitive content carried through that path. Observability needs traces for individual diagnosis, metrics for population behavior, structured events for state changes, and sampled evaluation for answer quality. Alerts should identify a user-impacting condition with an owner and response action. Logging every prompt without a plan is expensive, risky, and often less useful than preserving the right versioned identifiers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Begin with the questions operators must answer</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a user reports a wrong answer, the team should determine whether the source contained the answer, whether it was indexed, whether retrieval found it, whether selection discarded it, and whether generation misused it. When the service slows down, operators should distinguish queueing, search, reranking, provider prefill, and output generation. When cost rises, they should distinguish longer inputs, more retries, changed cache eligibility, and traffic growth.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">These diagnostic questions define the instrumentation. A request identifier alone is insufficient if the selected source revisions are missing. A total latency metric is insufficient if no stage timings exist. A successful ingestion-job counter is insufficient if the connector repeatedly scans an old snapshot. I would design the telemetry schema alongside the pipeline contracts, so it records the state transitions needed to test those contracts in production.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Trace the online critical path</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Create a root span at request admission and child spans for authorization, routing, rewriting, query embedding, each retrieval branch, fusion, reranking, evidence assembly, generation, and validation. Propagate trace context across queues and remote services. Record retries as distinct attempts associated with a logical operation. That makes it possible to see when three attempts at different layers turned one request into a burst of downstream calls.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The trace should distinguish parallel work from sequential dependencies. Dense and lexical searches can overlap, while generation waits for the selected context. Record queue wait separately from execution time, and distinguish first-token latency from completion duration. Attach bounded metadata such as configuration identifiers, candidate counts, input lengths, cache status, timeout outcomes, and partial-result status. Avoid placing high-cardinality chunk identifiers in metric labels; keep them in protected traces or events.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve evidence lineage for answer diagnosis</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve the actual evidence used</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Verification needs the final context supplied to the generator, including source identifiers, revisions, and any compression. Checking against the entire corpus can hide a generation failure by finding support the model never saw. Checking against an earlier candidate pool can also misdiagnose the result if prompt assembly discarded the relevant passage. I would retain an authorized trace of the selected evidence or a retrievable reference to its exact revision.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The trace must be privacy-conscious. Storing every prompt indefinitely creates another sensitive data store. Resource identifiers, content hashes, configuration versions, and protected short-lived evidence capture may be sufficient for many investigations. Whatever is retained should support the actual verification contract. If historical source text is unavailable, the detector should not pretend it has reconstructed exactly what the generator received.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A source identifier should resolve to the applicable revision, parser version, chunk boundaries, and lifecycle state. Record candidate identifiers and stage transitions where permitted so the team can identify where evidence was lost. Scores help interpret ranking but are not comparable across arbitrary models. Keep the score type and model configuration with the value. A bare number such as 0.83 is not a self-explanatory relevance judgment.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Monitor operational and quality dimensions separately</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Monitor production quality and operations separately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Operational metrics include request success, retrieval and reranking latency, time to first token, completion latency, queue depth, token demand, cache validity, source freshness, and cost per request. Quality monitoring includes sampled correctness, supported-claim rate, citation problems, missing-evidence cases, and abstention behavior. Separate these dimensions so a fast but wrong system does not appear healthy and a cautious correct system is not penalized without context.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Slice monitoring by relevant conditions. A global average can hide a regression in one language or one connector. Changes in query mix can also move aggregate scores without any model degradation. Track source ingestion failures and permission lag alongside answer quality because corpus problems often masquerade as model problems. Alert thresholds should correspond to a response plan, such as pausing a rollout, disabling a source, or increasing targeted review.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Do not label a nonempty retrieval response as a successful answer. Every query can return top-k neighbors even when none contains the needed fact. Conversely, an appropriate abstention is not necessarily an error. Define separate counters for empty eligible retrieval, insufficient evidence, source conflict, policy refusal, generation failure, and user cancellation. These categories make alerts actionable and prevent teams from &quot;improving&quot; dashboards by suppressing honest uncertainty.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Observe the offline knowledge pipeline</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For each connector, measure source-event age, scan checkpoint age, parsed and published revision lag, successful document counts, failures by format, deletion propagation, and permission-change latency. Queue depth is useful but incomplete: ten enormous documents can represent more work than ten thousand short notes. Track tokens or estimated processing work as well as item counts. A queue can remain shallow while one poison document is repeatedly retried forever.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reconciliation should report inventory mismatches and their age. A job that runs successfully but compares only a partial source inventory may give false reassurance. Keep per-source watermarks and completeness indicators. Alert on a connector that stops observing changes when changes are expected, not only on explicit exceptions. Expired credentials and upstream API changes often produce stale but apparently healthy answers unless source freshness is independently checked.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Watch exposure changes that may indicate content problems</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Monitor retrieval exposure, not just ingestion volume</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A small poisoned addition may have a large effect if it appears in many high-value queries. I would track per-source and per-chunk retrieval frequency, rank distribution, selection into final context, and citation frequency. Compare exposure changes with traffic changes and publication events. A sudden rise after an edit is a useful investigation signal, especially when one source begins displacing several previously useful authoritative passages.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exposure should be sliced by tenant, query category, and source family. Global counts can hide a poison targeting one product or region. Conversely, a popular new release note may legitimately become common across many queries. Alerts should identify which answers changed and what evidence was replaced. Merely reporting that a vector became &quot;central&quot; does not tell an operator whether users are receiving a false warranty rule or a useful updated overview.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exposure alerts should include the changed source revision, affected query slices, and whether answers actually cited or adopted the content. A candidate appearing frequently is less consequential than a false policy becoming the dominant answer. Rank distributions and source-family relationships help distinguish legitimate popularity from duplicate amplification. The operator needs enough evidence to quarantine a bad revision without disabling an entire useful connector unnecessarily.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build alerts around service objectives</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For availability and latency, alert on sustained or fast error-budget consumption rather than isolated slow requests. Define the eligible request population and the good-event criterion first. A sample policy might page for a rapid increase in user-visible failures and create a ticket for slower degradation. The exact thresholds depend on traffic and objectives. A low-volume service needs different statistical treatment from a high-volume public endpoint.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Quality alerts need enough sampled evidence to avoid reacting to noise. Report sample counts, uncertainty, and slice changes with any faithfulness or correctness estimate. A sudden increase in no-answer behavior can indicate missing ingestion, a distribution change, or a stricter correct abstention policy. Treat the metric as a symptom requiring diagnosis. Do not attach one universal root cause to a rate change without inspecting source state and request traces.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make every alert actionable</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An alert should identify the affected service or tenant cohort, severity, start time, relevant deployment or source changes, owner, and first diagnostic steps. Include links to a latency breakdown, source-lag view, and representative protected traces. Avoid exposing private content in notification text. Alerts for cost drift should distinguish a sudden runaway loop from an expected product launch that legitimately increased completed tasks.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Track alert precision and response usefulness. If every brief provider slowdown pages the team while no one responds differently, tune the rule or handling policy. If permission-revocation lag is severe but only appears in a dashboard, add a defined response. Alerting is an operational decision system, not a collection of thresholds copied from another stack. Its success is measured by earlier detection and effective mitigation of user-impacting failures.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect traces and administrative inspection</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect logs and operational access</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Application logs should avoid raw retrieved text by default. Useful audit fields include request identifier, tenant, pseudonymous principal reference, selected resource identifiers, policy decision version, serving configuration, and outcome. These logs still contain sensitive metadata and need access restrictions and retention rules. Debug tooling should require explicit authorized access to content, with auditability, rather than granting every developer a searchable copy of all prompts.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Support impersonation and break-glass access require separate, tightly controlled workflows. An internal administrator role should not silently bypass customer policies through ordinary retrieval. Encryption at rest and in transit protects data against some threats but does not fix an incorrect authorization decision. Similarly, a private model deployment does not prevent one employee from receiving another employee&#x27;s documents if the application constructs the wrong search scope.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design observability to minimize content exposure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Operational debugging usually needs timing, identifiers, configuration versions, and failure categories more often than raw document text. I would log these fields by default and keep content capture exceptional, access-controlled, sampled, and time-limited. Prompt and completion logs can be particularly sensitive because they combine multiple source documents with user intent. A trace collector may become the most concentrated sensitive dataset in the entire system.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Where content is necessary for quality review, use an approved review environment with scoped access and redaction where useful. Separate routine service metrics from protected evidence inspection. Developers should not automatically receive access to every tenant&#x27;s documents because they maintain the application. Support access, data exports, and debugging sessions should be auditable. Test failure paths to ensure secrets and private text do not appear in stack traces or alert messages.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make auditability proportionate</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">To investigate an answer, retain a request reference, serving configuration, source revision identifiers, permission-decision context, and selected citation mappings where permitted. This can establish which evidence and rules were used without storing every plaintext prompt forever. Exact replay may require access to historical source versions, which has its own retention implications. The audit requirement should state whether exact reproduction or a sufficient decision record is needed.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Protect audit records against unauthorized alteration and access. A tamper-evident design supports accountability, but immutability does not justify unlimited sensitive content retention. Audit who accessed protected traces and why. Report deletion and retention exceptions through approved processes. The engineering goal is evidence that the system follows its declared controls, not a decorative dashboard that cannot reconstruct a permission failure or locate an affected source revision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Control telemetry volume and cardinality</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">At scale, tracing every full payload can become a major cost. Preserve low-cost operational metrics broadly, then use deliberate sampling for detailed traces and content review. Tail-based sampling can retain failures and slow requests, but it should be complemented by a representative baseline sample for population estimates. Otherwise quality review sees only unusual failures and cannot estimate ordinary behavior accurately.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Bound attributes and avoid unbounded user-generated labels. Tenant-level metrics may be useful, but millions of unique query strings or document IDs can overwhelm metric storage. Distinguish deployment version, route, model contract, source type, and error class using controlled vocabularies. Store detailed identifiers where access and retention are appropriate. Verify telemetry loss or exporter backpressure does not block serving or silently drop the only evidence needed during an incident.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Join feedback to the correct serving event</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">User ratings, citation feedback, clarifications, and escalations should reference the actual response and trace, including cache hits and model version. A thumbs-down on a follow-up should not be attributed automatically to the first query&#x27;s retrieval. Preserve conversation and response identities while limiting personal data. A review interface can then show the relevant source spans and stage outputs rather than asking an analyst to reproduce a changing corpus from memory.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Implicit feedback remains ambiguous. A citation click may indicate trust, confusion, or verification. An escalation may be appropriate for a high-consequence question. Observability should capture the event accurately and let evaluation interpret it under a rubric. Treating every click as a positive relevance label can turn monitoring into a biased training pipeline and make the ranking system reinforce its own presentation choices.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify the observability system itself</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Inject controlled failures: pause a connector, delay a reranker, remove a source revision, and simulate an authorization-service timeout. Check that telemetry records the expected state and that the right owner receives an actionable alert. Use synthetic sensitive fields to confirm redaction and access controls. A dashboard that has never detected a known test failure provides little evidence that it will detect the next real one.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that RAG observability must connect knowledge freshness, retrieval decisions, model behavior, and user outcomes. I would preserve enough versioned lineage to explain individual answers, aggregate low-cardinality operational signals, and sample quality review responsibly. The best alert is one that reveals a violated product contract early and tells an operator what to inspect or change, while the telemetry itself respects the same data boundaries as the service.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://opentelemetry.io/docs/concepts/signals/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OpenTelemetry: signals and observability</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you design an ingestion pipeline that is idempotent, incremental and recoverable?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would build ingestion as a durable, versioned transformation from an authoritative source into a derived serving index. Idempotent means repeating the same logical work does not create a different effective result. Incremental means processing only changes that matter while still handling metadata and lifecycle updates. Recoverable means a crash, lost notification, or partial downstream failure can be reconciled without guessing which source revision users are seeing. Deterministic identifiers help, but they are only one part of those guarantees.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define a document publication state machine</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each source document has an identity, revision, content hash, permission version, and lifecycle status. Track processing states such as discovered, fetched, parsed, embedded, staged, published, failed, and deleted. The serving layer should know which complete revision is active. Publishing a few new chunks while leaving some old chunks active can produce contradictory answers even when every individual database upsert succeeds.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A practical design stages all chunks for a new revision, validates completeness, then changes an active-revision pointer or equivalent visibility marker. The old revision becomes ineligible and can be physically cleaned later. This separates logical publication from bulk writes. The database may offer another suitable atomicity mechanism, but the contract should be explicit: users see a valid revision boundary rather than an accidental mixture determined by worker timing.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build the transformation with durable boundaries</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a reliable ingestion pipeline</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Connectors should emit document create, update, delete, and permission-change events into a durable queue. Each event carries a tenant identifier, source document identifier, source revision, event identifier, and relevant provenance. Workers fetch the authoritative source, parse it, preserve headings and tables, create chunks, embed them, and publish searchable records. A document is not considered fully indexed merely because one embedding call succeeded; the publication state must identify which complete revision is available.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Chunk identifiers should encode stable source identity and revision or content identity, depending on the update strategy. Idempotent processing prevents retries from duplicating records. Out-of-order events require version checks so an older update cannot overwrite a newer revision. A transactional outbox or equivalent durable change mechanism reduces gaps between recording a source update and publishing its event. Periodic reconciliation compares source revisions with searchable revisions because event delivery alone is rarely a sufficient completeness guarantee.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Parsing is a quality-critical component. A table row without its column headings may produce an embedding that looks relevant while lacking interpretable evidence. I would preserve source offsets, section paths, page references, parent-child relationships, and document versions. Failed documents go to a dead-letter queue with categorized reasons, not into silent omission. Embedding batching improves throughput, but batch size depends on token lengths, provider limits, memory, and latency requirements rather than a universal recommended number.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep the source of truth outside the vector index</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Establish a durable source of truth</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The vector index is a derived serving structure. Authoritative document identity, source revision, lifecycle state, and permissions should be recoverable outside it. I would maintain a durable manifest or canonical document store and a replayable stream of source changes. Every record needs an ordering mechanism appropriate to its source, such as a monotonic revision or connector sequence, rather than relying exclusively on wall-clock timestamps.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A timestamp watermark can help with operational progress, but it is not sufficient by itself when clocks differ, timestamps have coarse precision, or events arrive late. Two updates can share the same timestamp. A deleted document may not appear in an ordinary &quot;updated since&quot; query. The migration must account for creates, updates, deletes, moves, and ACL changes, including events that occur while a historical snapshot is being scanned.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The manifest should let a repair worker answer whether a document is missing, stale, partially processed, or deliberately excluded. Store categorized failures and the version of each transformation. A source connector may produce the same bytes with different ACLs, so content equality alone cannot justify skipping the whole event. Distinguish the expensive semantic work from necessary lifecycle and authorization updates.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make identifiers reflect the intended identity</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">One option uses source identity, revision, and chunk position for staged records. Another uses stable content-derived chunk identities with a separate document-to-chunk membership manifest. Each has trade-offs. A hash that includes chunk index can cause many identifiers to change when a paragraph is inserted near the beginning. Content-only hashes can collide semantically across distinct source resources unless source membership and permissions remain separate.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would define identifier behavior before implementation and test edits, moves, duplicates, and repeated paragraphs. Hash the actual embedding input together with the representation contract for embedding reuse, while preserving distinct provenance for each source occurrence. The vector can be reused without collapsing document permissions or citations. Idempotency should be evaluated at the logical document revision level, not only by counting whether duplicate vector rows appeared.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Capture updates without losing races</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Capture changes before the snapshot can become stale</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">One safe pattern records a source-log position, takes or identifies a consistent snapshot, backfills that snapshot, and replays all subsequent changes. Another pattern continuously consumes the change stream into B while the backfill runs, using conditional revision checks to prevent stale snapshot records from overwriting newer updates. The precise implementation depends on the source system, but the correctness requirement is the same: no transition may lose or resurrect a document revision.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Blind synchronous dual writes are fragile because A can succeed while B fails. A durable outbox or event log makes each index an independently retryable consumer. Processing can be at least once if upserts are idempotent and version-aware. I would track failures separately for A and B and expose each consumer&#x27;s lag. A new index that is ninety-nine percent complete can still be unusable if the missing one percent contains a critical tenant&#x27;s current policies.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prevent stale writes and deletion resurrection</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose document D is revision five in the snapshot. While the backfill runs, revision six arrives and is indexed into B. If the backfill later writes revision five without a conditional check, B regresses. Each write should apply only when its source revision is newer than the stored revision, or when an equivalent ordering rule establishes that it is safe. Duplicate processing of revision six should leave the same effective result.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deletes require equally durable ordering. If D is deleted at revision seven, retain a tombstone or deletion ledger long enough to reject a delayed revision-five backfill. Otherwise a deleted document can reappear. Permission revocations must also override older indexed ACLs. I would explicitly test update-delete-retry sequences, not merely compare final vector counts. Counts can match even when the wrong documents are present.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Diff changes at the right granularity</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an unchanged document body, skip parsing or embedding when compatible cached artifacts exist, but still apply changed metadata. For an edited document, compare normalized section or chunk hashes to reuse unaffected embeddings. Avoid promises of a fixed tenfold saving: if every paragraph changes or a parser revision alters all text, almost the entire document may need processing. The observed edit distribution determines the return on fine-grained incrementality.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Chunk-boundary stability matters. Fixed windows can shift after a small insertion, making otherwise unchanged text appear different. Structure-aware boundaries and content-defined techniques may improve reuse, but they must preserve retrieval quality. Optimizing for stable hashes alone can create poor answer-bearing chunks. I would measure both embedding work avoided and evidence quality across representative document edits, including tables, headings, and reordered sections.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Plan normal and exceptional throughput</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Plan backfill and update throughput</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The initial corpus has eighty million chunks. If the sustained embedding throughput is five thousand chunks per second, embedding alone takes sixteen thousand seconds, or about 4.44 hours. That estimate assumes the provider accepts the token volume and that parsing, network transfer, storage, and indexing keep up. At four hundred tokens per chunk, the same rate means two million input tokens per second. A chunk-based throughput figure without token accounting can conceal an impossible rate-limit assumption.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For ongoing operation, suppose one percent of documents change each day. That is one hundred thousand documents or roughly eight hundred thousand chunks per day under full-document re-embedding, averaging about 9.26 chunks per second. Actual bursts may be far larger. Content hashes can avoid re-embedding unchanged sections. Permission updates often should update authorization metadata without recomputing semantic vectors, while deletes need tombstones that prevent stale content from returning during asynchronous cleanup.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Capacity should distinguish routine updates, large connector imports, permission changes, and rebuilds after model or parser changes. Use separate priorities so a backfill does not delay a critical deletion or policy revision. The queue should retain durable work rather than hold an unlimited in-memory list. Admission control may slow source scanning when the pipeline cannot catch up within its retention and storage limits.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Recover individual stages without duplicating work</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Workers acknowledge a message only after their output and checkpoint are durably recorded. If the output succeeds but acknowledgment fails, replay should observe compatible existing work or repeat it safely. A transactional outbox can coordinate a database state change with the next-stage event. At-least-once delivery is workable when transitions are idempotent and ordered; a transport&#x27;s exactly-once branding does not automatically make external embedding and index writes atomic.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Store provider job identifiers and batch manifests for asynchronous embedding. A timeout does not establish that a submitted job failed, so recovery should inspect the known job before submitting a duplicate expensive batch. Map returned vectors back to exact input records rather than relying blindly on response order unless the API guarantees it. Validate dimensions, item counts, and model configuration before publishing the results.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle retries and permanent failures explicitly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retry transient timeouts, throttling, and selected service errors within a bounded budget. Honor the service&#x27;s retry guidance and add jitter to avoid synchronized worker bursts. Malformed files, unsupported formats, policy exclusions, and invalid credentials need different handling. Retrying a corrupt PDF indefinitely wastes capacity and hides a permanent gap. Categorize the failure and expose an operator action rather than treating every error as a temporary outage.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A dead-letter record should retain the identifiers and protected references needed to reproduce the failure, not necessarily an unrestricted copy of the original sensitive payload. Reprocessing should use a recorded source revision or deliberately fetch a newer one under a new event. Keep retry counts and first-failure age. A queue with a stable size can still contain documents that have been silently failing for weeks.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make deletion reach every serving artifact</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build deletion around lineage</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deletion is difficult when the system cannot locate all derived copies. I would maintain mappings from source documents and revisions to chunks, vectors, generated summaries, cache dependencies, and evaluation records. A subject or data-owner index can assist where appropriate, but many documents mention multiple people, and a single subject identifier is not a complete solution. The connector and processing pipeline should preserve enough lineage to locate affected artifacts reliably.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A deletion workflow can immediately make a resource ineligible for retrieval, invalidate dependent caches, remove active copies, and schedule physical cleanup according to the approved policy. Tombstones prevent a delayed ingestion retry from restoring deleted content. Reconciliation verifies the resource is absent from serving paths and identifies remaining retention-bound copies. The workflow should record completion and exceptions without storing the deleted sensitive content again in the completion log.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For updates, obsolete chunks must become ineligible even if their physical rows remain temporarily. The new manifest defines the valid chunk set, which avoids relying on a best-effort delete after every upsert. Source moves and permission changes may require reassigning scope rather than recomputing embeddings. Test that a delayed retry cannot publish an old revision after deletion or restore an outdated ACL.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Reconcile against complete source inventory</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Events and webhooks are accelerators, not the only correctness mechanism. Periodically compare authoritative source identities and revisions with the serving manifest. Check missing documents, unexpected extra documents, stale versions, incomplete chunk sets, and permission mismatches. The source inventory must itself be complete and consistent enough for the decision; a paginated scan interrupted halfway cannot justify deleting every item it failed to observe.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use checkpoints or snapshots for large scans and distinguish &quot;not seen yet&quot; from confirmed deletion. Repair discrepancies through the same version-aware processing path where possible. Reconciliation can reveal drift but should not overwrite newer records with an older inventory snapshot. Record the comparison boundary and audit repair outcomes so operators can understand why a document changed state.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify publication and recovery behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test duplicate events, out-of-order updates, a crash before and after each durable boundary, partial vector writes, deleted sources, and permission revocations. Inject a failure after staging but before publication and confirm users continue seeing the prior valid revision. Inject another after publication but before acknowledgment and verify replay does not create a second active version. These tests target actual recovery semantics rather than merely repeating a successful ingestion twice.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Measure source-to-publication lag, parse failures by format, work reused, obsolete chunks still eligible, reconciliation mismatches, and deletion delay. Break down results by connector and tenant. A global ninety-nine percent success rate can conceal an entirely broken source type. The objective is an accurate, current, authorized searchable corpus, not a high count of embedding requests completed.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My answer would emphasize ordered revisions, atomic visibility, idempotent transitions, durable work records, and independent reconciliation. Content hashing reduces unnecessary work, while tombstones and revision checks prevent resurrection. Checkpoints and dead-letter handling make failures recoverable, but only if their meaning corresponds to completed durable transitions. The vector database remains a rebuildable serving view of authoritative source state.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">This design makes correctness inspectable. For any document, an operator can identify the source revision, transformation versions, active chunk set, permission state, and outstanding failure. Small edits can be processed efficiently, and large rebuilds can resume safely. The system is complete when users receive the intended published evidence and stale or deleted content is ineligible, rather than when every worker reports that it ran without an exception.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/making-retries-safe-with-idempotent-APIs/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Amazon Builders’ Library: making retries safe with idempotent APIs</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you handle rate limits and backpressure when embedding a large corpus?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would treat large-corpus embedding as a controlled scheduling problem with durable work, explicit quota accounting, bounded concurrency, and fair priorities. The goal is maximum sustainable useful throughput while preserving interactive service capacity and recoverability. Launching more workers does not increase a provider&#x27;s token allowance, and retrying faster can reduce effective throughput. Backpressure should propagate from the limiting stage toward producers before memory, queues, or downstream services become overwhelmed.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Identify every binding limit</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Embedding services can limit requests, input tokens, items per request, individual input length, concurrent jobs, daily volume, or regional capacity. Index writes and source downloads have their own constraints. Read the actual selected service contract and runtime headers rather than assuming a universal batch size. A quota may be shared across projects or models, so several apparently independent worker pools can still compete for one allowance.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separate hard limits from the throughput achieved under current conditions. A service can have a generous token quota but become latency-bound if too few requests are in flight. Conversely, a fast endpoint can reject work because aggregate tokens exceed the account&#x27;s rolling window. Instrument accepted requests, attempted tokens, throttling, and completion rate. This reveals which constraint is actually limiting useful progress.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate the token bottleneck first</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose a hypothetical account permits six hundred requests per minute and 1.2 million input tokens per minute. If the average chunk contains four hundred tokens, the token ceiling is three thousand chunks per minute, or fifty per second. Sending one chunk per request would hit the request ceiling first, at ten chunks per second. Batching ten chunks per call can remove that request bottleneck, but it cannot exceed the token ceiling.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">At fifty chunks per second, an eighty-million-chunk corpus requires at least 1.6 million seconds, or about 18.52 days, for embedding alone. This ignores retries, source processing, and indexing. The calculation prevents an unrealistic backfill deadline. Meeting a shorter deadline requires more approved capacity, fewer eligible input tokens, incremental reuse, or a different processing arrangement, not merely a larger asynchronous worker pool.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Batch by tokens and service constraints</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Group inputs into batches that respect both item and total-token limits, with headroom for estimation error. Use the selected tokenizer or a validated estimator. Document languages and unusual text can produce very different token counts from character counts. A maximum number of inputs is not permission to send that many maximum-length inputs in one request. Validate both constraints independently before dispatch.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Batch formation trades throughput against latency. Backfill items can wait longer for an efficient batch, while urgent updates may need a small batch immediately. Use a maximum waiting time and keep oversized items on a controlled path. Do not let one very long input block many short updates indefinitely. Preserve an input manifest so each returned embedding maps to the correct document revision and chunk identity.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Enforce quotas across the worker fleet</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A limiter in each process is insufficient if every process believes it owns the full account quota. Use a coordinated scheduler, distributed quota allocation, or a design that assigns bounded shares to workers. Model request and token budgets separately. Reserve capacity before sending work and update estimates using observed billing or usage information where available. Define how reservations expire when workers crash.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A token bucket is one possible mechanism, but its parameters must match the service&#x27;s real quota behavior. Providers may use rolling windows or other enforcement rules rather than an identical bucket. Keep conservative headroom and adapt to observed throttling. The scheduler should not assume every rejected request is free or that every timeout consumed no quota. Ambiguous outcomes require careful accounting and reconciliation.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Bound in-flight concurrency</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Concurrency controls how much work can be waiting for responses, not the total allowed rate. If target throughput is five requests per second and average response time is two seconds, roughly ten in-flight requests may be needed in steady state. This is an application of Little&#x27;s law under stable conditions. Add measured headroom without permitting unlimited fan-out, and revisit the estimate as latency changes.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separate semaphore limits for embedding, source fetching, and index writes can prevent one slow dependency from consuming all workers. Limit memory by bytes or tokens as well as task count. Ten large documents can use more memory than hundreds of short ones. When a dependency slows, reduce dispatch and let durable queues absorb bounded work rather than accumulating an unbounded collection of pending promises.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Retry with backoff, jitter, and a budget</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For throttling and eligible transient errors, honor Retry-After or equivalent service guidance. Otherwise use bounded exponential backoff with jitter so workers do not retry in synchrony. A retry is additional load on a service already signaling difficulty. Keep a total attempt or time budget and avoid retrying independently at every layer, which can multiply one logical job into many calls.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Distinguish permanent errors such as invalid input length from temporary failures. Split an invalid batch only when doing so addresses the actual error and preserves record mapping. Quarantine an unsupported item instead of repeatedly blocking the whole batch. If an asynchronous submission times out after acceptance may have occurred, inspect the known job or request identity before resubmitting. A transport timeout is not proof that no work happened.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make queues durable and bounded operationally</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Persist pending work with source identity, revision, model contract, priority, token estimate, attempt count, and deduplication key. A process restart should not lose the corpus inventory or require reconstructing progress from application logs. Queue acknowledgment follows durable completion of the intended transition. If embedding finishes but indexing fails, retain a recoverable reference to the result rather than immediately paying to recompute it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Durable does not mean infinite. Track queue storage, oldest-item age, estimated remaining token work, and expected drain time. If arrival rate persistently exceeds service capacity, backlog grows without bound unless producers slow or capacity changes. Pause connector scans, reduce low-priority admissions, or renegotiate deadlines before storage is exhausted. A queue delays overload; it does not solve an unsustainable rate mismatch.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Propagate backpressure to ingestion producers</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Backpressure should flow from the constrained embedding or indexing stage toward parsers and source scanners. A scanner can checkpoint its position and wait instead of downloading millions of files that cannot be processed soon. Parsed artifacts can be staged durably within limits. Keep control events such as deletion and revocation eligible even when ordinary content backfill is paused.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The response should depend on work class. A product may defer low-priority historical documents while preserving newly published policies. The producer needs a clear admission result and retry policy rather than silently dropping events. Track deferred work in the manifest so completeness reporting remains honest. Users should not be told the corpus is fully current merely because all admitted jobs finished.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect interactive and freshness-critical traffic</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reserve quota and concurrency for query embeddings and urgent updates when they share resources with backfills. A bulk migration should not make the user-facing service unavailable. Separate projects or endpoints may provide stronger isolation, but account-level limits can still be shared. Verify the actual quota hierarchy. Use bulkheads and explicit priority allocation rather than trusting one queue&#x27;s average fairness.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Priority should not create starvation. A constant stream of urgent work can prevent a low-priority backfill from ever completing. Allocate a minimum progress share or use weighted fair scheduling with deadline-aware adjustments. Report expected completion using observed throughput for each class. This is more useful than a single global progress percentage that hides one tenant&#x27;s import waiting behind another tenant&#x27;s large upload.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Provide tenant fairness by work, not just request count</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If one tenant submits long documents and another submits short notes, equal request counts do not provide equal resource access. Schedule using estimated tokens, bytes, or measured processing work. Weighted deficit round-robin is one possible approach. Tenant plans or urgency can determine weights, while global controls keep total demand within capacity. The policy should be explicit enough to explain delays to customers.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Apply per-tenant limits to retries and malformed work too. One broken connector should not monopolize parsing or embedding capacity. Keep source-level failure circuits and quarantine repeated permanent errors. Conversely, do not punish legitimate large files silently; expose supported limits and processing status. Fairness needs observability so operators can distinguish resource contention from a particular customer&#x27;s incomplete permissions or corrupt source data.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use asynchronous jobs where their contract fits</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Batch services can be appropriate for offline backfills that tolerate delayed completion. Compare actual price, capacity, completion guarantees, cancellation behavior, retention, and regional availability. Do not assume a universal fifty percent discount or that every batch finishes by a fixed hour. A large advertised batch allowance may still have account-specific constraints that determine the achievable schedule.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Persist the submitted input manifest and remote job identifier. Poll at a bounded rate or consume completion notifications, then validate item-level outcomes. A successful job can contain failed individual records. Resume only those failures when safe, preserving compatible successful vectors. Keep urgent content on a separate path if the asynchronous service cannot meet its freshness deadline. Offline economics should not redefine the product&#x27;s update contract.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Avoid paying for unchanged work</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cache embeddings by actual input content and representation configuration. A source revision that changes only permissions usually does not require new vectors, though the serving metadata must still change. Chunk-level reuse can reduce cost for small edits, but measure boundary churn and parser changes. Reusing an embedding from an incompatible model is a correctness failure even if it saves quota.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deduplicate logical pending work so repeated source notifications do not each create another expensive request. Newer revisions may supersede queued older revisions if the publication and audit requirements permit it. Preserve deletion ordering and never allow coalescing to resurrect a removed document. The scheduler&#x27;s optimization must operate on source semantics, not merely keep the most recently arrived message regardless of its revision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Checkpoint completion at durable boundaries</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Maintain progress for source discovery, fetched revisions, parsed chunks, accepted embedding jobs, received vectors, and published index state. A checkpoint labeled &quot;embedded&quot; should mean the output is durably available and validated, not just that a request was sent. Recovery uses these states to resume the missing transition. This avoids both lost work and duplicate billing after crashes.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Estimate completion from remaining token work and measured useful throughput, including retries. Report uncertainty for changing quotas and document sizes. Operators need to know whether the backlog is shrinking and whether deadlines remain credible. A percentage based only on documents can accelerate deceptively when early files are tiny and later files are huge. Token- or work-weighted progress is usually more informative.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use a worked backlog-control example</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose source scanners currently admit eighty chunks per second while the approved embedding capacity sustains fifty. The backlog grows by thirty chunks per second, or 108,000 chunks per hour. Adding ten more parsers increases the accumulation rate rather than fixing the constraint. The controller should reduce ordinary admission below fifty while preserving its reserved urgent-update share, or obtain additional embedding capacity. If ten chunks per second are reserved for urgent work, a fully utilized backfill share cannot be assumed to exceed forty during that period.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When arrivals later fall to twenty chunks per second and total processing remains fifty, the queue can drain at thirty chunks per second. A 108,000-chunk backlog then needs about one hour under those simplified assumptions. Real estimates should use remaining tokens and measured failure rates. This example distinguishes processing throughput from backlog drain rate, a common source of misleading completion forecasts. It also shows why the controller should respond before queue age exceeds the promised freshness bound.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Report the right control signals</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The scheduler dashboard should show quota utilization, useful completed tokens, rejection rate, retry amplification, in-flight work, oldest queued age, and per-priority drain estimates. Track the difference between estimated and observed input sizes to tune reservations. A rising provider latency with stable accepted throughput may call for a concurrency adjustment, while rising throttling at full token utilization calls for less dispatch. Different symptoms require different actions; a single &quot;increase workers&quot; response cannot manage them reliably.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify overload behavior deliberately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test throttling, increased provider latency, worker crashes, queue storage pressure, partial batch failures, and a large tenant import during normal interactive traffic. Confirm that memory stays bounded, urgent work meets its target, retries remain limited, and completed outputs are reused. Check that the system recovers after quota returns without immediately creating a synchronized retry storm.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that safe throughput comes from respecting the limiting resource, not maximizing concurrency. Token-aware batching, coordinated quotas, bounded workers, durable queues, fair priorities, and recoverable job state work together. The pipeline should slow predictably under constraint, preserve important updates, and explain its completion estimate. That makes a large embedding backfill operationally controllable instead of a collection of hopeful requests competing until something fails.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://d1.awsstatic.com/builderslibrary/pdfs/timeouts-retries-and-backoff-with-jitter.pdf\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Amazon Builders’ Library: retries, backoff, and jitter</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you make a RAG pipeline degrade gracefully under partial failure?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would define graceful degradation as returning the best behavior that still satisfies the product&#x27;s minimum correctness, authorization, and freshness requirements when a dependency fails. It does not mean always returning an answer. Skipping an optional reranker may be acceptable; skipping authorization is not. Serving partial evidence may be useful for a local lookup but unsafe for an exhaustive compliance question. Each fallback needs an explicit contract, quality evaluation, deadline, and observable status before an incident happens.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Classify dependencies by what they guarantee</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Authentication, authorization, tenant isolation, and required data-handling controls are mandatory boundaries. If they cannot establish permission, fail closed. Query rewriting, some reranking stages, and certain enrichment features may be optional enhancements. Retrieval and generation can have alternate implementations, but the alternatives must satisfy the same applicable security and regional constraints. A dependency&#x27;s role determines its fallback; a generic try-catch cannot make that decision safely.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would create a failure matrix listing each component, recognizable failure modes, timeout, permitted alternative, quality implications, and user-visible behavior. Include stale responses and partial success, not only connection errors. A vector database returning a nonempty but obsolete index can be more dangerous than one returning a clear failure. The matrix should be reviewed with product and security owners because degraded behavior changes what the service promises.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Budget time and isolate failures</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Set deadlines and failure behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would allocate an illustrative first-token budget across authentication, query embedding, retrieval, reranking, context assembly, and model prefill. For example, retrieval might receive a few hundred milliseconds and reranking another limited slice. These are budgets to validate, not component performance promises. Parallel retrieval duration follows the slower required branch plus coordination; dependent stages add sequential delay. Tail latency matters more than a collection of attractive median numbers.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Every remote call needs a deadline and bounded retry policy. Retry storms can multiply load during an outage, so retries should respect an overall request deadline and use backoff with jitter. If the reranker fails, fusion-only retrieval may be an acceptable evaluated degradation. If the authorization service fails, the system should refuse access rather than widen the corpus. Partial shard results must be identified internally and handled according to the application&#x27;s completeness requirements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Streaming improves perceived responsiveness, but unsupported content cannot be safely recalled after being shown. For sensitive workloads, validate evidence and citations before releasing claims or buffer a suitable unit of output. Cancellation should propagate when a user disconnects. Admission control, per-tenant concurrency limits, and separate queues for interactive and batch work prevent one expensive tenant or unusually long request from exhausting the whole system.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Set an overall request deadline, then give stages bounded budgets within it. Propagate cancellation so expired work does not continue consuming model capacity. Use circuit breakers to stop repeated calls to a failing dependency and controlled probes to detect recovery. Bulkheads separate ingestion, reranking, generation, and tenant workloads so one overloaded stage does not consume every worker or connection. Retry budgets must fit inside the remaining deadline.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make reranking optional only after measuring the fallback</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If the reranker times out, fusion-ordered results may remain useful. Evaluate that path on the real query distribution and decide which request classes can use it. The loss is not guaranteed to be modest: queries distinguishing similar policy versions may depend heavily on the reranker. A fallback can select a conservative evidence set or decline a precise answer when ambiguity remains, rather than assuming fewer passages automatically mean safer results.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Record the fallback reason and configuration. Monitor its traffic share, evidence sufficiency, and final quality separately from the primary path. Otherwise availability can remain green while a large fraction of answers becomes worse. Use a cooldown and bounded health probes to avoid oscillation between paths. A successful lightweight probe does not prove the reranker can immediately accept full production load.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle one retrieval branch failing</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If dense search fails, lexical search may answer exact term or identifier queries. If lexical search fails, dense retrieval may still handle conceptual questions. The branches are only operationally redundant when their dependencies are sufficiently independent. If both rely on the same cluster, credentials, network path, or regional outage, hybrid architecture may not provide the expected availability benefit.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Benchmark each single-branch path and define eligible tasks. A lexical-only result for a paraphrased question can miss essential evidence, while dense-only retrieval can confuse exact identifiers. The generator should not receive a falsely reassuring &quot;complete context&quot; marker. Preserve whether the evidence came from a degraded path and apply an appropriate answerability check. Security filters and source-version rules must remain identical across both branches.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Treat missing shards as missing evidence</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A shard timeout can remove an arbitrary portion of the authorized corpus. For some exploratory searches, partial results are useful if the product makes the limitation clear. For &quot;list every affected customer&quot; or &quot;are there any exceptions,&quot; absence of evidence from the responding shards cannot establish completeness. The service should not turn a partial search into an unconditional negative answer.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Replication may restore access to the missing partition, but retries need bounded deadlines and load control. A fallback to a replica must preserve the required freshness and permission state. Distinguish a complete response from an intentionally partial response in telemetry and the API. Whether users need an explicit notice depends on the task, but the system must internally know that coverage changed.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Skip optional query transformations carefully</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If query rewriting fails, use the original question when it is independently meaningful. In a conversation, &quot;what about the second one?&quot; may require contextual resolution; treating it as a complete standalone query can retrieve unrelated evidence. The fallback may instead ask for clarification or use a deterministic conversation reference mechanism. Optional in one request class does not mean optional in every class.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Speculative retrieval can run on the raw query while a rewrite is prepared, but only a rewrite used in a subsequent search can affect evidence. This adds cost and coordination. Cancel unnecessary work once an acceptable path wins, and compare evidence coverage before adopting the speculative design. Running extra tasks in parallel is a latency trade-off, not a guarantee that they are free or useful.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Fail over generation within an approved contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A secondary generator can preserve service when the primary provider fails, but models differ in context limits, tool protocols, structured-output behavior, latency, and quality. Prompt templates and validators need compatible versions. The fallback must also satisfy regional processing and retention requirements. It is not enough that another endpoint accepts the same text. Evaluate representative and high-consequence tasks before enabling automatic switching.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Keep spare capacity or approved quotas for the secondary path. A provider that is cold, untested, or sharing the same failed infrastructure may not help. If failover occurs after partial streaming, avoid splicing two independently generated answers without a clear strategy. The system may restart the response, provide a bounded partial result, or stop with an explanation. Already displayed unsupported content cannot be made safe merely by changing providers afterward.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use cached answers only when still valid</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cache may provide a useful outage path if authorization and source validity can still be established. It must not bypass a failed policy service or serve deleted information because the primary pipeline is unavailable. Define whether the task tolerates bounded staleness, and validate dependencies accordingly. A current answer to an old question is not necessarily applicable to a changed conversation or date.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cold caches and invalidation storms should be part of capacity testing. If normal availability depends on a high hit rate, a widespread source update can overload the fallback just when entries become ineligible. Rate-limit repopulation and prevent stampedes. Report cache-served degraded requests separately so operators can see whether the service is surviving on a temporary reserve that may expire soon.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Offer sources when generation is unavailable</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If retrieval remains healthy and authorized, a search-results response can be useful. Provide applicable titles, snippets, and links under the same access rules. This is a different product mode from a generated answer, so label it clearly and avoid implying that the sources were synthesized or fully checked. The user may still complete the task by opening the relevant document.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If retrieval is also unavailable, the service cannot honestly return freshly retrieved documents. &quot;Everything down&quot; does not produce a search list without another available index or valid cache. A clear temporary limitation can be the correct response. Keep minimal health and status paths independent enough to communicate the problem without disclosing protected source information or inventing a confident answer from model memory.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep rollback targets current</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve a credible rollback path</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Keep A current during the rollback window, including document deletes and permission changes. A warm but stale index is not a safe rollback target. Reserve enough capacity to return traffic quickly and keep A&#x27;s query embedding configuration available. Define rollback triggers and verify that changing the serving configuration restores a complete compatible path, including caches and routing. Exercise the rollback with test traffic before depending on it during an incident.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Rollback becomes harder if a simultaneous schema or chunking change removes information needed by A. I would avoid unrelated irreversible changes during the migration, or build explicit adapters and backward-compatible records. After the observation window, stop A&#x27;s consumer at a recorded position, retain required recovery artifacts, and remove A according to the retention plan. Decommissioning should be a planned operation, not an abandoned duplicate fleet.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Rollback is a fallback too. The older model, index, or configuration must remain compatible with current source revisions, permissions, and schema. A deployment rollback that restores a previously fixed authorization bug is not acceptable. Track compatibility and test recovery against representative current data. Operational reversibility should be an engineered property rather than an assumption that yesterday&#x27;s binary can always replace today&#x27;s safely.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify degraded behavior before relying on it</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate resilience and freshness explicitly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Quality testing should include operational changes, not only static question-answer pairs. Update an authoritative policy, remove a document, revoke a user&#x27;s permission, and simulate a connector delay. Verify whether answers follow the promised freshness and authorization behavior. A system that answers yesterday&#x27;s policy perfectly can still fail today&#x27;s task. Record source timestamps and indexed revisions so a stale answer can be attributed to synchronization rather than incorrectly blamed on generation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exercise dependency failures as well. If reranking becomes unavailable, evaluate the actual fusion-only fallback instead of assuming its quality matches the primary path. If a shard times out, check whether partial evidence causes unsupported certainty. These tests establish the quality of degraded operation and determine which failures permit a limited answer versus requiring a clear temporary limitation. Availability is useful only when the returned behavior remains acceptable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run controlled fault injection for slow dependencies, partial shard loss, quota exhaustion, corrupted responses, and cascading retries. Evaluate both availability and answer quality. Check whether timeouts leave orphaned generation requests or whether fallback loops consume more resources than the primary path. Use synthetic sensitive data to confirm that every degraded route preserves tenant and permission boundaries.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make recovery gradual and observable</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a dependency returns, ramp traffic rather than sending the entire backlog at once. Prioritize current requests and prevent stale work from delaying them. Reconcile any deferred ingestion before advertising normal freshness. Clear circuit-breaker state according to measured health, and verify that caches or temporary routing rules do not keep serving degraded results after recovery. The end of the outage is a state transition that needs its own checks.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Track degraded-mode duration, affected task classes, fallback success, additional cost, and user outcomes. A fallback that hides failures from the HTTP success metric can still consume the quality error budget. Alerts should distinguish a short, expected reranker bypass from a sustained regional failover or incomplete corpus. Owners need to know when the service remains available but no longer meets its intended answer contract.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the production principle</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview answer would be that graceful degradation preserves mandatory guarantees while relaxing evaluated optional capabilities. I would define fallback behavior per component and query class, enforce deadlines and isolation, and test the resulting answers under actual failures. Security failures stop access; evidence failures limit the answer; optional enhancements can be bypassed when their fallback remains acceptable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The key trade-off is not simply quality versus uptime. It is which reduced capability still helps the user without claiming more certainty or completeness than the system possesses. A source list can be useful, a partial search can be appropriate, and a clear refusal can be correct. The architecture earns reliability by making those choices explicit, bounded, observable, and reversible before an incident forces the team to improvise them.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://d1.awsstatic.com/builderslibrary/pdfs/timeouts-retries-and-backoff-with-jitter.pdf\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Amazon Builders’ Library: retries, backoff, and jitter</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://sre.google/workbook/alerting-on-slos/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Google SRE Workbook: alerting on SLOs</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you choose a vector store: pgvector, a dedicated vector DB, or a search engine?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would choose a vector storage and retrieval platform by benchmarking the complete workload, including metadata filtering, updates, permissions, hybrid ranking, recovery, and operator effort. Vector count alone is not a reliable product-selection rule. PostgreSQL with pgvector can be a strong option when relational integration and an existing operational footprint matter. Dedicated vector systems can provide useful specialized capabilities, while search engines can fit workloads where lexical retrieval and ranking are central. The decision should follow measured limits and requirements, not a universal migration threshold.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the workload before comparing products</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Record vector dimensions and representation, corpus growth, query rate, latency objectives, update rate, deletion volume, filter selectivity, tenant distribution, and expected replication.
