Include text and metadata access patterns. Eight million high-dimensional vectors with complex permission filters can be harder than a much larger lower-dimensional, mostly static corpus. A single average query benchmark misses the conditions most likely to cause production problems.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Also define what &quot;vector store&quot; is expected to do. It may only return identifiers and distances, with text, permissions, and lexical search elsewhere. Or it may provide an integrated retrieval layer with hybrid scoring and document storage. Comparing the price of those two architectures without accounting for the missing systems is misleading. The application needs a complete path from authorized query to versioned evidence.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate PostgreSQL integration honestly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">PostgreSQL with pgvector can keep vectors near relational metadata and source manifests. Transactions, familiar SQL, existing backups, and established operational ownership may reduce complexity. This is valuable when the team already operates PostgreSQL well and retrieval can share its data model. A dedicated search replica or separate database can protect transactional workloads when vector search and index builds are resource-intensive.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The cost is not that PostgreSQL becomes invalid above a fixed number of vectors. Instead, measure index memory, build time, write amplification, planner choices, concurrency, and interference with other workloads. Large-scale partitioning, replicas, or sharding may be possible, but they introduce their own design and operational work. The relevant question is whether the selected configuration meets requirements with acceptable headroom and maintenance effort.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Examine actual filtered-search semantics</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Understand filtered vector search behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A vector engine may apply constraints during candidate search, after generating candidates, or through an adaptive strategy. The externally observable guarantee must still prevent unauthorized content from leaving the trusted search layer. Post-filtering a small global top-k can return too few authorized results even when relevant authorized documents exist. That is a recall problem in addition to a security design concern.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would benchmark retrieval under different permission selectivities, including users who can access only a tiny fraction of the tenant corpus. Depending on the engine, a small eligible set may favor exact vector scoring. Larger sets may benefit from integrated filtered ANN. I would not assume filtering is free or that one candidate setting works for every ACL pattern. Security restrictions remain fixed while retrieval algorithms and candidate budgets adapt.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">In pgvector, approximate indexing and SQL filtering need workload-specific attention. Its documented iterative scanning facilities can search additional candidates up to configured limits, but a feature&#x27;s existence does not prove every filter pattern will meet your recall and latency target. Inspect plans and test restrictive ACLs, skewed tenants, and combinations of predicates. Compare against exact search on a suitable eligible subset to separate approximation loss from representation quality. <a href=\"https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Assess dedicated vector platforms by capability</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Dedicated systems may offer partitioning, replication, quantization, payload indexing, or managed operation suited to vector workloads. Verify each product and deployment mode individually. &quot;Dedicated vector database&quot; does not mean every system supports the same consistency, filter behavior, hybrid ranking, or regional options. Managed offerings can reduce some operator work while adding service limits, network costs, and vendor-specific behavior that still need evaluation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use the actual update lifecycle in the benchmark. Insert a new revision, delete obsolete chunks, revoke permissions, and query during compaction or maintenance. Ask whether deletes become logically ineligible promptly and how physical cleanup works. A service that performs well on append-only benchmark data may struggle with a rapidly changing enterprise corpus. Measure recovery and restore behavior as well as steady-state nearest-neighbor latency.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider a search engine for integrated retrieval</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Search engines can be attractive when term matching, field-specific ranking, filtering, faceting, and vector search all matter. An integrated system may reduce synchronization between separate lexical and dense stores. Products such as Elasticsearch, OpenSearch, and Vespa have different architectures and ranking capabilities, so compare the required implementation rather than treating the category as uniform. Existing team expertise can materially affect operating cost.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Avoid declaring that all search engines&#x27; vector features lag specialists. Capabilities and releases change, and the important feature may be filtered search or programmable ranking rather than an isolated ANN result. Integrated hybrid retrieval can simplify some workloads, while another system may offer a better memory-latency trade-off. Use the product version and configuration intended for deployment and preserve those details with every benchmark result.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Distinguish relevance from ANN approximation</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate ANN misses from embedding misses</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run exact similarity search for sampled queries over the same eligible vectors and compare its top results with ANN. If the answer-bearing vector ranks highly in exact search but ANN omits it, index approximation is a likely contributor. Tune search breadth, candidate exploration, partition probes, or quantization settings according to the engine. Measure the resulting latency and memory cost rather than assuming a parameter increase is free.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If exact search also ranks the passage poorly, the issue is not merely ANN. The embedding model may miss negation, domain terminology, entity identity, or the query&#x27;s constraints. Chunk content may be too broad or incomplete. Lexical retrieval, better preprocessing, domain adaptation, or a different representation may help. Exact search establishes the best neighbors under the current similarity function; it does not establish the most relevant evidence under human judgment.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The platform should be compared at the same acceptable evidence-retrieval quality. One engine returning results faster with lower ANN recall is not an equivalent configuration unless the quality trade-off is acceptable. Likewise, exact nearest neighbors can all be semantically wrong. Hold the embeddings and source snapshot constant for infrastructure comparisons, then evaluate the full answer pipeline separately. This avoids attributing a representation change to a database advantage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design tenant isolation independently of branding</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose a suitable isolation model</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Physical isolation can place sensitive tenants in separate clusters, databases, or service accounts. This gives strong operational boundaries and can simplify residency and customer-managed key requirements. It also increases deployment, backup, monitoring, and upgrade work. A cluster per small tenant may be unnecessarily expensive, while regulated or unusually large tenants may justify dedicated infrastructure. I would choose isolation based on threat model, scale, and contractual requirements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Logical isolation uses a shared cluster with tenant-specific collections, namespaces, partitions, or mandatory filters. Those mechanisms have different security properties across products. A namespace name is not automatically a security boundary if an application service credential can query every namespace. I would inspect the database&#x27;s actual authorization model and ensure the serving service cannot accidentally bypass the intended restrictions through another endpoint or administrative query path.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A mixed model is often practical: shared infrastructure for ordinary tenants, dedicated partitions or clusters for large and sensitive tenants, and the same document authorization contract everywhere. This avoids tying the application semantics to one storage layout. Moving a tenant between deployment tiers should not change who can access its documents. Infrastructure isolation reduces blast radius, but within-tenant permissions still require explicit enforcement.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare realistic memory and storage footprints</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Budget vectors, graph or partition structures, original vectors for rescoring, metadata indexes, text, replicas, logs, and maintenance headroom. A quantized payload can reduce one component while the total footprint remains dominated by something else. Some systems keep searchable structures in memory, others use different memory-disk arrangements. Measure actual resident memory and storage under representative data rather than applying one engine&#x27;s formula to another.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Include peak operations. Index construction, shard relocation, backup, recovery, and compaction can require additional space and bandwidth. A system that fits comfortably at steady state can fail during a rebuild. Compare both full-load and reduced-capacity behavior after a node loss. A slightly larger steady-state footprint may be acceptable if recovery is simpler and the service maintains its latency objective during maintenance.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Benchmark latency under concurrency and skew</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use the query length, filter distribution, k values, candidate effort, and concurrency expected in production. Report p50, p95, and p99, plus achieved throughput and error rates. Include cold and warm caches. A large tenant or a popular partition can create hotspots hidden by uniform random queries. Read replicas distribute some work but may add freshness considerations depending on the platform&#x27;s consistency model.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Benchmark end-to-end evidence access, not just vector distance computation. Network round trips, metadata fetches, authorization checks, and text materialization can dominate a very fast ANN kernel. Also record whether the service returns complete results or times out with partial coverage. The product&#x27;s acceptable result must be the unit of comparison. A fast partial answer should not be scored as equivalent to a complete one without an explicit task policy.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Understand partition and routing trade-offs</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Scale partitions without hiding routing losses</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Several scoped indexes can improve efficiency and relevance when boundaries reflect real query structure, such as tenant, product family, or source category. But partitioning introduces routing errors and cross-partition synthesis work. A question spanning two products must reach both relevant partitions. More shards can also increase scatter-gather overhead and tail latency. There is no general rule that several smaller indexes always outperform one large index.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would evaluate routing recall, per-partition load, skew, failure behavior, and the global merge strategy. Large tenants may require dedicated capacity while small tenants share infrastructure. Search parameters may adapt to authorized corpus size and filter selectivity. The architecture should preserve consistent security and evidence semantics across layouts so operational scaling does not change the meaning of a retrieved result or an answerability decision.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Include reliability and operating ownership</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Ask who handles upgrades, backup verification, shard repair, capacity expansion, certificate changes, and incident response. A managed service changes the division of responsibility but does not remove application-level recovery, permission synchronization, or cost management. An open-source system can be economical with experienced operators and expensive without them. Make those assumptions visible in the comparison rather than pretending that software license price is total cost.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run a restore exercise and verify source manifests, vectors, deletions, and ACL versions together. A vector snapshot without compatible text and permissions may not produce a safe serving state. Check observability and export capabilities so failures can be diagnosed. Evaluate regional placement and approved processing paths where required. Operational fit often determines whether a promising benchmark becomes a dependable production service.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep an application-level retrieval contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Define requests in terms of authorized scope, query representation, required filters, candidate budget, and result provenance. Return identifiers, source revisions, score type, and completeness metadata. This creates a useful boundary for testing alternate backends. It does not require hiding every database-specific feature behind a lowest-common-denominator API. Expose specialized capabilities deliberately when they provide value, with a clear fallback or migration strategy.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Preserve a canonical source and representation manifest so rebuilding elsewhere remains possible. Exporting raw vectors alone may omit permission semantics, source lineage, or text needed by the next engine. A measured migration path is more credible than claiming vendor independence while relying on undocumented ranking behavior. Version the backend configuration with evaluation results so compatibility can be assessed during a future move.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare a small set of concrete candidate configurations</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would shortlist two or three configurations rather than create a broad feature checklist with no workload evidence. For example, compare an isolated PostgreSQL deployment, an approved managed vector service, and an integrated search engine using the same embeddings and source revisions. Set an acceptable retrieval-quality floor first, then compare the cost and latency of configurations that meet it. Record tuning effort and failure behavior alongside the numerical results. This prevents an untuned candidate from being dismissed unfairly and prevents endless benchmarking after a suitable choice already meets the requirements.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make a conditional recommendation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an organization already operating PostgreSQL, I would evaluate pgvector first when it can meet the workload with adequate isolation from transactional traffic. For specialized vector requirements or scaling needs that the benchmark exposes, I would compare dedicated systems. For rich lexical and programmable ranking requirements, I would give integrated search platforms serious consideration. These are starting points for experiments, not fixed size-based rules.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be to choose the smallest operational system that meets quality, security, freshness, and recovery objectives under realistic load. The most valuable tests involve selective filters, updates, deletion, skew, and failure capacity. I would move platforms when a measured constraint or capability need justifies the migration, and keep the evidence contract and source lineage strong enough that the move can be evaluated rather than improvised.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">pgvector: indexing, filtering, and iterative scans</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://qdrant.tech/documentation/search/filtering/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Qdrant: filtering</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://docs.vespa.ai/en/querying/nearest-neighbor-search.html\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Vespa: nearest-neighbor search</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you route queries across multiple indexes and non-retrieval tools?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would build query routing as a policy-controlled planning layer that chooses the sources and tools needed to satisfy a request. It should distinguish document evidence, live transactional facts, structured aggregates, and requests that need no retrieval. The router&#x27;s output is a proposed typed plan, not permission to access a backend. Every selected route must independently enforce the user&#x27;s tenant, resource permissions, and allowed operations. Routing quality should be evaluated because a wrong route can look exactly like a weak retriever.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start from the kind of answer required</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">&quot;What is our cancellation policy?&quot; calls for applicable policy documentation. &quot;Was order O-482 delivered?&quot; calls for the current orders service. &quot;How many orders were delayed last quarter?&quot; needs a defined aggregate over structured records. &quot;Explain why this order was delayed and whether a refund applies&quot; may need both the live order state and policy evidence. A greeting can be answered without searching a private corpus.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">These requests differ in freshness, completeness, authority, and side effects. Retrieving ten support tickets cannot establish the total number of delayed orders. A static document may explain how to inspect an order but cannot establish its current status. I would define routes by information contracts rather than by vague topic labels. That makes it easier to detect when a tool result is insufficient for the requested answer.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define a route registry and typed outputs</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each backend description should specify supported questions, required inputs, authorized scope, data freshness, output schema, cost, and latency expectations. Separate read-only lookups from state-changing actions. The router returns route identifiers, structured arguments, unresolved ambiguities, and a bounded execution plan. The executor validates these fields against the registry; arbitrary model-generated URLs or database names should not become new routes.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For example, an order lookup route may require a tenant-scoped order identifier, while an aggregate route requires a metric definition and date interval. Missing arguments can trigger clarification or an approved lookup. Do not let the model invent identifiers simply to satisfy the schema. Structural validity makes a plan executable, but semantic checks must establish that the arguments correspond to the user&#x27;s actual request.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use the cheapest reliable routing mechanism</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Rules can handle unambiguous identifiers and explicit product selections. A classifier can learn recurring intents from labeled examples. Embedding-based route matching can provide a simple baseline, while a language model can help with ambiguous combinations and decomposition. Layering them may be economical, but each added stage increases maintenance and possible disagreement. Measure the benefit rather than assuming an LLM is always the most flexible correct choice.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Rules need careful scope. The appearance of an order identifier does not mean every question should go only to the orders API; the user may be asking which policy applies to that order. Classifiers need an unknown or ambiguous outcome and calibrated thresholds. A router that confidently chooses among incomplete options will hide missing capabilities as retrieval misses. Maintain an explicit unsupported-request path and monitor it for product opportunities.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Narrow document routes without losing evidence</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Narrow the corpus with reliable constraints</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Mandatory tenant and authorization filters come first. Additional metadata such as product, region, document type, effective date, and language can improve relevance when the query provides reliable constraints. Searching the applicable product&#x27;s current policies is often easier than distinguishing them from every historical policy after retrieval. Structured metadata can preserve distinctions that semantic embeddings blur.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">However, incorrect routing can destroy recall. If the user does not specify a region, silently choosing one may exclude the correct policy. I would distinguish certain constraints from inferred ones, evaluate router recall, and allow bounded multi-route search or clarification when ambiguity matters. Mandatory security filters are never relaxed. Relevance filters can be broadened in a controlled fallback when they were uncertain and evidence remains insufficient.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use hybrid retrieval when it adds complementary evidence</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Lexical search is useful for exact identifiers, acronyms, quoted clauses, and rare names. Dense retrieval handles paraphrases and conceptual similarity. Their combination can recover evidence missed by either alone. I would measure unique relevant contributions from each branch and fuse results with a calibrated or rank-based method. Raw lexical scores and cosine scores should not be added casually because their scales differ.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Hybrid search is not mandatory for every product. A small, well-structured semantic corpus may perform adequately with dense retrieval alone. An identifier-heavy catalog may benefit primarily from exact and lexical lookup. The decision follows the workload and operating cost. Adding a second retrieval engine without measurable complementary value increases complexity, latency exposure, and security-filter surface without necessarily improving answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Support multi-route plans and dependencies</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Independent evidence calls can run in parallel, while dependent calls must wait for required results. The order&#x27;s product edition may determine which policy index applies, so policy retrieval may depend on the live lookup. A plan should represent that dependency rather than launching a broad expensive search and hoping the final model resolves everything. Bound route count, depth, and total work to avoid an unbounded agent loop.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Multi-route evidence needs provenance and conflict handling. If a live API and an old support ticket disagree about status, define which source is authoritative for current state. If two policy sources disagree, retain applicability and effective dates rather than average their answers. The final generator should know which facts came from which backend and when they were observed. Fusion across document lists is different from combining heterogeneous tool results.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle aggregates through constrained data access</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For &quot;how many tickets last quarter,&quot; define the population, time zone, status semantics, duplicate handling, and date boundaries. A warehouse query or approved analytics API can compute the result over the complete eligible dataset. Text retrieval cannot prove completeness from a top-k sample. A router should recognize count, sum, average, trend, and grouping requests and decide whether a supported structured source can answer them.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Text-to-SQL should operate through a constrained read-only environment with approved schemas, row-level authorization, statement validation, execution limits, and result-size bounds. Parameterize values where possible and reject unauthorized tables or operations. A model-produced SELECT can still be expensive or disclose aggregate information outside the allowed scope. Database-side enforcement and a semantic query layer provide stronger guarantees than asking the model to remember restrictions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Distinguish live lookups from document retrieval</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Current inventory, payment status, incident state, and shipping events belong to systems of record with explicit freshness semantics. The router should not substitute model memory or an old document when a live source fails. It may provide general instructions or a clearly limited response, but it should not state a current transactional fact without evidence. Record observation times so users can interpret rapidly changing results.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Some tasks combine current values with derived calculations. Use deterministic computation for arithmetic and preserve the inputs and units. If a tool returns a partial dataset, do not label the aggregate complete. The route contract should expose completeness and pagination state. A successful network call can still produce an insufficient answer if only the first page was read or a relevant account was outside the authorized query scope.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep authorization outside routing predictions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The router may suggest a source, but a trusted executor verifies access for the current user and active tenant. A document mentioning an internal database cannot grant permission to query it. Credentials should be injected outside the model context and scoped to the route. Tool descriptions are controlled configuration; source text and tool results are data, even when they contain instruction-like language or suggest another tool call.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Default routes must remain permission-safe. If classification is uncertain, searching every tenant or using an administrator credential is not an acceptable fallback. The system can broaden optional relevance scope within the authorized corpus, try several approved routes, or ask a clarification. Separate the permission boundary from routing confidence so a low-confidence prediction changes search strategy rather than security policy.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use adaptive work budgets deliberately</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Add adaptive paths when the evidence supports them</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A simple identifier lookup may succeed with exact lexical retrieval and a small context, while a comparison across several policies may need query decomposition and a larger candidate set. An adaptive router can choose different budgets. However, the router becomes another failure source. A false &quot;easy&quot; classification may skip the reranker precisely when similar policy versions are hard to distinguish. I would evaluate routing errors and maintain a safe escalation path.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retries should respond to diagnosed gaps. If candidate coverage is poor, increasing the generation model&#x27;s size does not add missing evidence. A second retrieval attempt might broaden optional filters, use alternate terminology, or search a specific authorized source. Mandatory access filters must never be relaxed. The additional attempt also needs a bounded latency and cost budget so ambiguous queries cannot trigger unbounded retrieval loops.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A route can specify maximum calls, token budget, query timeout, and acceptable degraded behavior. More complex plans need evidence that their added work improves task completion. A planner that uses five backends for every question may be flexible but unnecessarily costly and slow. Record why each route was selected in structured terms, such as required current order state or unresolved policy condition, rather than relying on a lengthy generated rationale.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Identify exhaustive tasks before selecting top-k evidence</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Recognize the limits of top-k retrieval</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarity search is good at finding likely evidence, but it does not establish exhaustive coverage. If the user asks for every liability exception, the highest-ranked passages may omit a rare exception in an appendix. Increasing k helps only until the context budget is exhausted and still does not prove completeness. I would use section enumeration, structured extraction, or a coverage-aware scan for tasks where missing one item is unacceptable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The same issue applies to global summaries. A few highly similar passages can overrepresent the introduction and miss important later sections. For broad synthesis, the system needs a plan that accounts for the document&#x27;s structure and distribution of content. Retrieval can help identify relevant sections, but the answer should not claim to summarize the entire document unless the processing path actually provides suitable coverage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve conversation meaning and clarify selectively</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Follow-up questions often omit entities established earlier. The router should use the relevant conversation state, while checking that previous source access remains valid. &quot;What about the other account?&quot; may require clarification if several accounts are possible. Do not guess a high-impact target merely because one identifier appears most recently. The cost of a brief clarification can be lower than an incorrect lookup or action.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Language, locale, and time zone can affect route arguments. &quot;Last quarter&quot; needs the organization&#x27;s calendar convention, and a date such as 03/04 can be ambiguous. Store resolved values explicitly in the plan. When assumptions are safe and reversible, the product can state them and proceed; when they change a consequential result, gather the missing constraint. Routing is an interpretation task with a concrete acceptance criterion.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build routing-specific evaluation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Create labeled cases with required and prohibited routes, acceptable alternatives, needed arguments, and expected clarification behavior. Measure route recall for all necessary backends, unnecessary-route rate, argument correctness, unauthorized attempts, cost, and latency. A single-label accuracy score is insufficient when some questions require several sources. Evaluate the final answer too, because a technically correct route can return data the synthesizer misuses.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test ambiguous identifiers, paraphrases, aggregate requests, multi-hop dependencies, no-retrieval greetings, unsupported tasks, and injection-bearing source content. Include negative controls where routing to SQL or a live API would be unnecessary. Keep source and tool versions with results. A route regression after a tool-description edit can otherwise appear to be a retrieval-model failure and send debugging efforts to the wrong component.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Observe route behavior in production</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Log the selected route identifiers, arguments after validation, authorization outcome, execution completeness, fallback path, and final evidence references under appropriate privacy controls. Monitor route distribution changes alongside product launches and query drift. A sudden rise in the default route can indicate a classifier issue or a new legitimate use case. Correlate user feedback with the actual plan, not merely the final model response.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Roll out route changes through offline comparison, shadow planning, and bounded canaries. Shadowing can compare proposed plans without executing side effects. For read-only tools, a limited shadow execution may reveal latency and result differences, subject to authorization and cost. Do not let an experiment duplicate external actions. The execution policy should remain independent of whether the plan came from control or treatment.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would explain routing as choosing the right evidence mechanism under a task and permission contract. Documents answer applicable knowledge questions, live APIs answer current state, structured tools answer aggregates, and some requests need no backend. Multi-route plans represent dependencies and preserve provenance, while trusted executors enforce access, schemas, and work limits.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The strongest design is not the one with the largest collection of tools. It is the one that reliably identifies what information is required, obtains it from the appropriate authorized source, and exposes unresolved gaps. I would keep routing separately measurable, clarify consequential ambiguity, and use deterministic computation and policy enforcement where available. That improves quality by addressing the source-selection problem before trying to improve ranking inside the wrong source.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://www.postgresql.org/docs/17/ddl-rowsecurity.html\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">PostgreSQL: row security policies</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you approach multilingual and cross-lingual retrieval?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would separate multilingual support from cross-lingual retrieval, then evaluate the complete pipeline for each required language pair. Multilingual support can mean Hindi questions over Hindi documents and English questions over English documents. Cross-lingual retrieval means the query and useful source are in different languages, such as an English question whose best evidence is in Japanese. A model advertised as multilingual does not establish equal quality across languages, scripts, domains, or mixed-language queries.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the actual language matrix</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">List supported query languages, source languages, desired answer languages, and required cross-language pairs. Include transliteration, regional terminology, and code switching when users employ them. For an Indian enterprise, a user might write Hindi in Latin characters while documents are in English, or mix English product identifiers with Hindi explanation. A language detector that assigns one label to the whole request may miss that structure.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Requirements should specify whether the system must search other languages when same-language evidence is absent. If a French user can access an English policy, should the assistant translate it and cite the original? What happens when localized policies differ? Language similarity does not establish jurisdictional applicability. These decisions affect routing, ranking, answer generation, and evaluation, so they belong in the product contract before index construction.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Inspect extraction and normalization by script</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The first failure may be document parsing rather than embeddings. OCR can lose diacritics, join words, split characters, or mishandle right-to-left layout. Tables and bilingual headings can become scrambled. Inspect extracted text alongside originals for each important format and script. If the answer-bearing span was corrupted, a stronger retriever cannot recover information that never entered the index correctly.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use Unicode normalization deliberately and preserve meaningful distinctions. Case folding, punctuation removal, and transliteration can help some searches while changing names or identifiers in others. Keep original text for display and citation. A search-normalized field can coexist with the original rather than overwrite it. Version normalization rules so changes can be evaluated and reindexed predictably instead of silently altering retrieval behavior.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose embeddings using representative pairs</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cross-lingual embedding model aims to place semantically corresponding text from supported languages in a compatible representation space. Evaluate candidate models on the actual query-document language pairs and domain vocabulary. Published multilingual benchmarks are useful starting evidence, but averages can hide weak performance on a low-resource language or specialized terminology. A model can perform well on same-language retrieval and poorly on a required cross-language direction.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test both directions when relevant. English-to-Japanese performance need not match Japanese-to-English behavior under the query and document distributions you have. Include named entities, abbreviations, negation, numbers, and policy conditions. Follow the selected model&#x27;s query/document input conventions and token limits. Truncation or missing task prefixes can damage results even when the underlying model supports the language pair.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Treat lexical search as language-aware</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Lexical retrieval still matters for names, exact product codes, quoted text, and domain vocabulary. Ordinary term matching does not automatically bridge translations, but shared identifiers, dictionaries, transliteration fields, and translation can provide useful connections. It would be too strong to say lexical retrieval never transfers across languages. Its effectiveness depends on the representation of terms and the actual query-source overlap.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use appropriate analyzers, tokenization, stemming, and segmentation for supported languages. Whitespace tokenization is insufficient for many writing systems. Aggressive stemming can also collapse meaningful distinctions or damage names. Maintain raw keyword fields for exact identifiers where needed. Evaluate lexical and dense branches separately so a broken analyzer is not hidden by acceptable dense retrieval on common questions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make chunking token-aware and structure-aware</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Token-per-character behavior varies by language and tokenizer. There is no universal twofold or threefold multiplier that applies to every language pair or model. Measure actual lengths using the representation and generation tokenizers. Preserve sentences, headings, table relationships, and definitions rather than applying an English punctuation splitter to every script. Overlap should restore useful relationships without duplicating excessive text.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A fixed token budget can contain different amounts of semantic content across languages. Compare retrieval and answer quality at realistic passage sizes rather than assuming one chunk size transfers perfectly. Mixed-language documents may need language metadata at section or span level. Keep neighboring context accessible so a short technical statement can be interpreted with its heading and applicable conditions, regardless of the script used.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare shared and language-specific indexes</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A shared multilingual vector index can simplify cross-language candidate search and avoid a hard language-routing boundary. Its quality depends on embeddings, corpus composition, filters, and ranking; sharing does not inherently dilute quality in every case. Language metadata can support preference or diversification while leaving cross-language evidence eligible. Tenant and resource permissions must remain mandatory filters independent of language.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Language-specific indexes can support specialized analyzers and operational tuning. They do not inherently prevent cross-lingual retrieval: the router can translate or embed a query compatibly and fan out across several indexes, then merge results. The trade-off is routing complexity, duplicate work, and cross-index calibration. A wrong language prediction should not permanently exclude the only authoritative source when the product requires cross-language coverage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider translation at query time</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Translating a query into one or more source languages can help lexical retrieval or compensate for a weak cross-language embedding path. Preserve names, codes, dates, and user constraints during translation. A mistranslated negation or product edition can produce confidently irrelevant evidence. Keep the original query alongside the translation for reranking and debugging, and record which translation configuration was used.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Translation adds latency and cost, especially with multi-language fan-out. Use it selectively when evaluation demonstrates benefit. A dictionary or domain glossary may be more predictable for technical terms than free-form translation alone. If the query is ambiguous, translation can amplify the ambiguity rather than resolve it. Clarification may be appropriate when alternate meanings lead to materially different sources or answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider translation at ingestion time</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Creating a pivot-language representation can support lexical search and simplify some downstream processing. Retain the original source and an alignment between translated spans and original spans. The translation is a derived artifact with its own version, permissions, and deletion dependencies. It should not be presented as an independently authoritative document or remain searchable after its source is removed.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Translation can change legal or technical meaning, especially with exceptions and specialized terms. For high-consequence claims, verify against the original or use reviewed translations. Citation interfaces can show the original passage plus a clearly identified translation. This is more honest than citing a generated translation as if the source itself contained that exact wording. The economic choice depends on update frequency and repeated query volume.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use a compatible reranker</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A multilingual retriever followed by an English-only reranker can lose the cross-language evidence it successfully found. Evaluate the reranker&#x27;s supported language pairs and input lengths. Joint query-passage scoring must preserve the actual constraints and source language. If translation is used before reranking, its errors become part of the ranking pipeline and should be included in evaluation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Scores may differ across languages and source types. Avoid comparing uncalibrated branch scores as if they shared a universal relevance scale. Rank fusion, language-specific calibration, or learned ranking can be tested with labeled cases. Do not boost same-language evidence unconditionally when a different-language source is more authoritative or current. Language preference and source applicability are distinct signals.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Generate in the requested language with source fidelity</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The answer language should follow the user&#x27;s request or product preference, while preserving exact identifiers and important terminology. Evidence may be in another language, so the generator effectively performs translation and synthesis. Check that quantities, units, dates, exceptions, and uncertainty survive. Fluent output alone does not establish faithful cross-language grounding, and an English judge may miss errors in another script.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For mixed-language users, define a reasonable response convention and allow explicit overrides. Do not translate code identifiers or proper names unnecessarily. When a term has no precise equivalent, retaining the original with a short explanation may be clearer. The answer should cite the applicable original source, making it possible for an authorized reader to verify the claim even when the prose is translated.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve locale and applicability separately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Language does not determine country, legal regime, currency, time zone, or product availability. An English question can concern an Indian policy, and a German-language source can describe a global product. Resolve relevant locale from explicit user context and trusted organizational settings rather than a language label alone. If uncertain and consequential, ask rather than infer a policy region from spelling.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Date and number formats also require care. Decimal separators, digit scripts, calendar conventions, and unit names can vary. Normalize values into structured internal forms while preserving source display. Deterministic calculations should operate on validated quantities and units. A translated answer that changes 1.000 from one thousand to one can be fluent and catastrophically wrong despite matching the general topic.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build evaluation by language pair and task slice</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Create human-reviewed cases for same-language and cross-language retrieval, including answerable, unanswerable, ambiguous, and region-specific questions. Measure answer-bearing recall, final evidence sufficiency, citation support, translation fidelity, and answer correctness. Report each important language pair separately. A traffic-weighted average can be dominated by English and conceal an unusable experience for a smaller user group.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use native or domain-competent reviewers for high-value slices. Machine translation can help scale review, but it can also mask or introduce the very errors being measured. Synthetic translated questions should be checked for naturalness and equivalence. Include code switching, transliteration, named entities, and noisy OCR rather than evaluating only clean textbook sentences. Version sources and translations to make comparisons reproducible.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Test translated no-answer cases</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Include questions whose answer is absent in every supported language, as well as cases where only an inapplicable regional translation exists. The system should not treat finding a semantically similar foreign-language passage as evidence that the requested fact is available. Appropriate uncertainty must survive translation just as carefully as dates and quantities.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Monitor multilingual production behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Track route distribution, no-answer rates, source-language selection, fallback translation use, latency, token usage, and sampled quality by language. Inspect changes in language detection and tokenizer behavior after model updates. A new product term may cause one language&#x27;s lexical branch to degrade while global metrics remain stable. Capture feedback on the specific passage and translated claim when possible.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Protect privacy across translation services as well as embedding and generation services. A query translation can contain personal information, and document translation can transmit the whole source. Apply approved regional, retention, and access rules to every processor. Multilingual support should not create an unreviewed data-transfer path simply because translation is considered a preprocessing convenience.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Walk through a cross-language policy example</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose an English-speaking employee asks whether a Japanese product edition permits exports after cancellation, and the authoritative current policy is Japanese. The request first resolves the product edition and active tenant. A multilingual dense branch searches eligible sources, while a lexical branch can use a reviewed terminology mapping and preserve the exact edition identifier. The two branches return source spans, revisions, language labels, and applicability metadata rather than anonymous snippets.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The reranker must evaluate the English question against Japanese evidence or a validated aligned translation. If it supports only same-language pairs, route through an evaluated translation path rather than assuming its score is meaningful. Context selection adds the cancellation exception and definition from the same source revision. The generator then answers in English, retaining the precise retention interval and citing the original Japanese passage. A translation preview can help the user inspect the source, but it remains labeled as a translation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If only an older English policy is found, the system should not prefer it merely because it matches the query language. Source currency and edition applicability govern the decision. If the Japanese text is corrupted by OCR or the exception cannot be verified, the answer should identify that evidence limitation or seek a better source. This example shows how language support interacts with ordinary RAG correctness rather than replacing it. The language model&#x27;s fluent English response is the final presentation layer, while reliable evidence selection depends on every preceding step.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would frame multilingual RAG as a chain of language-sensitive components: extraction, normalization, chunking, representation, lexical analysis, routing, reranking, generation, and evaluation. Cross-lingual embeddings are important, but they cannot fix corrupted source text, an incompatible reranker, or a mistranslated condition. The architecture should preserve originals and provenance while using translation and language-specific indexes where measured benefits justify them.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The decision follows the required language matrix and source lifecycle. I would start with representative language-pair tests, keep language separate from jurisdiction and authorization, and measure task success rather than fluency alone. That produces a system where a user can ask naturally, receive an answer in the right language, and verify the decisive claim against the correct authorized source, even when that source is written differently.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.05672\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Multilingual E5 Text Embeddings: technical report</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://bge-model.com/bge/bge_m3\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">BGE-M3: model documentation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you build a feedback loop that actually improves retrieval over time?","g":["rag observability"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A useful retrieval feedback loop converts observed problems into verified labels, targeted changes, and measured production improvements. It does not automatically train on every click or negative rating. I would connect feedback to the exact response and evidence trace, diagnose the failure, correct for important collection biases, and turn confirmed cases into regression tests, training examples, or content work. The loop closes only when an evaluated change improves the intended task without amplifying its own ranking and presentation biases.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Capture signals at an interpretable level</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Whole-answer ratings are easy to collect but ambiguous. A user can dislike tone, latency, missing evidence, an outdated source, or a correct refusal. Citation-level relevance feedback identifies a particular passage, but even that may mean the passage is poorly displayed rather than irrelevant. Offer a small set of useful failure reasons and allow optional detail. Keep the interaction lightweight enough that people actually use it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Implicit signals include clicks, dwell, copied text, regeneration, rephrased questions, and escalation. None is automatically a truth label. An expert may click a citation to verify a good answer; a confused user may click because the answer is wrong. Escalation can be appropriate for a sensitive case. I would retain the event&#x27;s meaning as an observation and let a defined interpretation process decide whether it supports a training or evaluation label.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Join feedback to versioned evidence</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each event should reference response identity, conversation turn, serving configuration, source revisions, candidate pool, selected context, cache status, and relevant authorization context. Use protected identifiers rather than indiscriminate raw-content logging. Without that connection, a complaint about yesterday&#x27;s answer may be evaluated against today&#x27;s changed corpus. The team can then mislabel the retriever or train on evidence the original system never saw.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Distinguish what was exposed to the user from what was merely considered internally. A passage ranked twentieth and never shown cannot receive direct click feedback. A cached answer may reflect an older retrieval configuration. Feedback on a follow-up may concern a conversational misunderstanding. These distinctions are necessary for meaningful attribution and prevent simplistic pipelines from assigning every failure to the embedding model.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a representative reviewed dataset</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Construct a representative versioned dataset</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would combine authorized production samples, expert-written cases, known incidents, and carefully reviewed synthetic questions. Each record should include the question, relevant tenant and permission context, applicable source snapshot, expected answer properties, supporting passages, and acceptable abstention behavior. Reference answers are useful, but a single wording should not define the only correct response. The rubric should permit equivalent phrasing while requiring the decisive facts and conditions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Slice the dataset by query type, language, tenant size, document format, freshness requirement, permission selectivity, and difficulty. Include exact identifier lookups, paraphrases, multi-hop questions, tables, long documents, temporal comparisons, no-answer cases, and adversarial source content. Weighting should reflect the production distribution while retaining enough difficult cases to detect rare serious failures. A dataset containing only popular easy questions can give misleadingly strong results.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate development and final assessment</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use a development set for prompt tuning, retrieval parameters, chunking choices, and judge calibration. Keep a held-out set for final comparison, with a process to prevent repeated tuning against its failures. If every held-out failure is immediately used to optimize the next run, that set eventually becomes development data. Periodic refresh and new source snapshots help maintain a meaningful generalization check.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Regression cases serve another purpose: they ensure known failures do not recur. They can be included in a dedicated suite even when the broader benchmark is held out. I would distinguish public-to-the-team regression cases from protected final-assessment cases rather than pretending one dataset can serve both roles indefinitely. Production examples also need privacy controls, retention rules, and source-deletion lineage before they become reusable evaluation assets.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Diagnose before creating training labels</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a negative response, ask whether the source contained the required information, whether it was ingested correctly, whether retrieval found it, whether reranking or context selection lost it, and whether generation misused it. A missing policy is a content gap. A stale policy is a synchronization issue. An unsupported generated sentence is not necessarily evidence that the cited passage is an irrelevant negative for retrieval training.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use a review rubric and permit uncertain labels. A passage may be partially relevant but insufficient alone, or correct for a different product edition. These distinctions can support graded ranking labels and hard-negative categories. Human reviewers need the task constraints and source applicability, not just the question and snippet. Otherwise the dataset can encode confident but inconsistent judgments that make the next model worse.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Address position and exposure bias</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Top results receive more attention because the system placed them there. Raw click counts can therefore teach a new ranker to reproduce the old ordering. Presentation, source title, user expertise, and answer wording also influence behavior. Counterfactual methods can help when their assumptions and propensity estimates are appropriate, but they are not a universal correction that makes all implicit feedback unbiased. <a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/1608.04468\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Controlled randomization or interleaving can provide better evidence about relative ranking quality for suitable interfaces. Exploration must preserve authorization and minimum quality constraints. Log exposure probabilities when the method requires them, and verify that the estimator&#x27;s assumptions fit the product. Extremely small propensities can create unstable weights. Use diagnostics and bounded strategies rather than applying inverse-propensity weighting mechanically to a dataset that lacks valid exposure information.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calibrate automated labelers against humans</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calibrate thresholds against human judgments</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Build a labeled calibration set containing supported claims, contradictions, unsupported additions, correct calculations, missing exceptions, and ambiguous cases. Measure detector precision and recall by failure type. A high false-positive rate can overwhelm review queues or cause excessive abstention. A low false-positive detector can still miss many harmful claims. Thresholds should reflect the consequence of each error and the capacity for downstream review.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Consider base rates. If serious unsupported answers occur in one percent of traffic, a detector that flags five percent of otherwise good answers may generate far more false alarms than true ones. Operators need to know the likely composition of the queue, not only an impressive sensitivity figure. I would estimate expected review volume at production QPS and verify that the escalation path can actually process it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Model judges can prioritize review, identify unsupported claims, or suggest passage labels, but their outputs should not silently become ground truth. A judge can prefer fluent prose, miss domain exceptions, or share the generator&#x27;s wrong assumption. Sample both flagged and unflagged cases for human review. Monitor agreement by language and task type, and keep the judge configuration separate from the model being improved.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Mine useful hard negatives</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A hard negative is a passage that looks plausible for the query but does not satisfy the required relevance condition. Examples include the wrong product edition, an obsolete policy, a similar identifier, or a rule missing the decisive exception. These cases teach more useful distinctions than random unrelated text. Verify that a proposed negative is not actually another valid answer or one necessary part of a multi-passage evidence set.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Avoid mining only from the incumbent&#x27;s top results forever. That can leave unobserved failure modes outside the training distribution. Mix reviewed production examples with representative domain data and targeted difficult cases. Preserve positive-source diversity so the model does not learn one document style as a shortcut for relevance. Track data provenance and prevent the same source revision from leaking across development and final assessment through near-duplicate questions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Improve representations only when that is the bottleneck</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider representation improvements after diagnosing the gap</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If relevant passages consistently rank poorly under exact dense search, a better embedding model or domain adaptation may help. Training data should include hard negatives: passages that share terminology but differ in product, date, entity, or condition. Random unrelated negatives can make training look successful without teaching the distinctions that cause production failures. Keep training and evaluation queries separated to avoid inflated results.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Changing embeddings requires a compatible reindexing and migration plan. Compare query and document representations under the new model, retune thresholds, and inspect long-tail regressions. A larger model is not automatically better for every domain. Metadata extraction, chunk prefixes, or exact identifier handling may solve a specific failure more economically. Choose the intervention that addresses the observed ranking error rather than defaulting to the most expensive representation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Sometimes a metadata rule, parser correction, or source update fixes more problems than fine-tuning. If a model retrieves the right passage but the prompt builder truncates it, embedding training targets the wrong layer. Maintain a failure taxonomy and quantify the contribution of each category. Prioritize fixes by affected tasks, severity, expected improvement, and engineering cost rather than by which technique is most fashionable.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Turn repeated gaps into content work</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cluster unanswered or poorly answered questions and compare them with the authorized corpus. If no suitable evidence exists, create a reviewed content-gap record with representative questions, missing facts, source owners, and expected audience. A retriever cannot recover a policy that was never written or published. New content should pass the same provenance, approval, permission, and freshness controls as existing sources.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Do not infer that every unanswered topic deserves a new document. Some requests are outside product scope, sensitive, ambiguous, or better served by a live tool. Routing improvements can address these cases. The backlog should distinguish knowledge gaps from capability gaps and unsupported tasks. That prevents a documentation team from receiving an undifferentiated pile of queries that no static article could answer correctly.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect the loop from manipulation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Production feedback can be noisy or adversarial. A user may repeatedly mark a source as irrelevant to suppress it, or coordinated activity can distort popularity signals. Apply rate limits, provenance, reviewer checks, and anomaly monitoring to feedback-derived changes. Do not let one user&#x27;s rating directly alter shared authoritative ranking or publication state without an evaluated policy. Tenant boundaries must also hold in training-data access.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Privacy rules continue to apply after feedback is collected. Evaluation and training copies may need redaction, retention limits, source deletion dependencies, and restricted access. A support conversation&#x27;s availability for resolving one issue does not automatically authorize indefinite model training. Keep the intended use of each data asset explicit and provide a way to remove or invalidate records when their underlying source becomes ineligible.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate changes with controlled comparisons</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make changes through controlled comparisons</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run ablations for candidate count, ANN effort, metadata filters, lexical retrieval, reranking, deduplication, and context budget. Hold other variables fixed where practical, then evaluate important interactions. Plot quality against latency and cost rather than optimizing a single metric. Review individual recovered and lost cases. A small average gain can conceal an unacceptable regression on a critical policy category.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that recall is protected early and precision is improved later, while evidence coverage is preserved throughout. Growth creates more opportunities for approximation misses, confusing near-neighbors, duplicates, and source-management problems, but the correct fix depends on measured failure. I would trace the answer from original source to indexed chunk, candidate pool, final context, and generated claim. That diagnosis supports a targeted redesign instead of blindly increasing k, adding GPUs, or replacing the model.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Freeze the candidate model or ranking configuration before final assessment. Report per-slice gains and losses, uncertainty, and severe failures, not only the best run. A model can improve feedback-heavy popular queries while harming quiet long-tail users. Keep a representative baseline sample of production behavior so the feedback stream does not become the entire definition of quality. Silent users still matter.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Close deployment and monitoring loops</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Roll out through shadow comparison and a bounded canary where appropriate. Monitor task success, evidence sufficiency, latency, cost, and the distribution of retrieved sources. Revert if mandatory guardrails fail. An offline score gain does not establish better user outcomes, especially when users adapt their questions to the changed system. Preserve enough version information to distinguish model effects from simultaneous content or product changes.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Close the loop without contaminating the benchmark</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a production failure is confirmed, capture the minimum authorized reproduction: question, source revisions, expected behavior, relevant evidence, and stage-level traces. Classify its cause and add a regression case. Keep a record of the fix and verify that the new case detects the original failure. Do not copy private customer material into unrestricted test repositories. Use controlled fixtures or approved protected evaluation storage.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Refresh datasets as the product and corpus change, while preserving historical suites that protect important behavior. Monitor whether the judge itself drifts after a model update. My interview conclusion would be that evaluation is an ongoing engineering loop connecting evidence retrieval, answer behavior, user outcomes, and operations. The system earns confidence through versioned, representative, consequence-aware checks before release and continuous verification after deployment, rather than through one impressive benchmark score.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Track whether the loop delivers value</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Measure how many reviewed failures become actionable cases, how long fixes take, what proportion require content versus model changes, and whether the same failure categories recur. A growing label warehouse is not itself an improvement. The loop should reduce a defined error or increase successful completion at acceptable cost. Also track reviewer disagreement and data freshness so apparent progress is not driven by changing labels.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that feedback improves retrieval when it is attributable, calibrated, and connected to the right intervention. I would collect useful signals, review their meaning, account for exposure bias, mine verified examples, and maintain both regression and held-out assessment. Deployment remains gated by measured quality and security. The system learns from users through a controlled engineering process rather than automatically reinforcing whatever its previous ranking happened to show them.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/1608.04468\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Unbiased Learning-to-Rank with Biased Feedback</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"When should you abandon RAG in favour of long-context, CAG, or fine-tuning?","g":["rag system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would replace or supplement RAG when another mechanism meets the task&#x27;s evidence, freshness, authorization, latency, and cost requirements more simply. Long-context processing can be effective for bounded document sets. Cached context can amortize repeated prompt processing for stable inputs. Fine-tuning can improve behavior or domain performance when suitable training data exists. These are complementary tools, and none automatically inherits the properties of the others. The right decision follows measured task outcomes and lifecycle constraints rather than an architectural preference.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Clarify which RAG problem needs solving</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">RAG retrieves external evidence at request time. Its failure surfaces include parsing, chunking, indexing, query interpretation, candidate retrieval, context selection, and generation. If the entire relevant source set is already known and small enough, retrieval may add unnecessary complexity. If the problem is output style or repeated task behavior, retrieval may be an indirect solution. First identify the requirement and bottleneck rather than replacing the whole architecture because one component performs poorly.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Also distinguish selecting sources from reading them. A user who explicitly uploads one contract has already scoped the source, while an enterprise assistant answering across millions of changing documents needs a discovery mechanism. Long-context processing can still involve retrieval to choose which documents enter the window. The alternatives are often combined in practice, so &quot;abandon RAG&quot; may really mean remove one unnecessary retrieval stage for a particular request class.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Measure usable context and task scope</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start by measuring the task and the document</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The relevant length is token count under the selected model&#x27;s tokenizer, not page count alone. A four-hundred-page filing may contain dense tables, repeated headers, appendices, or scanned images. OCR can expand or corrupt the text. The available context budget also includes instructions, conversation history, source labels, tool outputs, and reserved answer tokens. I would calculate the usable input budget before deciding whether the original document truly fits.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Question scope matters more than document length. &quot;What notice period applies to termination for convenience?&quot; may be answered from a small contract section and its definitions. &quot;Summarize the major risks across all business segments&quot; requires coverage across many sections. &quot;List every subsidiary&quot; is an exhaustive extraction task, where missing one row matters. These tasks should not all be routed through the same top-five semantic retrieval path.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep retrieval for local evidence when it helps</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use retrieval with expansion for localized questions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a clause lookup, retrieve small answer-bearing passages, rerank them against the actual question, and expand to the minimum surrounding section needed for interpretation. The expansion might add the heading, a preceding definition, a following exception, or a table legend. This gives the generator focused evidence without processing the whole document. It is often economical because most document tokens never enter the online prompt.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Expansion should be constrained. Pulling every parent to the document root defeats the token-saving purpose and introduces distractions. Track which required conditions remain unresolved and retrieve those specifically. If the clause references a definition elsewhere, follow that reference or issue a targeted second search. Reauthorize all expanded material when permissions vary by section.
