A child chunk&#x27;s visibility does not automatically authorize the entire parent document.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use direct long context for appropriate bounded tasks</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If all relevant authorized text fits with room for instructions and output, direct processing can remove candidate-retrieval misses. It may be attractive for a single-document review or a small case file requiring relationships across sections. It still requires correct parsing, source labeling, answer verification, and permission enforcement. A larger context window is a capacity feature, not a guarantee that every important detail will influence the answer.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Long context does not inherently remove document-level access control or precise citations. The application can select only authorized documents and preserve span identifiers in the prompt. The challenge is implementing those controls and evaluating citation support, not an impossibility of the architecture. Likewise, evidence-position effects observed in research should motivate testing the actual deployed model, not a claim that every future model fails identically in the middle.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Consider long-context models as another tool</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If the full document fits with sufficient answer budget, direct long-context processing can avoid some intermediate summarization loss. It may be attractive for one-off analysis or documents whose cross-references are difficult to partition. However, reading more tokens increases cost and prefill work, and performance can vary with evidence position, distracting content, and task complexity. A large advertised context limit is not a guarantee of reliable exhaustive analysis.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would compare direct long context with retrieval and hierarchical approaches on the same tasks. Include localized questions, global summaries, exact extraction, and cross-section reasoning. Check whether the model cites the right spans and preserves exceptions. Prefix caching can reduce repeated processing of a stable document where supported, but it does not remove the need to verify answer quality or to enforce current authorization for the cached content.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use hierarchical processing when full coverage exceeds a window</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use hierarchical summarization for broad synthesis</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Hierarchical summarization processes manageable document units, creates intermediate representations, and combines them in later stages. A map stage summarizes sections or extracts facts. A reduce stage synthesizes those results into a broader account. Multiple reduction levels may be necessary. The useful design principle is controlled coverage under a bounded context, rather than the assumption that repeatedly shortening text preserves every important fact.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The map output should follow the task. For a risk summary, it might preserve risk category, affected business unit, severity indicators, supporting quotations, and source spans. For a contract review, it might preserve obligations, parties, deadlines, exceptions, and cross-references. Task-aware structured notes are often easier to verify than generic prose summaries. The reduce stage then has explicit evidence fields rather than a collection of fluent but potentially incomplete paragraphs.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define what cached context actually reuses</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cache-augmented generation is used for designs that preload or reuse context-derived model state for a bounded corpus. Exact implementations vary. Distinguish caching the prompt&#x27;s key-value state, caching a response, and caching retrieved passages. Reusing prompt computation can reduce repeated prefill work; it does not automatically generate new answers for free or make stale source text current. Verify the backend&#x27;s compatibility, retention, and invalidation rules.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A stable product manual used by many authorized users can be a good candidate. A rapidly changing account dataset with highly individualized permissions may be harder to cache safely. But permission variation does not make caching universally impossible: the application can use permission-scoped prefixes or validate access before reuse. That may reduce economic benefit or complicate storage. The decision is whether the safe implementation remains worthwhile under the actual reuse pattern.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare precomputation and online work</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare on-demand and precomputed work</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">On-demand hierarchical summarization pays processing cost when a user asks a broad question. Precomputed summaries pay upfront and can amortize across repeated queries. The economic comparison depends on corpus size, update rate, query volume, and the share of queries requiring global synthesis. A rarely queried document that changes daily may not justify an elaborate precomputed hierarchy. A stable manual queried thousands of times may benefit from reusable summaries.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would estimate the break-even using measured build and maintenance costs versus per-query savings at comparable quality. Include storage, invalidation, permission complexity, and operator time. Precomputation can improve online latency but create freshness risk. On-demand processing can use the latest source but take longer. Neither is inherently superior; the product&#x27;s freshness and interaction requirements determine which cost is more acceptable.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate cache economics with a simple scenario</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose every request uses a forty-thousand-token manual and produces a short answer. Without reusable state, repeated prefill can be a substantial part of cost and time. If the backend can reuse a compatible authorized prefix, measure actual cache eligibility and billed or provisioned savings. Include cache storage, eviction, reload after deployment, and cold-start capacity. A small warm benchmark can overstate the value for bursty traffic or many distinct manuals.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Now suppose the manual changes every hour and each user sees different sections. Frequent invalidation and fragmented prefixes may reduce reuse. Retrieval could send a few current relevant sections more economically. Neither scenario establishes a universal winner. Compare cost per correct task using the expected update and access distribution, including the worst important cold-cache period. Stable context and repeated use create an opportunity, not a guarantee.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use fine-tuning for a defined learnable behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Fine-tuning can improve structured extraction, task style, domain terminology, instruction following, or specialized reasoning patterns when training and evaluation support the claim. It can also distill a larger system&#x27;s behavior into a smaller model. Define the expected behavior and available verification before training. A rigid format requirement may also be better served partly by constrained generation and semantic validation rather than training alone.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Using weights as a substitute for a changing factual database has drawbacks. Updates require additional training or another knowledge path, provenance is harder to expose, and deletion or permission-specific access is difficult to enforce through shared parameters. This does not mean fine-tuning can never learn useful facts; it means memorized factual behavior is usually a weak replacement for current, auditable, resource-scoped evidence when those properties matter.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Govern training reuse separately from retrieval</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Govern evaluation and training reuse</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Production failures are valuable evaluation examples, but copying them into a golden dataset can extend retention and access beyond the original purpose. I would classify evaluation records, preserve source dependencies, redact unnecessary identifiers, and set deletion behavior. Synthetic examples can reduce exposure, but they should not be mistaken for a complete substitute for representative authorized evaluation. The review process needs both usefulness and a clear data-handling contract.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Using RAG content to fine-tune a model is a separate decision from retrieving it at inference time. Fine-tuning can make removal and provenance more difficult because information may become encoded in parameters. A document&#x27;s presence in a search index should not automatically authorize its use in training. I would keep retrieval data, evaluation data, and training datasets distinguishable, with separate approved purposes and lineage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a hybrid when requirements differ by request</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A mature assistant can use a fine-tuned small generator, a cached stable reference prefix, retrieval for changing policies, and a live API for current account state. A router chooses the required mechanisms based on the task. Keep each component&#x27;s evidence and authorization contract explicit. More components are justified only when they improve quality or economics enough to offset complexity and failure exposure.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Route according to the acceptance criterion</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a local factual lookup, start with retrieval plus bounded parent and reference expansion. For a broad summary of one document, use a coverage-aware hierarchical plan or a validated long-context path. For repeated mixed local and thematic queries over stable material, evaluate a precomputed hierarchy such as RAPTOR. For exhaustive extraction, enumerate sections or rows and reconcile outputs against a manifest rather than trusting ordinary top-k retrieval.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The router should detect uncertainty about task scope. &quot;Review this agreement&quot; is underspecified compared with &quot;extract every renewal deadline.&quot; A clarification may improve the result substantially, but the system can still inspect document structure and estimate the workload first. If the product chooses a default review scope, state that scope. Silent routing to a narrow retrieval path can produce a polished answer that appears comprehensive without actually being so.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare alternatives under equal requirements</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Hold source snapshots, authorization rules, output expectations, and evaluation cases constant where possible. Compare local lookups, global synthesis, exhaustive extraction, no-answer behavior, and changing-source cases. Include citation support and source applicability. If one option receives the entire document and another receives a very small retrieval budget, report that resource difference. Equal model size is not necessarily equal cost, and equal token count is not necessarily equal capability.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test source updates and revocations after warm-up. A cached-context system can look excellent until its invalidation path is exercised. A fine-tuned model can answer common examples well but fail a newly published policy. A RAG pipeline can be current yet omit a decisive passage. The strongest comparison includes these lifecycle behaviors rather than only static accuracy on familiar documents.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Plan transitions and rollback</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Switching mechanisms changes cache keys, provenance, evaluation, and operational assumptions. Introduce a new path behind a versioned serving configuration and preserve an evaluated fallback. If moving from retrieval to cached context for a source class, verify that citations and deletion remain correct. If adding fine-tuning, retain the external evidence path for facts that the model should not invent or memorize as authority.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that RAG remains valuable for large, changing, permission-scoped evidence, while long context and cached context can simplify bounded repeated tasks. Fine-tuning addresses learnable behavior and can complement any of those paths. I would choose the least complex mechanism that demonstrably meets the requirement and use a portfolio only when distinct request classes need it. The decision rests on complete task and lifecycle measurements, not a slogan that one generation technique has replaced all the others.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.15605\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Cache-Augmented Generation for bounded knowledge tasks</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.03172\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Lost in the Middle: use of long contexts</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"What SLOs would you set for a RAG service, and how do you budget latency across the pipeline?","g":["rag observability"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would set RAG service objectives from the user journey and task consequence, then budget the critical path using measured latency distributions. Availability and responsiveness matter, but a fast unsupported answer is not a successful service event. I would define separate objectives for request success, first useful output, completion, evidence freshness, authorization behavior, and sampled answer quality. Numerical targets should be negotiated and validated for the workload, not presented as universal requirements for every RAG product.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Establish the service contract</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start with the service contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Assume an enterprise knowledge assistant with ten million documents, eight searchable chunks per document on average, a mixture of exact identifier lookups and natural language questions, and a peak arrival rate of one thousand requests per second. I would ask whether that rate is sustained or bursty, how many tenants share it, and whether a small number of tenants dominate. I would also ask whether answers require citations, whether documents change hourly, and what happens when sufficient evidence is unavailable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">As an illustrative target, suppose the service should begin streaming within two seconds at the ninety-fifth percentile, deliver a typical answer within ten seconds, and make ordinary source updates searchable within five minutes. These are proposed requirements, not capabilities implied by the architecture. Authorization revocations may require a much shorter bound. I would establish separate correctness targets for answerable questions, appropriate abstention, and complete citation support rather than accepting a fast service that confidently returns incorrect answers.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the task contract before choosing metrics</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An internal policy assistant should answer current policy questions with applicable citations and abstain when evidence is missing. A research assistant may need to compare conflicting sources. A support assistant may be judged partly by successful issue resolution. These products require different rubrics. I would define correctness, completeness, evidence support, acceptable uncertainty, and prohibited disclosures in terms of the actual task rather than treating a generic RAG score as sufficient.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separate answerability from answer quality. A question may be unanswerable because the authorized corpus lacks the information, because it is ambiguous, or because the source itself is incomplete. A correct system response may ask a clarification or state the limitation. If the evaluation assumes every question requires a substantive answer, it rewards fabrication. The dataset should explicitly label the expected behavior for missing evidence, conflicting evidence, and incomplete user constraints.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Distinguish SLIs, SLOs, and contractual commitments</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A service-level indicator is a measured property, such as the fraction of eligible requests that produce a valid response within a deadline. A service-level objective is its target over a defined window. A contractual commitment may carry additional consequences and exclusions. Keep these concepts clear so an internal aspiration does not accidentally become an unsupported customer promise. Define the population, measurement point, and good-event rule for every objective.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For streaming, decide what first token means. A loading phrase emitted immediately can make a token metric look excellent while the user still waits for useful content. Measure first meaningful answer content or another justified product event, and keep raw model first-token timing for diagnosis. User cancellation and client-network failures also need explicit treatment so eligibility rules do not conceal ordinary service problems.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Propose an illustrative initial objective set</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an interactive enterprise assistant, an initial proposal might be 99.9 percent eligible-request availability over thirty days, ninety-five percent of qualifying requests beginning useful output within two seconds, and ninety-five percent completing within eight seconds for a defined answer-length class. These are hypothetical planning targets. Long document analysis, exhaustive extraction, and batch workloads need separate classes rather than unrealistic shared deadlines.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Freshness might require ordinary approved source updates to become searchable within five minutes, with a stricter defined bound for revocations. Quality could use sampled rubric scores for correctness, evidence support, citation coverage, and appropriate abstention. An authorization leak is not something to trade against a percentage of excellent answers. Treat critical security invariants separately from average quality targets and define an immediate incident response.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define availability as acceptable behavior</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An HTTP 200 is insufficient if the answer body is empty, malformed, unauthorized, or generated from an unacceptable partial corpus. Specify which degraded modes count as successful for each request class. A source list may be acceptable when generation fails in an exploratory workflow, but not equivalent to a completed structured extraction. The objective should reflect what the user can accomplish.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Keep policy refusals, unsupported tasks, and appropriate no-answer responses distinguishable from infrastructure failures. Otherwise the team may avoid honest uncertainty to improve success metrics. Track both attempted tasks and successful valid outcomes. Exclusions should be narrow and reviewable. Removing every difficult or slow query from the denominator creates an attractive metric that does not describe the actual service experience.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Budget the first-output critical path</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose a two-second target allocates fifty milliseconds to admission and authorization, fifty to query embedding, two hundred to parallel retrieval and merge, one hundred fifty to reranking, and fifty to context assembly. That leaves 1,500 milliseconds for model admission, prefill, first output, and transport under the simplified budget. The numbers are proposed allocations to test, not measured guarantees. Include networking and queueing in the actual trace.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Dense and lexical branches running in parallel contribute roughly the slower required branch plus merge overhead, while dependent stages add sequentially. Optional rewriting may run speculatively, but a rewrite that changes retrieval requires additional work. Pin the request to a compatible configuration. A budget spreadsheet should represent dependencies accurately; summing every concurrent stage or pretending every stage overlaps can both produce misleading conclusions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Do not add percentiles as if they were constants</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The sum of each stage&#x27;s p95 is not generally the end-to-end p95, because latency distributions and correlations matter. A shared overload can make several stages slow together. Use end-to-end measurements and traces under realistic concurrency, source sizes, and cache conditions. Stage budgets are useful engineering constraints, but the final objective must be tested as a user-visible distribution.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Likewise, averages hide queueing and skew. A large tenant, a restrictive permission filter, or a very long prompt can dominate the tail. Report relevant slices and workload classes. A short-prompt benchmark with warm caches does not establish the latency of long-context policy synthesis. Preserve input and output lengths with performance measurements so changes in request mix can be distinguished from serving regressions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Budget completion separately from first output</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">After first output, completion depends on generated token count, token rate, pauses for tools or verification, and transport behavior. If a response needs four hundred tokens, a first-token target alone says little about when the task finishes. Bound expected output by task and evaluate whether truncation damages completeness. A faster first token followed by a long stall can be worse than a slightly later steady response.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Decide whether detection happens before or after release</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Asynchronous monitoring is economical for measuring trends and discovering regressions, but it cannot prevent an already displayed error. A real-time gate can block or revise unsupported content before disclosure, at the cost of latency and additional failure dependencies. High-consequence answers may justify that trade-off. Lower-risk general assistance may use cheap real-time checks plus sampled post-response review. The product should state which guarantee it offers.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Streaming complicates intervention. Once a false claim is shown, later verification cannot fully retract its effect. Options include verifying the planned evidence first, buffering a sentence or answer segment, or using a final validation stage before release. None eliminates all errors. I would evaluate user experience and safety together rather than treating immediate token streaming as an unconditional requirement for every workload.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Connect capacity to queueing</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">At arrival rate Q and average active duration W, steady-state concurrency is approximately Q times W under suitable stable conditions. One thousand requests per second occupying generation for six seconds implies roughly six thousand concurrent active requests. That calculation informs memory, quotas, and admission control. It is not enough to benchmark a single request and assume linear scaling to the target load.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Provision headroom for bursts and failures. High utilization can create disproportionate queueing delay even before a service reports errors. Use bounded queues and per-tenant concurrency controls so overload fails predictably or defers permitted work. Autoscaling takes time and may not create provider quota. Monitor queue wait separately from execution time to identify whether more capacity, smaller work units, or better admission policy addresses the bottleneck.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define quality measurement with uncertainty</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Sampled correctness or faithfulness is an estimate, not a real-time count of every successful answer. Specify the rubric, sampling design, reviewer or judge, sample size, and reporting interval. Keep an unbiased baseline sample alongside targeted review, and report important slices. A target such as ninety percent supported claims is meaningless without defining claims, support, and the consequences of the remaining errors.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Citation validity needs more than existing identifiers. Measure whether citations support the associated claims and whether important claims are covered. Also monitor current-source applicability and abstention. A system can satisfy a source-support metric while faithfully repeating an obsolete policy. Quality objectives should reflect the task contract and complement deterministic security and schema checks rather than replace them.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calculate and use the error budget</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For a 99.9 percent request-success objective, the allowed bad-event fraction is 0.1 percent. If there are ten million eligible requests in the window, that corresponds to ten thousand bad requests. A time-based objective over thirty days yields 43.2 minutes of allowed unavailability, but request-based and time-based budgets are not interchangeable when traffic varies. State which one the service uses.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Burn rate compares the observed bad-event fraction with the allowed fraction. A two percent failure rate against a 0.1 percent allowance is a twentyfold burn rate. Use short and longer windows to distinguish a severe active incident from noise or a slow persistent problem. Exact paging policies should fit the service and response time. The objective is detecting material budget consumption early enough to act.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Turn budget policy into actual decisions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Agree in advance what happens when reliability deteriorates: pause risky rollouts, prioritize an ingestion fix, reduce optional work, or restrict an expensive request class temporarily. Avoid a ceremonial rule that all feature work stops under every metric movement. The policy should reflect severity, ongoing risk, and the evidence that a proposed change affects reliability. Security fixes may remain urgent even during a general release pause.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Degraded modes need quality evaluation and explicit telemetry. Skipping reranking might preserve an availability objective while consuming a quality budget. A partial-shard search might be acceptable for exploration but not for exhaustive tasks. Keep those distinctions visible rather than counting every fallback as ordinary success. Reliability management is a coordinated product and engineering policy, not a collection of independent green dashboards.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Relate the budget to a concrete product decision</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose evidence retrieval meets its target, but the first-output tail is dominated by long prompts waiting at the generator. Adding vector replicas will not address that bottleneck. The team can reduce redundant context, improve admission control, obtain approved inference capacity, or route suitable tasks to an evaluated alternative model. Each option should be tested against quality and cost. The latency budget is useful because it identifies which intervention can plausibly improve the user&#x27;s experience.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Conversely, if narrowly authorized searches dominate the tail, reducing answer length will not fix first-output delay. Investigate filter selectivity, query plans, candidate exploration, and permission-service latency. The same overall objective can therefore drive very different engineering work depending on measured stage behavior. Keep the end-to-end target stable while using traces to locate the cause. This avoids optimizing a cheap, fast component merely because it is easier to change.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify objectives before committing externally</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Load-test representative query classes with realistic document sizes, filters, concurrency, cache misses, and dependency limits. Include a node loss or provider slowdown to assess failure headroom. Measure source-update and revocation propagation end to end. Validate that the monitoring implementation matches the written SLI definition. A metric that starts after queueing or ends before meaningful output can understate the user&#x27;s wait.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Revisit objectives as product use changes, but do not redefine them opportunistically to hide a regression. Version the measurement contract and explain changes in workload classes. A new long-document feature may need its own asynchronous completion target instead of weakening the interactive objective for everyone. The numbers should remain useful for deciding capacity and reliability work.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would lead with user-visible successful behavior, then define measurable indicators, realistic targets, and a critical-path budget. I would separate first useful output from completion, operational availability from sampled answer quality, and mandatory security from average service performance. Stage-level allocations guide architecture, while end-to-end tail measurements determine whether the objective is met.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The senior point is that SLOs should drive choices: candidate budgets, prompt size, batching waits, routing, admission control, fallback eligibility, and release policy. I would make those trade-offs explicit and validate them under representative load and failure. A service objective is valuable when it helps the team preserve a dependable user experience, rather than when it supplies an impressive latency number unsupported by the actual request distribution.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://sre.google/workbook/alerting-on-slos/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Google SRE Workbook: alerting on SLOs</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you A/B test a retrieval change safely, and why is it harder than a normal product experiment?","g":["rag observability"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would test a retrieval change through offline assessment, bounded shadow comparison, a monitored canary, and an experiment whose unit and metrics match the user journey. The challenge is that retrieval affects evidence, generated answers, and later user behavior. Clicks alone may not capture correctness, and different query slices can move in opposite directions. The objective is to estimate a defined treatment effect while preserving authorization, minimum quality, and a reliable rollback path.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">State the hypothesis and treatment precisely</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For example, the hypothesis might be that a new reranker improves answer-bearing evidence placement for product-policy questions without increasing unsupported answers or latency beyond agreed limits. Define exactly what changes: model, candidate count, fusion weights, chunking, or context selection. If several components change simultaneously, the experiment evaluates the combined configuration; it cannot identify the contribution of each one without additional comparisons.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Specify a primary outcome and guardrails before looking at treatment results. Possible primary outcomes include reviewed correct task completion, successful issue resolution, or a task-specific evidence metric. Define a meaningful improvement, not merely any statistically detectable difference. Latency, cost, source freshness, abstention, citation support, and security remain guardrails. A quality-preserving cost reduction can be worthwhile even if raw accuracy does not increase.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Pass minimum offline requirements first</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build a release gate with meaningful thresholds</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">CI should run a fast representative suite on changes to prompts, retrieval, parsing, chunking, models, and authorization logic. Larger evaluations can run before release or on a scheduled cadence. Gate on predefined quality and operational requirements, including critical slices. For example, a candidate should not improve average correctness while introducing cross-tenant disclosure or a major regression on no-answer behavior. Some failures require absolute rejection rather than a weighted trade-off.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Account for sampling uncertainty. A one-point improvement on a tiny dataset may be noise, while a serious failure hidden in the mean may be material. Paired comparisons on the same questions and source snapshot improve sensitivity. Bootstrap intervals or appropriate statistical tests can quantify uncertainty when their assumptions fit. The release decision should consider effect size, confidence, operational cost, and severity rather than a single green dashboard indicator.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The candidate need not beat the incumbent on every metric. It may trade a small acceptable latency change for a meaningful quality improvement, or maintain quality with lower cost. Write that acceptance rule in advance. Mandatory permission and disclosure checks should pass regardless of the average score. Offline assessment reduces known risk, but it cannot establish how real users will respond to an altered system.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare both retrieval and complete answers</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Compare retrieval under controlled conditions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Run a versioned query set against A and B using the same authorized source snapshot where possible. Measure answer-bearing Recall@k, ranking metrics, multi-passage coverage, no-answer behavior, latency, and memory. Include rare terminology, exact identifiers, multilingual requests, long documents, narrow filters, and near-duplicate revisions. If B improves conceptual questions but regresses on product codes, the aggregate score alone should not decide the migration.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarity thresholds must be recalibrated because score distributions change between representation spaces. A cosine threshold of 0.8 in A has no automatic meaning in B. Even rank-based decisions need evaluation because the candidate distribution can change. Hybrid fusion weights and reranker input distributions may also need adjustment. I would tune on development data and retain a held-out set for the final comparison.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate the complete answer pipeline</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Better retrieval metrics do not guarantee better answers. B might retrieve longer passages that crowd out a crucial exception during prompt assembly. It may favor a different source version or create more duplicate evidence. I would run end-to-end generation with the intended context budget and measure correctness, support for claims, citation accuracy, appropriate uncertainty, and cost. Where generation is stochastic, use repeated or paired evaluations to distinguish variation from a meaningful change.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Human review should focus on disagreements and high-consequence slices, with a separate random sample to avoid inspecting only obvious differences. Keep model-based judges calibrated to human rubrics and avoid assuming that a judge&#x27;s preference proves correctness. Migration acceptance criteria should be written before looking at the final results. Otherwise teams can unconsciously redefine success around whatever metric the new model happens to improve.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use shadow traffic to expose operational differences</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Shadow live traffic without exposing B&#x27;s answers</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">In shadow mode, a bounded sample of authorized live queries goes to B while A&#x27;s result remains user-visible. B must receive a compatible query embedding and the same effective access policy. Record retrieval differences, latency, errors, and source versions. Avoid doubling expensive generation for every request unless the evaluation budget supports it. Shadowing retrieval alone can answer many operational questions at substantially lower cost.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Production traffic reveals distributions missing from curated tests, including bursts, repeated prefixes, extremely selective ACLs, and unusual document types. However, shadowing also consumes capacity and can expose private text to new processing paths. Apply the same approved regional, logging, and retention controls as the live system. Shadow traffic should not silently create an ungoverned copy of sensitive queries or retrieved documents.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use shadow evaluation before exposure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Replay or shadow a bounded sample of production traffic against the candidate while the incumbent remains user-visible. Compare retrieved evidence, output properties, latency, and errors. Preserve the same authorization context and record source revisions so differences are interpretable. Avoid assuming disagreements are automatically candidate failures; the incumbent can be wrong. Human review should adjudicate a useful sample of disagreements and a random sample of agreements.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Shadow traffic finds workload gaps such as unusually long prompts, narrow ACLs, rare document formats, and traffic bursts. It does not measure user reactions to the candidate because users never see it. It can also double processing cost and create additional sensitive traces, so its scope should be deliberate. Shadowing retrieval alone may answer initial questions before running expensive duplicate generation on a smaller subset.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Shadow execution is not literally zero risk. It consumes capacity, can expose data to new processors, and may create additional sensitive logs. It must be authorized and isolated appropriately. It does avoid showing candidate answers to users when implemented correctly. For tools with side effects, shadow only the plan or use a sandbox; duplicating a real action is not an acceptable experiment method.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose the randomization unit carefully</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Request-level assignment can provide many observations but mix treatments within a conversation. User-level assignment preserves a consistent experience but may have carryover from earlier exposure. Tenant-level assignment can reduce organizational contamination while increasing variance because tenants differ. Choose the unit based on the effect being measured and the likely interaction between observations, not simply the easiest implementation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If users ask related follow-ups, their requests are not independent. Analysis should account for clustering at the assigned unit. A change may also alter which questions users ask next, making later query distributions part of the treatment effect. For controlled component comparisons, replay the same queries; for product impact, observe the resulting journey. These experiments answer different questions and should not be interpreted interchangeably.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Control corpus and configuration drift</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Version the source snapshot or record source revisions used in each response. Live ingestion can legitimately change during the test, but both groups should have comparable access to updates. A treatment using fresher data is a combined freshness-and-ranking experiment, not a clean ranking comparison. Record generator, prompt, router, and decoding settings so unrelated changes do not confound the result.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separate cache namespaces or eligibility where needed. A treatment request served from a control-generated answer cache has not actually experienced the intended retrieval change. Conversely, disabling all treatment caching while control remains warm creates a latency and cost imbalance. Define whether the estimand includes steady-state cache behavior or a controlled uncached component path, and implement the measurement accordingly.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use interleaving only where its assumptions fit</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Interleaving can compare rankers by mixing their results into a shared list and attributing suitable user interactions. It can reduce some between-user variation for search interfaces. It is not automatically the best method for generated-answer systems, where passages interact and the user may see only synthesized prose. A mixed context can create an answer that neither ranker alone would produce.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use an established interleaving design when the displayed results and feedback support its attribution assumptions. Preserve source permissions and avoid duplicate presentation artifacts. For final-answer quality, paired human review or a conventional randomized product experiment may be more interpretable. Statistical efficiency is valuable only when the measurement still corresponds to the effect the team intends to estimate.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Plan sample size and stopping rules</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Estimate baseline variability, minimum useful effect, assignment clustering, and available traffic. Low-frequency serious failures may require targeted evaluation in addition to the main experiment. A small average effect may be real but economically unimportant, while a large apparent gain on a tiny sample may be noise. Report confidence intervals and practical consequences rather than a p-value alone.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Avoid repeatedly checking results and stopping at the first favorable moment unless using a valid sequential design. Predefine duration or stopping criteria and account for multiple comparisons when exploring many outcomes. Slice analysis is valuable, but post hoc discovery should be distinguished from preregistered decisions. A promising subgroup can motivate a follow-up experiment rather than become an unsupported universal release claim.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Maintain operational canary controls</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Canary the complete serving configuration</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The switch must select the embedding contract, index, retrieval configuration, thresholds, and compatible cache namespace together. An alias alone may be insufficient because the query embedding must change at the same time. I would use a versioned serving configuration and pin each request to one configuration for its lifetime. That prevents an in-flight request from embedding under A and searching B after a mid-request deployment.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Canary steps might move a small tenant cohort or a small percentage of traffic, then expand after enough representative observations. Fixed percentages such as one, ten, fifty, and one hundred are examples, not required stages. I would watch quality guardrails, authorization failures, latency percentiles, source freshness, and fallback rates. A low-traffic cohort may need more elapsed time to provide useful evidence than a busy percentage-based canary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Analyze heterogeneity and severe regressions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Inspect query type, language, tenant size, permission selectivity, document format, and source freshness. A new model may help conceptual questions while harming exact identifiers. A global average can conceal that trade-off. Predefine critical slices and examine query-level disagreements. Weight the overall result according to the intended population, while reporting important small groups separately rather than letting their failures disappear in the mean.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Security failures and severe factual errors may need absolute stop rules. A gain in citation clicks does not compensate for unauthorized disclosure. Appropriate abstention should be evaluated by question answerability, not simply minimized. A treatment that reduces no-answer responses by fabricating plausible content is a regression. Human or calibrated evidence review helps interpret behavioral metrics that are ambiguous on their own.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Account for adaptation and novelty</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Users learn what a system handles well, so treatment can change the difficulty and composition of later requests. Novelty may temporarily increase engagement without improving usefulness. Consider a sufficient observation period and examine early versus later behavior where relevant. Keep the randomization unit stable and avoid switching users repeatedly in ways that make carryover impossible to interpret.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The experiment&#x27;s operational state also evolves. Cache warm-up, newly indexed sources, and scaling adjustments can affect performance over time. Report ramp periods and exclusions explicitly rather than silently removing inconvenient intervals. If a critical bug is fixed mid-test, decide whether the treatment definition has changed enough to require restarting or analyzing a new phase. A clean causal question is more useful than a large but incomparable dataset.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Roll out based on the full decision rule</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Run production experiments at the right unit</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An A/B test can measure whether a candidate improves actual user outcomes. Choose randomization at the request, user, session, or tenant level according to contamination risks. If users ask related follow-ups, switching models within one conversation can mix conditions. Tenant-level assignment avoids some interference but may require more traffic because tenant behavior varies. Predefine the outcome, minimum effect of interest, duration, and stopping rules.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Business proxies need interpretation. Fewer escalations may reflect better resolution or users giving up. Citation clicks can indicate useful evidence or distrust. A low &quot;I do not know&quot; rate can mean strong coverage or overconfident fabrication. I would combine product outcomes with sampled correctness and safety review. Experiments should not expose users to a candidate that already fails mandatory authorization or security requirements in offline testing.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When evidence is inconclusive, say so. The team can retain the simpler incumbent, gather more data, or deploy only a clearly supported request class if the routing and evaluation justify it. Do not turn a nonsignificant result into proof of equality. If the goal is noninferiority at lower cost, use a suitable margin and design rather than interpret failure to detect a difference as guaranteed unchanged quality.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain why this differs from a simple product experiment</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retrieval changes affect hidden evidence before they affect the visible answer. The generator can mask a retrieval improvement or amplify a small ranking error. Outcomes are often delayed, subjective, and clustered within conversations or organizations. Corpus updates and user adaptation complicate comparisons. These properties do not make rigorous testing impossible, but they require more careful treatment definition and interpretation than a standalone button-click experiment.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be to reduce known risk offline, understand disagreements through shadowing, and estimate user impact with a properly scoped randomized experiment. Keep mandatory guarantees fixed, measure complete task outcomes, and inspect important slices. The release decision should combine quality, uncertainty, cost, latency, and severity, with a tested rollback path. That produces evidence for a reliable deployment rather than a search for whichever metric happens to favor the new retriever.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/1608.04468\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Unbiased Learning-to-Rank with Biased Feedback</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15217\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Ragas: automated evaluation of RAG</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"What are the most common ways production RAG systems fail after launch?","g":["rag observability"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Production RAG often fails through stale knowledge, incomplete evidence, changing traffic, permission mistakes, and uncontrolled operational complexity rather than an obvious model crash. I would group failures by violated contract and instrument each contract directly. A successful request is not necessarily a correct or current answer. The system should make silent drift visible, preserve enough source and configuration lineage to diagnose it, and provide a safe repair or rollback path before users lose confidence.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Silent connector failure leaves a frozen corpus</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A connector token expires, an upstream API changes, or pagination stops advancing. The service keeps returning polished answers from old data because retrieval and generation remain available. Job-success counters may stay green if the connector repeatedly scans an empty or stale response. Measure source revision age, checkpoint progress, and expected change activity, then compare source inventory with published index state.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Repair starts with authoritative reconciliation and credential or parser diagnosis, not increasing model size. During a freshness incident, distinguish current and stale source classes and avoid claiming up-to-date answers where the contract cannot be met. Preserve deletions and revocations while rebuilding. A full replay that restores old removed content can turn a freshness repair into a confidentiality incident.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Corpus growth changes candidate competition</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Do not assume growth automatically causes degradation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Adding irrelevant documents that are far from the query may have little effect. Adding authoritative missing material can improve both recall and precision. Adding many near-duplicates or confusingly similar policies can make ranking harder. Growth can also coincide with changes in language, document quality, permissions, or query mix. I would compare controlled source snapshots so that observed degradation is not attributed to scale when another change caused it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The claim that precision worsens while recall stays constant also needs a metric definition. With a fixed k and an unchanged set of relevant passages, replacing relevant results with irrelevant ones reduces both retrieved-relevant count and recall. Some aggregate metrics can move differently across a changing corpus, but that does not justify a blanket statement. A careful senior answer names the measured quantities and explains the actual competition among candidates.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The response should follow measured failure. More near-duplicate versions can crowd results, while new languages or document types can expose parsing gaps. Keep a fixed regression set alongside a current representative set. Compare exact and approximate retrieval where needed, and inspect whether evidence is lost before or after reranking. Growth is a condition to investigate, not a root cause sufficiently precise to guide a fix.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Parsing and chunking fail on real document diversity</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Diagnose parsing and chunking before tuning ANN</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">First establish that the answer exists in the authorized source and survived extraction. Inspect the actual indexed text, not just the original PDF or webpage. A table may lose its column headings, an exception may be split from the rule, or a scanned page may fail OCR. If no indexed chunk contains interpretable evidence, increasing ANN search effort cannot solve the problem. The representation pipeline must be repaired.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For suspected chunking failure, locate the exact answer span in the source and trace it to chunks. Check whether the heading, subject, number, qualifier, and reference remain together or can be restored through expansion. Compare retrieval with and without parent context on a controlled set. A strong diagnostic is that the correct document is found but its retrieved passages lack the answer relationship. Structure-aware chunking and reference expansion then address an observed failure rather than a guessed one.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Version parsers and inspect failures by format. A release may work on ordinary Markdown and break financial tables or scanned PDFs. Preserve source spans, headings, and parent relationships so diagnosis can compare original and indexed evidence. If an extraction component changes, evaluate representative layouts before broad reprocessing. Re-ingesting millions of documents with a broken parser can propagate the regression faster than ordinary incremental updates.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Duplicates and revisions crowd out useful evidence</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Remove duplicates without discarding important differences</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Exact and near-duplicate documents can occupy many top-k positions, reducing evidence diversity. Canonicalize known copies, group overlapping chunks, and limit redundant context. Preserve source families so repeated copies are not treated as independent corroboration. This is especially useful in large enterprise corpora with copied templates, mirrored documentation, and repeated email attachments. Reducing duplication can improve effective recall within a fixed context budget.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Deduplication needs care with revisions and exceptions. Two policies can be nearly identical while differing in the one sentence that answers the question. A similarity threshold that merges them may erase a meaningful regional or temporal distinction. I would retain version metadata and compare changed spans. Deduplicate presentation or storage where safe, but preserve the ability to retrieve and cite the exact applicable source revision.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Source applicability matters as much as semantic relevance. A policy from last year can look almost identical to today&#x27;s version while differing in the decisive sentence. Track effective dates, publication state, and canonical ownership. The generator should not decide currency from writing style. Serving the wrong revision is a lifecycle failure even when every retrieved passage is internally coherent and faithfully cited.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Query behavior drifts after launch</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Users initially ask the examples shown during onboarding, then discover new use cases. A product launch introduces terminology and questions absent from the corpus. A team starts using the assistant for aggregates that require structured tools. Monitor query categories, unanswered clusters, clarification patterns, and route changes. A launch-day benchmark no longer represents the workload indefinitely.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The fix may be content, routing, or product scope rather than retrieval tuning. A missing support policy needs an approved source. A current account question needs a live API. An ambiguous request may need clarification. Keep a failure taxonomy so every thumbs-down does not become a negative embedding label. Otherwise the system trains on mixed causes and may degrade the component that was already working correctly.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Thresholds and answerability become miscalibrated</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Calibrate thresholds and abstention carefully</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Absolute similarity thresholds often shift in usefulness when the embedding model, corpus, language, or query mix changes. A cosine value is not automatically a probability of relevance. Relative score gaps and rank-based features can be useful, but they are not universally stable either. A narrow gap may reflect many good passages or many equally bad ones. I would calibrate answerability decisions against labeled evidence sufficiency.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A useful abstention policy combines signals such as candidate coverage, reranker scores, source applicability, contradictions, and missing query conditions. Evaluate false abstentions as well as unsupported answers. Returning &quot;I do not know&quot; for every difficult request avoids some fabrication but fails the product. The threshold should reflect task consequence and available follow-up options, including clarification or a targeted authorized retrieval retry.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Monitor false abstentions and unsupported answers together. A stricter threshold can reduce visible hallucinations while making the product unhelpful. A looser threshold can increase apparent coverage by answering from unrelated passages. Calibrate against evidence sufficiency on current slices and preserve the conditions under which a response was judged acceptable. A single cosine cutoff cannot express every task&#x27;s consequence or multi-source requirement.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Deletes and permissions lag behind text updates</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Document deletion, unsharing, and group removal often travel through different source mechanisms than content edits. If those paths are missing, removed sources remain searchable or cached. Treat them as first-class events with measured propagation bounds. Keep tombstones and conditional revision handling so late retries cannot resurrect old state. Reconciliation should verify lifecycle and policy state, not only document existence.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cache keyed by question and tenant can leak within an organization when users have different access. User-specific entries can also become stale after revocation. Validate evidence dependencies or effective authorization before reuse. Parent expansion, summaries, citation previews, and traces need the same boundary. A correct filter on the first vector query does not protect an alternate fallback that fetches the full document without checking permissions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Generation fails despite correct retrieval</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The final prompt may omit the relevant passage, truncate an exception, or mix conflicting revisions. The model may overgeneralize, miscalculate, follow untrusted document instructions, or stop before completing the answer. Inspect the actual assembled context and output settings, not only the earlier candidate list. A correct document identifier in a trace does not prove that the answer-bearing span reached the model intact.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use source-preserving context, deterministic calculation for suitable tasks, claim-support checks, and appropriate abstention. Evaluate output-token limits and stopping behavior for the expected answer format. Higher model capacity can help some failures, but it cannot recover evidence discarded before generation or fix an incorrect authorization decision. Diagnose the stage before selecting the remedy.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prompts, models, and serving configurations drift</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A prompt edit changes source handling without an evaluation run. A provider alias moves to a new model. A tokenizer or embedding configuration becomes incompatible with the index. Keep prompts and contracts versioned, run appropriate regression assessment, and pin supported versions where practical. Version pinning reduces uncontrolled changes but does not eliminate infrastructure or dependency variation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Roll out complete compatible configurations and preserve rollback paths. A query embedding from one representation must not search another model&#x27;s index just because dimensions match. Cache namespaces must reflect relevant changes. Record the actual model and prompt contract used for each response. Without that lineage, the team can spend days trying to reproduce a failure against a configuration that was never the one serving the user.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Cost and latency fail through hidden multipliers</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Longer conversations, broad retrieval, multiple verification calls, retries, and agent loops can multiply token use. A low average cost during a prototype can hide an expensive tail of large documents or repeated escalations. Set per-run work limits, bounded concurrency, cancellation propagation, and cost monitoring by task class. A request count alone does not describe resource demand.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Backfills and migrations can also starve serving if they share quotas or databases. Reserve interactive capacity and use fair durable queues. Retry storms can turn a partial provider issue into a full outage. Measure queue wait, retry amplification, and downstream calls per logical request. A fallback that returns success while doubling compute can exhaust the system quietly unless degraded paths are observable.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Poisoned or compromised content gains influence</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Relevant-looking content can be false or contain instructions aimed at the assistant. Source provenance, publication controls, conflict review, and external action enforcement reduce risk. A highly ranked source is not automatically authoritative, and repeated copies are not independent corroboration. Monitor unusual exposure and changed high-impact sources while keeping detector false positives visible.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prepare containment that reaches derived artifacts</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a source is confirmed malicious, mark the affected revision ineligible immediately and identify all dependent chunks, summaries, caches, and answers. Revoke or disable compromised connector credentials where appropriate. Preserve a restricted incident record before cleanup so the team can reconstruct what happened. The containment path should not require re-embedding the entire corpus before dangerous content stops appearing in retrieval.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Versioned indexes can support rollback, but an alias flip is not automatically safe. The older index may contain stale ACLs, deleted documents, or the same poisoned source from an earlier revision. A rollback must preserve current revocations and deletions. Sometimes a targeted deny-list of affected resource revisions followed by controlled rebuilding is safer than reverting everything. I would test both approaches and define which incident conditions justify each one.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Investigate exposure with evidence lineage</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Request traces should reveal which source revision influenced each answer, subject to privacy and retention constraints. This enables an exposure assessment: which tenants saw the source, which claims were produced, and whether any unauthorized actions were attempted. A source appearing in a candidate list is different from being included in the final prompt or cited in the answer. The lineage should preserve those distinctions to avoid overstating or understating the incident.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">After containment, identify the failed control: unauthorized publication, compromised connector, ranking overexposure, missing conflict handling, or unsafe tool execution. Add the case to regression evaluation and improve the relevant boundary. Simply adding the malicious sentence to a blocklist is fragile because the next attack can express the same false claim differently. The durable fix should address why that source could acquire influence without appropriate review or verification.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluation and monitoring become stale</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Teams can optimize repeatedly against a small golden set until it no longer predicts real behavior. Model judges can change or share the generator&#x27;s mistakes. User feedback can be biased toward top results and the most vocal users. Maintain held-out assessment, current representative samples, and known regression cases with distinct roles. Review severe failures directly instead of trusting one aggregate score.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test the monitoring system with controlled failures. Pause a connector, delay a dependency, revoke access after cache population, and introduce an unsupported synthetic claim. Verify that the expected detector and response activate. If the telemetry cannot distinguish stale source data from generation error, improve the lineage. Making silent failures loud requires evidence that the alerting and diagnosis paths work.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Recover through a defined incident process</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Classify impact, contain the affected source or configuration, preserve protected evidence, and restore an evaluated serving path. Do not blindly roll back an index if it reintroduces stale permissions or deleted documents. Reconcile deferred changes before announcing normal freshness. Add a minimal authorized reproduction to regression assessment and fix the violated boundary rather than only the latest visible symptom.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that production RAG reliability depends on maintaining contracts over time: source completeness, freshness, authorization, evidence sufficiency, compatible configuration, and bounded work. Most failures can remain fluent and apparently successful, so ordinary uptime monitoring is insufficient. I would combine lineage, lifecycle checks, sampled quality, failure injection, and controlled releases so the team can detect drift early and repair the right layer with confidence.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.15217\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Ragas: automated evaluation of RAG</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OWASP: prompt injection risks and mitigation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Design a production multi-agent system for autonomous incident response in an SRE org. Walk through the architecture and the controls.","g":["agent system design"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would design autonomous incident response as a durable, policy-governed workflow that gathers evidence, proposes hypotheses, and executes only explicitly permitted remediation classes. Multiple specialist agents can improve parallel investigation when they have distinct tools and bounded responsibilities. They should not form an unrestricted conversation that collectively acquires broad production credentials. The supervisor coordinates incident state, while trusted services enforce authorization, action preconditions, budgets, and approval. I would begin with proposal-only operation and expand autonomy based on measured performance for specific actions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the incident-response contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Specify supported services, alert types, operating regions, response objectives, and excluded systems. Define what the automation may observe, propose, and change. A memory-pressure alert for a stateless worker has different risks from a database integrity incident. The product should not advertise general autonomous repair if only a few runbook actions are validated. Explicit boundaries make both evaluation and operator expectations more realistic.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Measure outcomes such as time to useful diagnosis, time to safe mitigation, unnecessary changes, repeated remediation, and incident severity after intervention. Root-cause accuracy matters, but mitigation can succeed without a complete root-cause explanation. Conversely, a plausible explanation does not justify a risky action. Keep observation, hypothesis, action, and outcome as separate records so the system does not confuse its own narrative with evidence.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Ingest and correlate alerts before launching agents</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An alert gateway validates events, deduplicates repeated pages, identifies the affected service, and links related signals to an incident record. Enrich with ownership, deployment history, topology, service objectives, and current maintenance state. A deterministic correlation layer can prevent hundreds of alerts from launching hundreds of competing investigations. Rate-limit incident creation and preserve an operator override for incorrect grouping.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Triage can use rules or a classifier to decide whether automation is applicable and what investigation budget to grant. An unsupported alert should reach the normal on-call path rather than enter an unbounded agent loop. Record why a case was accepted or excluded. Classification confidence is not authorization to act, and high severity does not automatically justify broader privileges. The incident policy determines permitted capabilities.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use a supervisor with typed specialist tasks</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The supervisor owns the incident state machine, assigns bounded questions, and combines findings. A metrics specialist examines latency, errors, saturation, and dependency behavior. A logs specialist searches approved log scopes. A deployment specialist inspects recent changes. A runbook specialist retrieves current procedures and relevant past incidents. These are example roles, not a requirement to use four separate models when a simpler tool workflow is sufficient.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Each task has a service scope, time interval, evidence requirements, deadline, and call budget. Specialists return structured observations with source links, timestamps, uncertainty, and unresolved questions. Avoid raw log dumps that consume context and expose secrets. The supervisor should distinguish directly observed facts from inferred causes. A specialist&#x27;s confidence does not make an unsupported assertion authoritative, even if several agents repeat it.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Parallelize independent investigation and control shared load</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Metrics and deployment inspection may run concurrently, while a targeted log query may depend on identifying the affected version. Represent these dependencies explicitly. Limit concurrent queries to production observability systems so the investigation does not worsen an outage. Expensive wide-range searches should require narrower parameters or a separate budget. Cache compatible read-only observations within the incident when their age remains acceptable.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Multiple agents are useful only if their additional findings justify coordination cost and correlated errors. Agents using the same model and evidence are not independent voters. Agreement can reflect one shared mistaken assumption. Compare the multi-agent topology with a simpler baseline during evaluation, including total tool calls, useful evidence found, latency, and operator effort. Architectural complexity should be earned through better incident outcomes.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Build hypotheses with evidence and disconfirming checks</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The supervisor can propose hypotheses such as a recent deployment causing increased errors or a dependency saturation event. Each hypothesis should reference observations and identify a discriminating check. For example, compare affected and unaffected versions, regions, or request classes. A temporal correlation with a deployment is not proof of causation. The workflow should seek evidence that could contradict the leading explanation before choosing a consequential remediation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Represent current system state outside the model&#x27;s prose: affected resources, observed health, prior actions, approvals, and expiry times. Re-read relevant state before mutation because conditions can change during investigation. If an operator already mitigated the issue, the agent must not execute a stale plan. Concurrency control and preconditions are essential when humans and several automation systems can act on the same service.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Classify actions by actual risk</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Read-only queries, bounded reversible changes, and high-consequence operations need different policies. But reversibility is not determined by the tool&#x27;s name alone. Scaling can exhaust quotas or overload a dependency; draining a node can violate availability; rollback can be safe in one application and incompatible with a database migration in another.
