Classify actions using blast radius, statefulness, data impact, preconditions, and tested recovery behavior.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A policy registry can permit a particular action only for defined services and conditions, with rate limits and maximum cumulative change. The executor validates the current resource state and exact arguments. The model does not choose its own privilege tier. Actions outside the approved autonomous envelope produce a concrete proposal for an authorized incident commander. An approval timeout defaults to the policy&#x27;s no-action or escalation behavior, never implied consent.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make human review specific and binding</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle high-consequence actions with concrete review</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When the product requires human approval, the review should show the actual action, destination, resource, and relevant content. Asking a user to approve a vague &quot;continue&quot; operation does not provide informed control. Approval must be bound to the reviewed arguments so the model cannot change the destination or payload afterward. Low-impact, reversible actions may follow a different policy, but that policy should still be established by the application and user intent.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The review interface must also resist injection. A malicious document might insert text that looks like an approval button or falsely claims the user already authorized an action. Render source quotations distinctly from application controls. Do not let arbitrary document markup become trusted interface elements. Security depends on the full workflow, including what the person sees and what the executor actually receives after approval.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The approval record should identify the incident, resource revision, operation, arguments, expected impact, expiry, and authorized reviewer. If those details change materially, obtain a new decision under the policy. Keep approval separate from ordinary chat text so a retrieved runbook or log entry cannot impersonate it. The execution service checks the recorded decision, not a model-generated assertion that someone approved the action.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use short-lived scoped credentials</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Mint or obtain credentials for the incident&#x27;s permitted services and operations, with audience and lifetime restrictions. Keep secrets outside model prompts and inject them at the trusted tool boundary. Separate agent identity from the human or service authority under which it operates. Audit both. A read-only specialist should not inherit the supervisor&#x27;s mutation token simply because they belong to one incident run.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The downstream service or a strongly isolated trusted gateway must enforce resource access independently. Tool schemas constrain arguments, but they do not establish permission. Logs and runbooks are untrusted instruction sources even when operationally useful. If an incident workflow needs external communication, destinations and payload handling require explicit controls so private production data cannot be redirected by retrieved text.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make remediation durable and idempotent</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Persist action intent and an operation identity before execution. Use downstream idempotency or reconciliation to handle uncertain outcomes after timeouts. A workflow restart must not repeat a rollout or scaling change blindly. Record the result and verify system health after a defined observation period. If the outcome is unknown, resolve it from the authoritative service rather than asking the language model to infer whether the call probably worked.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use compare-and-set preconditions or resource versions where supported. An approved change for version A should not apply accidentally after the service moved to version B. Coordinate ownership so two runs do not issue conflicting remediations. Durable execution preserves incident progress through process restarts and long human waits, but it does not by itself make external side effects exactly once or safe.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Bound loops and provide a real kill switch</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Set per-incident limits for elapsed time, model tokens, tool calls, search range, and mutations. Stop or escalate after repeated failed remediation rather than increasing blast radius automatically. The kill switch should revoke or disable execution capabilities and stop new work, not merely ask the supervisor politely to stop. In-flight operations may still complete, so cancellation requires outcome reconciliation and a defined recovery process.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Maintain a cumulative action budget. Several individually small changes can combine into a large effect. For example, repeated scaling steps can exceed an intended resource envelope. The policy layer tracks total change across retries and child runs. Operators should see active investigations, pending approvals, current capabilities, and recent actions in one incident view, with a straightforward way to take over.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect long-lived context and memory</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect summaries, memory, and multi-step workflows</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An injection may persist beyond one request if the assistant stores a contaminated summary in memory. Future turns may then treat it as established context without remembering its untrusted origin. I would preserve provenance and trust labels through summaries and memory records, avoid storing unverified instructions as user preferences, and provide expiration or correction mechanisms. Derived content does not become trustworthy simply because an earlier model generated it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Multi-step agents also need bounded plans and repeated policy checks. A safe first action does not authorize an unsafe fifth action. Tool results should be treated as data unless they come from a defined trusted control channel. If a retrieved page tells the assistant to expand its search to a new service, the application should assess that request against the user&#x27;s task and allowed capabilities. The model&#x27;s plan is not an independent source of permission.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Past incidents can provide useful procedures, but a previous successful fix is not automatically appropriate now. Store confirmed conditions, action preconditions, outcomes, and source links, with review and versioning. Avoid saving speculative hypotheses as established root causes. Memory should support investigation, while current evidence and the policy registry decide what can be executed in this incident.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Walk through a bounded remediation scenario</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose a stateless worker service shows increased queue age after a deployment. The metrics specialist finds rising backlog and stable downstream latency. The deployment specialist identifies a new worker version, while the logs specialist finds repeated parsing errors only on that version. The runbook specialist returns a reviewed procedure with rollback preconditions. The supervisor records a deployment-related hypothesis and the evidence that distinguishes it from simple demand growth. It does not treat the timing alone as proof.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Before proposing rollback, the executor checks whether the release included incompatible state changes, whether another operator already mitigated the incident, and whether the target version is available. If policy requires approval, the incident commander reviews the exact service, version, expected effect, and verification plan. The workflow persists the approved operation identity, executes through the trusted deployment service, and observes error rate and backlog for the defined interval. A timeout triggers status reconciliation rather than a second independent rollback request.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If errors fall but backlog remains high, the system should report partial mitigation and investigate remaining capacity. It should not repeat rollback or declare a confirmed root cause solely because one metric improved. If health worsens, the next action follows the approved recovery policy and cumulative budget. This scenario shows the division of responsibility: agents gather and interpret evidence, durable state tracks progress, and deterministic controls decide whether a concrete production change is permitted and still applicable.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate through replay and controlled exercises</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Start with historical incident replay where tools return recorded or simulated observations and mutations cannot affect production. Include misleading alerts, incomplete evidence, simultaneous incidents, injected instructions, stale runbooks, and an operator changing state mid-run. Measure diagnosis, safe proposal quality, tool efficiency, and prohibited action attempts. A convincing final narrative is insufficient if the workflow took an unsafe path to produce it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Move to shadow investigation and proposal-only assistance for live incidents. Operators review usefulness and identify missing evidence. Promote specific remediation classes only after their preconditions and outcomes are validated. Continue monitoring false actions and near misses, not only successful recoveries. Autonomy should be earned per operation and service context rather than granted globally after a few impressive demonstrations.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would describe a durable incident workflow with bounded parallel specialists, an evidence-focused supervisor, and an independent trusted action tier. The key controls are scoped authority, current-state preconditions, idempotent execution, concrete approval, cumulative budgets, and enforceable stop mechanisms. Observability and incident memory preserve verified evidence without converting model speculation into operational truth.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The objective is safer and faster incident handling, not maximum autonomy. I would begin by improving diagnosis and proposals, then enable narrow actions where measured outcomes justify them. The production architecture must remain reliable when models are wrong, tools time out, evidence is malicious, or humans intervene. That distributed-systems and policy foundation is what makes agent assistance useful in an SRE environment under real pressure.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://docs.temporal.io/workflows\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Temporal: workflows and durable execution</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OWASP: prompt injection risks and mitigation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Why do long-running agents need durable execution, and what does that change about your design?","g":["durable execution"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Long-running agents need durable execution because their work outlives individual processes, requests, and network connections. They can wait for external jobs, human decisions, scheduled conditions, or slow tools while deployments and failures occur underneath them. I would represent the run as a persisted workflow with explicit state transitions, recoverable external operations, and versioned execution logic. Saving conversation messages is useful, but it is not a complete substitute for durable control flow, pending timers, approvals, and side-effect records.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Start with a concrete crash scenario</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An agent prepares a report, submits it to an external system, and waits for approval. The external submission succeeds, but the worker crashes before recording the response. An in-memory loop loses its place. Restarting from the beginning may submit the report twice; restarting after the call may incorrectly assume completion. The system needs a durable record of the intended operation and a way to determine its authoritative outcome.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Now extend the workflow over several hours. A human approves after a deployment, a token expires, or the referenced resource changes. The resumed agent must recover the pending decision, validate current authorization and state, and continue with compatible logic. This is a distributed-systems problem involving uncertain outcomes and evolving state. A larger context window does not solve it, because remembered prose cannot establish whether an external effect committed.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate workflow state from model context</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Persist task identity, current step, source references, tool-operation identities, pending jobs, approvals, budgets, and terminal status in a structured state model. The language model may receive a summary of that state, but the summary is not the authoritative ledger. If a generated summary omits a completed action, the executor should still know it happened. State transitions should be validated by application logic rather than inferred from conversational wording.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Conversation history can be large, sensitive, and nondeterministic. Keep only what is necessary for reasoning and audit under the retention policy. Tool results can be stored as protected references with hashes and revisions. The workflow state should support recovery without replaying every token through the model or asking it to reconstruct control flow from scratch. This also makes operator inspection and targeted repair more practical.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Choose a persistence model deliberately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Some workflow engines reconstruct state by replaying a durable event history; other designs persist explicit checkpoints and pending work. Framework checkpointing may cover graph state but leave external scheduling, approval delivery, or operation reconciliation to the application. Compare the actual guarantees needed rather than assuming every product labeled durable provides identical behavior. Recovery semantics, retention, scaling, and versioning should be part of the selection criteria.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A database-backed state machine can be sufficient for a bounded workflow if the team implements durable dispatch, timers, concurrency control, and recovery carefully. A mature workflow engine can provide useful primitives and operational tooling. The engineering trade-off is not simply library versus database. It is which failure responsibilities are delegated and which remain in the application, especially around effects outside the workflow&#x27;s transaction boundary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Keep nondeterministic work outside replayed control flow</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">In replay-based systems, the workflow&#x27;s decisions must remain compatible with its recorded history. Live network calls, current time reads, random values, and language-model generation are nondeterministic. Execute them through the engine&#x27;s designated activity or side-effect mechanism and record the relevant results. Replaying the workflow should consume the recorded result rather than ask the model to generate a new plan for an already completed decision.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">This does not make the model deterministic. It makes the workflow&#x27;s use of a particular model result durable. New steps can still call the model under a versioned configuration. Preserve enough input and output identity to explain that result without storing unnecessary sensitive text indefinitely. A retry of a failed model call and a deliberate request for a new plan are different logical operations and should be recorded distinctly.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define checkpoint boundaries around meaningful transitions</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Useful boundaries include accepting the task, completing a tool result, choosing a validated plan, requesting approval, receiving a decision, and publishing a final artifact. Persist the state and outgoing work atomically where possible, such as through an outbox. A checkpoint saying &quot;action complete&quot; is incorrect if only the intent was stored. The state schema should distinguish prepared, dispatched, accepted, completed, and unknown outcomes when the tool requires it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Calling a step a transaction boundary does not make an external service part of the same database transaction. If a tool call occurs between local writes, a crash can still leave an uncertain remote effect. Design the recovery path explicitly. Durable execution reduces lost progress; correct external semantics additionally require idempotency, status queries, conditional operations, or human reconciliation depending on the tool.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make activities retryable under their actual semantics</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Read-only activities are often safe to retry, though results can change and queries can still be expensive. Mutating activities need stable logical operation identities and downstream deduplication or a reconciliation protocol. The same action retry must reuse its identity, while a genuinely new action needs a new one. A run-and-step key can work, but continuation or workflow recreation may require a broader stable business-operation identity.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The deduplication record should bind the key to validated arguments and preserve the result long enough for recovery. Reusing a key with different arguments should fail rather than silently execute another operation. If the downstream service does not support idempotency, the workflow cannot promise exactly-once effects merely because its own state is durable. It may need a controlled adapter, an observable resource identity, or a policy that stops on uncertainty.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Persist external jobs and resume by identity</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Long-running tools often return a job identifier rather than a final result. Store that identifier and poll or subscribe according to the service contract. A worker restart should resume observing the existing job, not submit another one automatically. Validate item-level completion for batch jobs and preserve partial successes. A job marked complete can still contain failed records that need separate handling.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Callbacks can be duplicated, delayed, or arrive out of order. Authenticate them, correlate them to the expected operation, and make state transitions conditional on the current revision. An old completion event should not overwrite a later cancellation or a replacement job. Keep event identity and outcome timestamps where useful, while recognizing that source ordering rules are more reliable than unrelated wall-clock comparisons.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make human pauses explicit workflow states</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Approval should be a durable object tied to concrete action arguments, resource state, authorized reviewers, and expiry. The workflow suspends without occupying a worker indefinitely. A decision callback resumes it through an authenticated state transition. Duplicate approvals should not execute the action twice, and denial should have a defined terminal or replanning path. Silence follows the documented timeout policy rather than being interpreted as consent.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">After a long pause, revalidate authorization, resource versions, evidence freshness, and whether the action is still necessary. Approval for an old target state may no longer apply. The model should not be allowed to replace the approved arguments after resumption. This is why approvals belong in structured workflow state rather than a conversation sentence that the agent later paraphrases as permission.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Support durable timers and deadlines</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use persisted timers for retries, delayed checks, and approval expiration instead of blocking a process with an in-memory sleep. Distinguish a step timeout from the total task deadline. A retried step should not reset the entire run&#x27;s allowed duration or cost. Record remaining budgets so a restart cannot accidentally grant unlimited additional work. Long-lived workflows need explicit expiry and cleanup policies.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Timers and callbacks can race. A decision arriving at the deadline should follow a defined ordering or conditional transition, not create two paths. The same applies to cancellation and completion. A durable state machine should allow one valid transition from the expected version. Operators need visibility into stuck states and time spent waiting so durable work does not become forgotten work that survives forever without progress.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Treat cancellation as a protocol</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Stopping the workflow prevents new steps, but an already dispatched remote operation may continue. Propagate cancellation where supported, then reconcile its result. Marking a run cancelled does not undo a payment, deployment, or message that already committed. Record which actions are complete, pending, or uncertain, and provide an appropriate operator path. A kill switch should also restrict execution capabilities when necessary.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Compensation can restore some business state but is not equivalent to erasing history. A reservation may be released; an external message cannot be unsent in a meaningful general sense. Define compensation semantics and failures separately. Do not ask the model to improvise undo operations without policy and current-state checks. Durable cancellation is useful when it produces a trustworthy account of what did and did not happen.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Version code and state for in-flight runs</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A deployment can change workflow logic while existing histories still need compatible execution. Use the engine&#x27;s supported versioning, patching, worker routing, or draining strategy. Version state schemas and provide explicit migration rules where needed. New code should not reinterpret an old approval or tool result under incompatible semantics. Test recovery of representative old runs against the deployment plan before changing production workers.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Model, prompt, tool schema, and policy versions also matter. A run may intentionally keep its initial configuration or adopt a new one at defined boundaries. Record that decision. If a policy becomes stricter, current authorization checks may need to override an old plan. Compatibility is not permission to preserve obsolete privileges; workflow continuity and live security requirements need a deliberate interaction model.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve observability and operational repair</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Expose run status, current state, pending operations, retries, elapsed time, budget consumption, and approval age. Link structured workflow history to protected tool traces and evidence references. An operator should be able to distinguish a healthy long wait from a stuck activity or missing callback. Avoid relying only on application log lines that disappear when a worker is replaced.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Repair tools should perform validated state transitions with audit records, not arbitrary database edits. A manual retry must reuse or replace operation identities according to the intended semantics. Protect access to sensitive workflow payloads and credentials. Durable history can accumulate private data over long periods, so retention and deletion require design. Reliability does not justify keeping every model context permanently.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Walk through recovery after an ambiguous submission</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose a workflow submits a document-processing job to a remote service. It first stores the logical operation identity and validated input manifest. The service accepts the job, but the response connection fails before the worker records the remote job identifier. If the API supports idempotent submission, the retry uses the same operation identity and receives the existing result or job reference. If it supports lookup by a client reference, recovery queries that reference. Either mechanism resolves the uncertainty without creating a new logical job.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If neither mechanism exists, the workflow cannot safely assume the submission failed. It records an unknown outcome and follows a bounded reconciliation or operator path. The appropriate behavior depends on consequence: duplicate inexpensive read-only processing may be tolerable under policy, while duplicate external publication may not be. Durable execution makes this uncertainty visible and persistent; it does not remove the information gap in the remote API. That distinction should appear in the tool contract before the workflow relies on it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">After the job reference is known, a later worker can resume monitoring it. A duplicate completion callback is accepted idempotently, and publication occurs only once under a conditional state transition. If the user cancels during processing, the workflow records cancellation, requests remote cancellation where supported, and checks whether a result still completed. The final status accurately distinguishes cancelled-before-effect, completed-before-cancellation, and unresolved remote outcome. That account is more useful than a single generic &quot;failed&quot; flag because it tells the user and operator what recovery remains possible.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve budgets and policy decisions across restarts</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A restarted workflow must not reset its tool-call allowance, elapsed-time limit, or cumulative mutation budget. Persist consumption and evaluate the remaining allowance before new work. The same applies to an approval decision: its exact arguments and expiry survive, while current authorization is rechecked according to policy. A fresh worker process should not mean a fresh grant of authority. These details distinguish durable execution from merely serializing a chat transcript and starting a new unrestricted loop with that transcript as context.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Test the recovery contract</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Crash a worker before dispatch, after remote success, before local acknowledgment, during approval wait, and during cancellation. Verify that work resumes at the correct logical state and external effects are not blindly duplicated. Test duplicated callbacks, expired credentials, incompatible deployments, and lost job notifications. Recovery tests should inspect authoritative external state, not only whether the workflow eventually reports success.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that durable execution changes the agent from a request-bound loop into a versioned distributed workflow. It preserves progress and pending decisions, but correctness still depends on external-operation semantics, current authorization, and explicit recovery. I would design state, activity boundaries, timers, approvals, and reconciliation together. The result is an agent that can wait, restart, and evolve without losing accountability for what it has already done.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://docs.temporal.io/workflows\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Temporal: workflows and durable execution</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/making-retries-safe-with-idempotent-APIs/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Amazon Builders’ Library: making retries safe with idempotent APIs</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you get correct failure semantics for agent tools that have side effects?","g":["durable execution"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Correct failure semantics for side-effecting agent tools come from the application and downstream systems, not from instructions telling a model to avoid duplicates. I would define each logical operation, persist intent, enforce authorization and argument validation, use idempotency or conditional state changes where supported, and reconcile uncertain outcomes. Multi-step work needs explicit partial-success and compensation rules. The honest guarantee is often effectively once under stated assumptions, not a universal exactly-once promise across arbitrary external services.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Begin with the ambiguous-outcome problem</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An agent calls a tool to create a support case. The server commits the case, but the response is lost. The caller sees a timeout and cannot tell whether the operation happened. Retrying with a fresh request can create another case; refusing to retry can leave the workflow incomplete if the first attempt never committed. A network error describes what the caller observed, not the authoritative state of the remote system.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The same pattern applies to payments, deployments, emails, reservations, and record updates, with very different consequences. Transport delivery guarantees do not automatically define business-effect guarantees. A queue may deliver a message once while the consumer crashes between an external action and its acknowledgment. The design must cover every boundary at which local knowledge can diverge from remote state.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define the logical operation and its identity</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Assign an operation identifier when a validated action is prepared, and persist it before dispatch. It should remain stable across retries of that same intent. A workflow and step identifier can help, but recreated workflows, continued runs, or user retries may need a stable business-operation reference. Do not derive identity only from arguments when two legitimate identical actions must remain distinct.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Bind the identity to the tool version, target resource, principal context, and canonical validated arguments. If a retry presents different arguments under the same key, reject it as a conflict. A new plan that changes the amount or recipient is a new intent and may require fresh authorization or approval. The model should not be allowed to regenerate idempotency keys casually whenever it retries a tool.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Implement downstream idempotency atomically</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A service supporting idempotency should coordinate the operation record and effect so concurrent requests cannot both execute. It can store states such as pending, completed, and failed, together with the result. Repeated requests with the same valid identity return or resume the existing logical operation according to the contract. The record&#x27;s lifetime must cover the expected retry and reconciliation horizon.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A cache in the agent wrapper is insufficient if it can lose its record after the external action commits or if another caller bypasses it. Stronger guarantees require the effect owner or a controlled adapter to make deduplication and mutation atomic within its authority. Even then, downstream actions beyond that service may create another uncertainty boundary. State the scope of the guarantee precisely rather than extending it to the entire workflow by implication.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Persist intent and outcome separately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Before executing, store the validated action, operation identity, authorization decision, relevant resource version, and approval reference if required. After execution, store the authoritative result or remote operation identifier. Distinguish dispatched, accepted, completed, rejected, and unknown. A single boolean &quot;tool called&quot; cannot represent the states needed for safe recovery. The record should support both machines and operators.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An intent record is not proof that an effect occurred, and a missing outcome is not proof that it did not. Recovery examines unresolved intents and queries the downstream system using the stable identity. If the result is found, record it and continue. If the operation is definitively absent and retry is permitted, dispatch safely. If the result remains uncertain, follow an explicit stop, wait, or escalation policy.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Prefer observable declarative operations</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">&quot;Ensure deployment replicas equal six&quot; is often easier to reconcile than &quot;add two replicas,&quot; because the desired state can be read. But declarative syntax is not automatically safe under concurrency. Another actor may intentionally change replicas after the first attempt. Use a resource version, compare-and-set condition, or ownership rule to avoid overwriting newer intent. Read current state before applying a stale plan when the tool contract requires it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Similarly, &quot;set status to shipped&quot; may be inappropriate if the order was cancelled in the meantime. Validate business transitions, not only equality. Idempotency prevents duplicate execution of one operation; optimistic concurrency prevents an operation from applying to an incompatible state. Both may be required. The model&#x27;s explanation that a change is harmless does not replace these preconditions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Classify errors by retry semantics</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separate validation errors, authorization failures, business rejections, transient infrastructure failures, and ambiguous outcomes. Invalid arguments should be corrected under a new validated intent, not retried unchanged indefinitely. Authorization denial should not trigger a broader credential fallback. A conflict can require rereading current state and replanning. Transient failures can use bounded retry only when the operation&#x27;s idempotency contract makes that safe.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Honor downstream retry guidance and use backoff with jitter. Keep one retry policy owner where possible so SDK, tool adapter, workflow, and model do not each multiply attempts independently. Bound total elapsed time and attempts. A readable structured result should tell the planner whether the operation succeeded, definitively failed, or requires reconciliation. Avoid ambiguous prose such as &quot;something may have gone wrong&quot; without a machine-usable status.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Make side-effect authorization independent of the plan</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The trusted executor checks the current user&#x27;s authority, resource scope, exact arguments, and any action-specific policy. An approved tool name does not authorize every target or payload. Credentials should be injected outside the model context. If a human decision is required, bind it to the concrete operation and relevant state, with expiration. The model cannot self-approve by claiming that a document or previous agent granted permission.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Recheck conditions after long waits. A previously authorized user may lose access, or the resource may move to a different state. Idempotency should not become an authorization bypass that lets a caller retrieve an old sensitive result without permission. The service contract must define who may observe operation status and stored responses as well as who may create the effect.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use reservations for suitable cross-system work</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A reserve-then-confirm protocol can make some workflows safer. Reserve inventory or capacity with a bounded lifetime, then confirm only after prerequisites succeed. Expiration releases abandoned reservations. This is an application protocol, not automatically a database two-phase commit across every participant. Each step still needs identities, state checks, and recovery for a timeout after confirmation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Reservations fit resources that can be held and released. They do not generalize cleanly to publishing a message or triggering an irreversible external action. Evaluate the business semantics and failure windows. A reservation that expires while another system still expects confirmation can create inconsistency unless the workflow handles that transition. The protocol should make the permitted outcomes explicit rather than assume the sequence is atomic.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Define sagas and compensation honestly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For multi-step work that cannot be one transaction, record completed operations and define compensating actions where meaningful. A compensation may release a reservation, cancel an unfulfilled booking, or restore a previous configuration. It may not recreate the exact prior world. Fees, notifications, concurrent user changes, and external observations can remain. Describe the business guarantee rather than call every compensation an undo.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Compensation can fail too, so it needs its own idempotency, retries, authorization, and escalation. Reverse order is a common starting strategy but not universally correct when dependencies or business rules require another sequence. Preserve the original failure and the compensation state separately. A workflow should not report clean rollback merely because it attempted compensations without verifying their authoritative outcomes.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Place irreversible actions deliberately</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If an operation has no meaningful compensation, perform prerequisite validation first and minimize the remaining failure surface around it. Some workflows can place publication near the end, while others require explicit human review or a stronger domain-specific protocol. Approval reduces unwanted intent but does not solve duplicate effects after a timeout. It must be combined with operation identity and downstream semantics.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For an outbound message, a provider-supported idempotency or client reference may help. Without such support, a timeout can leave an unavoidable uncertainty about delivery. The safe policy may be reconciliation or a clear operator decision rather than automatic resend. Be candid about that limit. An application cannot guarantee a property that the effect owner provides no way to observe or enforce.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Handle concurrent agents and human intervention</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Several agents may independently propose the same remediation, or an operator may act while a workflow waits. Coordinate using a shared operation registry, resource ownership, leases with fencing, or conditional updates appropriate to the system. A lease alone can expire while an old worker still runs, so the effect service may need a fencing token to reject stale owners. Choose the mechanism based on the actual concurrency threat.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Do not let separate child runs reset cumulative action budgets or create new identities for the same business intent accidentally. The supervisor and executor need a shared authoritative view of prior effects. Human actions should be reflected through current-state checks and incident or task coordination. The objective is compatible intent under concurrency, not simply preventing one process from invoking a function twice.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Design cancellation and operator repair</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Cancellation stops new work, requests remote cancellation when supported, and reconciles any in-flight effects. It does not imply that committed work disappeared. Report partial completion with clear operation statuses and safe next steps. A manual retry should be a validated transition, preserving the same identity when retrying the same intent. Arbitrary edits to workflow state can destroy the evidence needed for future recovery.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Operators need access-controlled tools to inspect unresolved outcomes and record authoritative resolutions. Keep an audit trail of who changed status and why. Do not ask the model to guess a remote state from an incomplete log. When the evidence is insufficient, retain uncertainty explicitly. That is more reliable than marking the workflow failed and silently allowing the user to repeat a potentially completed action.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Walk through a multi-system booking example</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose an agent coordinates a reservation and a notification. It first creates a durable booking intent with a stable business-operation reference, validates the user&#x27;s authority, and reserves the resource through an idempotent API. The reservation returns an identifier and expiry. The workflow stores them before proceeding. If the response is lost, recovery queries or repeats the same reservation operation under the service&#x27;s contract rather than requesting an unrelated second reservation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Next, the workflow confirms the reservation. A timeout after confirmation leaves an uncertain outcome, so it checks the authoritative reservation state. If confirmed, it records completion and prepares one notification operation. If definitely expired, it follows the defined recovery policy, which may require a new quote or user decision. It does not silently create a replacement reservation with different conditions under the original approval. The resource&#x27;s current state and the task&#x27;s authorized parameters govern what can happen next.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The notification has its own identity and outcome. A notification failure does not necessarily justify cancelling a successfully confirmed booking. The business policy might retry notification, expose a pending-notification status, or ask an operator to resolve it. If cancellation is required, it is a new compensating operation with its own fees, permissions, and failure semantics. Reporting the overall workflow simply as failed would hide the fact that the customer already has a valid reservation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">This example shows why each effect needs an explicit contract and why a saga is a business process rather than automatic reverse execution. The desired final state may be a confirmed booking with delayed notification, not restoration of the initial state. The workflow should report that partial success accurately and provide safe recovery steps. Stable identities, authoritative status checks, and conditional transitions make the account trustworthy even when the agent process crashes between any two steps.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Verify the semantics with failure injection</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Test crashes before intent persistence, after intent persistence, during dispatch, after remote commit, and before local result recording. Simulate duplicate requests with the same key, conflicting arguments, concurrent workers, expired deduplication records, and delayed callbacks. Inspect the downstream business state to confirm the claimed guarantee. A unit test that the wrapper called a mock once does not establish production failure semantics.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">My interview conclusion would be that agents should propose and interpret actions while deterministic systems own execution guarantees. Stable operation identities, atomic downstream deduplication, conditional state changes, durable intent, and reconciliation address uncertain outcomes. Reservations and sagas help only where their business semantics fit. I would state the remaining limits plainly and design safe handling for them, rather than promise exactly once because a workflow engine or prompt uses those words.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/making-retries-safe-with-idempotent-APIs/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Amazon Builders’ Library: making retries safe with idempotent APIs</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"Explain the lethal trifecta and how you architect around it.","g":["lethal trifecta","agent security"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The lethal trifecta is a threat-model shorthand for an agent combining access to private data, exposure to untrusted content, and a channel that can send information to an attacker. Simon Willison popularized this framing. The combination creates an opportunity for indirect prompt injection to turn useful access into disclosure. It does not mean every such agent inevitably leaks on every request. I would use the framing to identify and constrain information flows, with enforcement outside the model rather than a promise that prompting makes the risk disappear. <a href=\"https://simonwillison.net/2025/Jun/16/the-lethal-trifecta/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Map the three capabilities concretely</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Private data includes internal documents, account records, credentials, conversation history, and sensitive tool results. Untrusted content includes webpages, emails, tickets, uploaded files, images, and any externally influenced text. External communication includes explicit send tools, arbitrary network requests, browser navigation, and automatically loaded resources. The relevant question is whether sensitive information can reach an attacker-observable destination under the influence of untrusted material.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">All three need to be considered across the entire workflow, not only one named agent. A reader can pass a contaminated summary to a privileged planner, which then sends data through another component. Splitting roles is useful only if the boundaries constrain authority and information flow. A diagram showing several agents can obscure a combined capability path that remains effectively as powerful as one unrestricted context.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Understand the instruction-boundary problem</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the trust-boundary failure</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A RAG prompt typically combines trusted application instructions, the user&#x27;s request, and retrieved text. The application intends the retrieved text to supply facts. An injection attempts to promote part of that text into authority over the assistant&#x27;s behavior. It might claim to be a system update, an evaluation instruction, or a required step for completing the task. The fundamental problem is confusion between content to analyze and instructions to obey.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For example, an employee asks for the current travel reimbursement policy. A retrieved page contains valid-looking policy text plus a statement directing the assistant to disclose unrelated internal information or initiate an external action. The source page does not gain the user&#x27;s authority merely because retrieval ranked it highly. The system must preserve the distinction between answering from a document and executing that document&#x27;s requests.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Model consequences before choosing a boundary</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Model the consequences before choosing controls</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The risk depends strongly on what the assistant can access and do. A read-only public FAQ assistant may produce misleading answers. An enterprise assistant with private documents and outbound tools can expose information or perform unwanted actions. A model connected to workflow tools can create tickets, change records, or send messages if the surrounding system accepts its suggestions without independent authorization. The same injected text can therefore have very different impact across deployments.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would identify sensitive data sources, untrusted input channels, available tools, network destinations, and user-visible rendering surfaces. This is a data-flow exercise rather than a list of suspicious phrases. Retrieval, summarization, conversational memory, and tool responses can each carry attacker-controlled text. A design that protects the main retrieved passage but trusts a generated summary of that passage may simply move the injection to another stage.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Create a capability matrix</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For every execution context, record readable data classes, untrusted inputs, callable tools, permitted destinations, output renderers, and credential scope. Include derived summaries, logs, and memory. A simple matrix can reveal that a supposedly read-only assistant still emits remote image URLs or has a general-purpose fetch tool. The design review should follow data from its source to any attacker-observable sink.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Consider a low-privilege webpage reader, an internal evidence retriever, a reasoning coordinator, and an action executor. Define what each receives and returns. A coordinator that sees private records and free-form webpage summaries still encounters untrusted content, even if the raw page never reaches it. Labeling the summary &quot;sanitized&quot; does not establish that malicious instructions or sensitive leakage cannot survive.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Remove unnecessary private access</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An agent whose task is summarizing public pages may not need enterprise search at all. Give it only the information required for that task and let a trusted broker handle narrowly defined requests for private facts if necessary. Minimize fields and scope. A tool that returns an entire customer record when the task needs only shipping status increases the amount available for accidental or induced disclosure.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Private data minimization also applies to prompts and memory. Do not place long-lived secrets in context to simplify tool authentication. Use a broker that injects credentials at the transport boundary. Historical conversation details should not automatically accompany every external-content task. Reducing available information limits impact even when the model misinterprets a source instruction.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Constrain outbound communication semantically</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Control outbound data paths</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Information can leave through more than an explicit email tool. Model-generated links, image URLs, webhook destinations, analytics parameters, or browser navigation can carry sensitive content. A user interface that automatically loads remote images from an answer may initiate a network request without a separate model tool call. I would constrain remote resource loading, sanitize rendered markup, and avoid automatically following model-generated URLs when sensitive evidence is in context.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Network egress policies should match the application&#x27;s task requirements. If the assistant needs external lookups, route them through approved tools with destination and payload controls. Logging pipelines are also outbound paths: sending full prompts to an unapproved monitoring service can disclose data even when the final answer is harmless. The architecture should make legitimate data movement explicit and auditable rather than relying on the model to recognize every exfiltration attempt.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A host allowlist is helpful but insufficient if an allowed host exposes attacker-controlled paths, public content creation, or arbitrary query parameters. The executor must check the operation, recipient, resource, and payload, not only the destination domain. An approved messaging service can still deliver secrets to an unauthorized recipient. A private data flow should require a task-specific authorization rule and permitted audience.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The same applies to browser and rendering behavior. Source links may be useful for citations, but automatic requests should not include private content in URL parameters or resource names. Render external content through a constrained interface and avoid executing document-provided markup as application controls. Keep data-plane content separate from the trusted UI that records user decisions and action status.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate reading from action with enforced contracts</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate reading from consequential action</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The strongest controls limit what a compromised model response can cause. A model that reads untrusted content should not automatically gain unrestricted ability to send information elsewhere or modify systems. Where action-taking is required, I would use a separate trusted workflow that checks the user&#x27;s actual request, the target resource, the destination, and the proposed arguments before executing anything. The model may propose an action, but proposal is not authorization.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Separating models can help reduce exposure, but two models alone do not create a security boundary. If the reader model passes attacker-authored instructions as a &quot;summary&quot; and the action model trusts them, the attack survives. The boundary must constrain the representation and authority of what crosses it. Typed factual fields, source references, policy checks, and narrowly scoped capabilities are more meaningful than simply giving the second model a different prompt.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A typed summary can reduce attack surface when it restricts what crosses a boundary, but schema validity alone checks shape. A string field can still contain an instruction or encode private data. Validate semantics where possible, preserve provenance, and limit what the receiving component can do with the field. Do not let a free-form &quot;next_action&quot; field become an instruction channel from an untrusted document to a privileged executor.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For high-consequence workflows, use controlled data types and predefined operations rather than arbitrary text commands. Even then, policy checks must verify that a valid operation is authorized for the current user and task. The architecture should remain safe when a model chooses a malicious but syntactically valid field value. This is the difference between a formatting boundary and an execution-authority boundary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use structural prompting as a supporting measure</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve instruction hierarchy structurally</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Retrieved content should be clearly labeled as evidence, separated from application instructions, and associated with immutable source identifiers. The system instruction should state that source text cannot redefine the assistant&#x27;s role, tool permissions, or disclosure policy. Delimiters and structured fields help the model interpret the intended boundary and can reduce accidental confusion. They are useful defenses, but they are not a proof that the model will always respect the boundary.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Avoid assembling prompts by concatenating arbitrary source text into instruction-like templates without escaping or labeling. If a document contains XML-like tags, Markdown headings, or role labels, the application should not let those characters alter the actual message structure. The model may still interpret their meaning, but the application can at least prevent syntactic corruption of its own prompt format. Trusted instructions should be generated from controlled templates rather than from retrieved content.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Apply task-bound authorization at the executor</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The executor should know the user&#x27;s actual requested action, not merely the model&#x27;s latest plan. If the user asked for a summary, retrieved text cannot authorize sending the summary elsewhere. If the user asked to send a report, the executor still validates the recipient, included data, and resource permissions. Tool access is an available capability; it is not blanket permission to use it for any purpose.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Approvals, when required by policy, should show the exact destination and payload or a meaningful reviewable representation. Bind the decision to those arguments and an expiry. A broad &quot;continue&quot; click does not establish consent to an unseen disclosure. Approval also does not make the model resistant to injection; it creates an additional decision point whose quality depends on what the person can inspect and what the executor enforces afterward.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Protect derived memory and intermediary results</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Untrusted instructions can persist in summaries, cached answers, and procedural memory after the original source is removed. Preserve trust and source labels through transformations. Do not store a document&#x27;s requests as if they were user preferences or administrator policy. A future context that reads the stored summary needs to know its origin and limited authority. Derived content does not become trusted because an earlier model wrote it.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Logs and monitoring are potential disclosure channels too. A model can produce sensitive text that never reaches the user but is copied into an unapproved trace system. The capability review should include operational processors and support access. Minimize raw content capture and enforce retention. Protecting the final answer renderer alone leaves other paths through which private data can leave the approved boundary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Evaluate actual consequences, not refusal wording</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Test the actual deployment boundary</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would build an authorized red-team suite covering instruction-like documents, hidden extraction differences, conflicting source authority, multilingual content, malicious citation destinations, and contaminated summaries. Tests should exercise the real parser, retriever, prompt builder, renderer, and tool executor. A model-only benchmark can miss a browser image fetch or a logging leak that occurs after generation. The desired outcome is no unauthorized disclosure or action, not merely a refusal phrase in the answer.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Include benign documents that discuss attacks so the system&#x27;s usefulness is measured alongside resistance. Track attack success by consequence, detector false positives, evidence rejection rate, and task completion under protection. Use synthetic secrets and controlled destinations during tests. Re-run representative cases after changes to models, prompts, connectors, parsers, or tools because any of these can change how untrusted material reaches the action boundary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Work through a bounded assistant design</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Suppose an assistant must compare a public vendor page with an internal requirements document. A low-privilege reader extracts vendor facts with source spans and no internal access. A separate authorized retrieval service obtains only the required internal criteria. The comparison component receives clearly labeled evidence and no arbitrary outbound tools. It can produce a local answer and approved citations, but it cannot send the internal document to a destination suggested by the vendor page.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">If the user later requests an external message, that becomes a separate validated operation with a specified recipient and content policy. The trusted executor checks the recipient and reviews which internal facts may be shared. The public page cannot create that authorization. The design does not assume the comparison model will always ignore injection; it limits what a mistaken response can cause and makes any proposed disclosure pass an independent boundary.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Be precise about residual risk</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Removing one leg narrows this particular exfiltration path, but it does not eliminate all AI risks. A public-only assistant can still give a misleading answer. A private assistant with no external tools can still disclose too much to an unauthorized user if access control is wrong. A constrained output channel can still carry sensitive data if its policy is too broad. Use the trifecta as one useful lens within a fuller threat model.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Detectors and source trust help prioritize risk but do not establish a universal safe-content class. Trusted repositories can be compromised, and legitimate documents can quote malicious instructions. Keep controls around capabilities even when content scanning passes. Measure false positives and normal-task success so protections do not make the assistant unusable while leaving a hidden alternate egress path untouched.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Give the senior interview explanation</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would explain the trifecta as a dangerous information-flow combination, then show a per-context capability matrix and the trusted boundaries that prevent arbitrary disclosure. Minimize private inputs, constrain untrusted content handling, and restrict outbound operations according to user intent and audience. Role separation, typed data, prompting, detection, and approval are useful layers when their actual guarantees are understood.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The production claim should be that the architecture limits defined consequences under a stated threat model and verifies those limits through realistic tests. I would not claim that two models, a summary schema, or an allowlisted domain automatically breaks every attack. The senior contribution is tracing how data and authority move through the whole workflow and making the application enforce the boundary even when the language model is confused.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Further reading</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://simonwillison.net/2025/Jun/16/the-lethal-trifecta/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Simon Willison: the lethal trifecta for AI agents</a></p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\"><a href=\"https://genai.owasp.org/llmrisk/llm01-prompt-injection/\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">OWASP: prompt injection risks and mitigation</a></p>\n</div>"},{"q":"How do you handle authorisation for agents acting on behalf of users, and what is the confused-deputy problem here?","g":["delegated auth"],"a":"<div style=\"font-size:15px; line-height:1.9; max-width:100%; min-width:0; overflow-wrap:anywhere\">\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">I would authorize an agent using the intersection of the requesting user&#x27;s current authority, the agent application&#x27;s permitted capabilities, the task&#x27;s approved scope, and the downstream resource policy. The agent&#x27;s ability to plan does not grant it the broad privileges of a shared service account. A confused deputy arises when a more privileged component is induced to use its authority for a caller or purpose that is not entitled to that action. Trusted enforcement must remain outside the model and close to the resource being protected.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Explain the confused deputy with a concrete example</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">An assistant has a service credential that can read every support ticket. A user should see only tickets for their account, but asks for another customer&#x27;s history. If the assistant queries with its broad credential and relies on a prompt to filter the answer, it becomes a deputy using greater authority than the caller possesses. The same problem can be induced indirectly by instructions inside a retrieved ticket or webpage.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A benign request can also exceed intended automation scope. A user may personally have permission to delete a project, but an assistant may require a concrete review step before doing so automatically. User entitlement, application capability, and task authorization are separate checks. The goal is not always to copy every user privilege into the agent; it is to give the workflow only the authority needed and permitted for this task.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Preserve identity and active organization context</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Separate identity, organization membership, and resource permission</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">A user can belong to multiple organizations. Authentication establishes who the user is; it does not establish which organization&#x27;s data a particular request may search. The request needs an active tenant context that the server validates against the user&#x27;s memberships. Within that tenant, the user may have access to only some projects, folders, or documents. Tenant membership is therefore necessary in many systems but insufficient for document access.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Consider a consultant who belongs to organizations Alpha and Beta. While working in Alpha, the consultant should not receive Beta documents merely because both memberships exist. Even within Alpha, membership in the engineering group may permit technical runbooks but not finance forecasts. I would represent the request as a principal, an active tenant, effective groups or roles, relevant attributes, and an authorization-policy version. These values come from trusted identity and policy services rather than client assertions.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">State the resource-access invariant</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">State the security invariant clearly</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For every resource selected into the evidence set, the server must establish that the caller can read that resource in the active tenant. A useful expression is: the resource tenant matches the validated active tenant, the resource is in an allowed lifecycle state, and the policy decision permits this principal to read it. Additional rules may depend on project membership, region, classification, or source-system permissions.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The invariant applies to text, titles, snippets, counts, suggested queries, and citation metadata, not only the final answer body. A document title can reveal an acquisition or disciplinary investigation. An error that says &quot;you cannot access the confidential merger plan&quot; may disclose its existence. The product should return authorization-safe responses without exposing protected identifiers or names through diagnostic text that reaches the user.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">For state-changing tools, extend the invariant to the operation and arguments. Reading a record does not imply permission to modify it, and access to one project does not imply access to another project with the same name. Resolve identifiers through authorized lookups when necessary. Client-supplied user or tenant identifiers are request data until validated against the authenticated session and current policy.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Use delegated credentials where the platform supports them</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">OAuth token exchange and on-behalf-of patterns can obtain downstream credentials carrying appropriate user and actor context. RFC 8693 defines a token-exchange protocol that can express delegation and impersonation semantics; particular identity platforms implement specific supported flows. Use the intended downstream audience, limited scope, and lifetime. Do not assume that every OAuth integration supports arbitrary exchange or that a scope string fully represents resource-level authorization. <a href=\"https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8693.html\" style=\"text-decoration:underline; text-underline-offset:3px\" rel=\"noopener noreferrer\">Source</a>.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The token broker should authenticate the workload, validate the user context, and apply policy before issuing a credential. Keep secrets outside model context and inject credentials at the tool transport layer. An agent should not choose its own subject identity or request unrestricted tokens because a plan mentions another service. The broker and downstream service enforce what the workflow can actually obtain and do.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Put enforcement at trusted service boundaries</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Enforce tool authorization outside the model</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Every tool invocation should be checked by a trusted executor. The executor verifies that the tool is allowed for the user and task, the resource belongs to the authorized scope, and the arguments satisfy a strict schema and policy. A document cannot authorize sending a message, changing an access policy, or uploading data. Approval for a general assistant session does not imply approval for every action that appears in retrieved text.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Use capability-limited tools rather than broad network or shell access where possible. A tool that searches one approved knowledge source is easier to constrain than a tool that can fetch arbitrary destinations with arbitrary payloads. Destination allowlists, resource-scoped credentials, bounded query parameters, and transaction limits reduce potential impact. These controls must be enforced in code or infrastructure because an injected prompt may explicitly instruct the model to ignore a textual restriction.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">It is too absolute to say authorization must never exist in a tool wrapper. A strongly isolated trusted gateway can be an important enforcement point, especially for legacy services. The problem is relying only on an untrusted or bypassable wrapper while a broad credential remains usable elsewhere. Use defense in depth, narrow credentials, and downstream enforcement where possible. Document the gateway&#x27;s trust boundary and prevent alternate paths around it.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Centralize scoped retrieval and tool access</h3>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Centralize retrieval enforcement</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">All search operations should go through a trusted retrieval service that requires a validated authorization context. The service constructs the tenant and permission constraints itself. Its API should make an unconstrained production query difficult or impossible, for example by requiring a scoped search object rather than accepting an arbitrary filter dictionary from callers. Database-side row or document policies can provide an additional independent layer where supported.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Both lexical and vector branches must use the same effective restrictions. Fusion should combine only eligible candidates. Administrative indexing credentials should not be reused by interactive serving if narrower credentials are available. Feature-specific retrieval paths, such as related-document suggestions, conversational memory search, or a fallback to global search, need the same enforcement. Most authorization incidents arise from an overlooked alternate path rather than from a failure in the primary happy path.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Express policies beyond OAuth scopes</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Many business restrictions involve resource attributes, transaction values, destination audiences, operating windows, or segregation of duties. A token that permits &quot;write orders&quot; may not express a maximum refund amount or a requirement for independent review. A policy service can evaluate principal, agent identity, task, resource, operation, and environment. The executor passes validated structured facts rather than a model-generated claim that policy requirements were satisfied.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">Approval should be bound to exact reviewable actions and expire when relevant state changes. An instruction inside a document cannot substitute for the user&#x27;s request or an authorized reviewer&#x27;s decision. If the model changes a recipient, amount, or target after approval, the executor must reject the mismatch or seek a new decision under policy. Authentication alone does not solve this task-level consent problem.</p>\n<h3 style=\"margin:28px 0 12px; font-size:17px; font-weight:600; line-height:1.5\">Limit authority across delegated agents</h3>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">When a supervisor assigns work to a specialist, issue a bounded capability for the relevant data and tools rather than forwarding all parent credentials. A metrics reader may need one service&#x27;s telemetry for a time range, while an action executor needs a separate short-lived permission for one approved operation. Child agents cannot mint broader authority by nesting another delegation. Track the delegation chain for audit and revocation.</p>\n<p style=\"margin:0 0 16px\">The effective authority should narrow as work is delegated unless a trusted policy decision explicitly grants something additional. A specialist&#x27;s result is evidence, not authorization for the supervisor&#x27;s next action. Typed messages and source labels help preserve that distinction, but the executor still validates the resulting operation.
